regionally interdependent, with development more closely bound with national policies than is true of cities, towns, and metropolitan areas. 

It follows, therefore, that the logical starting point for planning in the United States is with a national plan. Growth and character of development of States and of regions, and, to somewhat less extent, the growth and character of cities and towns, are primarily dependent upon broad environmental conditions, upon land utilization, upon great national projects such as waterway development, upon broad policies as to control and guidance of land utilization, upon differentials in transportation rates, upon location of natural resources, upon national policies as to development or conservation, and upon other equally important factors only nationally determinable. By increased knowledge of and more searching analysis of the highest and various development and potentialities of the several great sections of the country and of their many regions and localities it should be possible to assign to each section and to each region its functional part in the national complex. Concomitant with such defined functional part will be certain development characteristics and limitations. gions will be primarily agricultural, others primarily industrial, still others primarily recreational. Upon these primary functions will depend, in large part, the distribution and density of population, the size, character, and location of towns and cities, the kind and extent of transportation facilities, recreational areas, and the various other public improvements required to serve population and functions so distributed. 

Some sections or re- Primary determinants of regional functions are climate, rainfall, quality and condition of soil, topography, natural resources including both those of raw materials and power sources, natural transportation channels including waterways and those for the several forms of land transportation, accessibility to world markets, and other similar factors principally environmental. These forces have been sufficiently dominant largely to control and direct the development of the United States to date, so far as the character of regions is concerned, without very much conscious planning. The so-called laissez-faire development of this country has not been so unguided as might at first appear. There has been a more powerful guidance than man could devise exerted by physical geography. 

Natural controls, however, become less dependable with the increas ing age and complexity of a nation. By continued occupation of a land with increasing intensity and by both plan and planlessness and by ingenuity, man imposes both improvements and desecrations which materially alter both the specific and relative potentialities of great land areas. Temperature, rainfall, deserts, and mountains are largely proof against his enterprise, but soil fertility is reduced to exhaustion by bad farming and erosion. Timber, coal, oil, minerals, metals, and other such resources are drained from whole regions withdrawing from such regions primary reasons for their intensive habitation. Natural obstacles such as swamps and desert areas are over come by reclamation, thereby rendering these areas inhabitable. Great waterways are built with resulting shift of relations between sources and markets and with significant effect upon the activities and relative prosperity of large sections of the country; as illustrations, the Panama Canal and the proposed 3.

St. Lawrence Waterway. Superimposed upon these elements of change are those of mechanical invention and development including such highly significant things as improvements in the transmission of electric power, the automobile, the radio, airplane, telephone and other improved means of communication. These latter developments and improvements have the dual effect of altering the relative potentialities of regions and of equalizing the potentialities of regions. Superimposed again are such artificial controls as those of freight differentials which may favor one region as opposed to another with resulting more or less artificial stimulation of prosperity in certain sections at the cost of others. Closely related to the exhaustion of soils by bad farming and land erosion is the despoliation of natural location for cities through bad city building. Large portions of the great cities of this country have, by bad planning, neglect, and exploitation, become as relatively submarginal for human use as have the denuded and eroded hillside farms of the South. 

We have, therefore, first of all a national planning problem. 

The nature of this problem will be treated in greater detail in a later chapter. It is for the national plan to predetermine as well as possible the great use areas of the country and to provide primary accessibility to these areas by the several forms of transportation; all this with recognition that major improvements may be employed both to serve population and to guide and in some degree determine the distribution of population, the distribution of occupations, and the functions of regions. 

With the logical functioning of regions and sections so determined and so assured by national plan and improvements, individual cities and metropolitan areas will be in better position to predetermine their growth and development probabilities, and States will be better enabled to serve their intermediate function of filling in the pattern between urban, rural, and national life. 

Even with such a national concept and designation of the real potentialities of sections and regions there will undoubtedly continue to be towns and cities of agricultural areas aspiring to emulate New York and Detroit in bigness and industrial importance. There may continue to be cities self-inflated by the over-exploitation of mineral resources. 

But these cities will pursue their vain and costly ambitions with preknowledge of the facts and not in blindness of probabilities, as is now so generally the case with cities throughout the country. Such national predetermination of the potentialities of regions and cities will assist the Nation and States in intelligent assistance to cities through loans and subsidies for such things as public works and housing. The lack of such predetermination of the probable growth potentialities and requirements of cities and regions has perhaps been one of the greatest elements of uncertainty in deciding the kind and extent of projects to be assisted or encouraged by the Public Works Administration. 

Planning, to be fully effective, should extend through the entire physical structure of the country, following the sequence suggested above from national plan, through State, regional, and county plans, to municipal 4

plans, with each lower jurisdiction extending its plans into greater detail within the framework provided by the jurisdiction next above. To accomplish this effect many arbitrary political lines must either be broken over by close cooperation between States, counties, or municipalities concerned; or, State, county and municipal lines must be redrawn to encompass areas of greater real social and economic unity to the end that, in many instances, existing multitudinous small municipalities and counties will be consolidated to form more efficient and more economical governmental and planning units. GND It is to national as well as local interest that planning and its benefits and controls be extended uniformly throughout the country to include all localities and jurisdictions. Local or regional wastes, exploitation, and substandard living environment fall ultimately as costs upon the nation as a whole. No better illustration is needed than the variation in current relief demands coming most heavily from the more severely exploited areas both urban and rural. 

To the

While treating more specifically of physical plan and physical plans and physical planning in the above paragraphs, it is only upon the premise as stated at the beginning of this chapter, that physical planning is merely the preconception and formulation of those facilities and services essential to attainment of a prescribed manner of living. In other words, physical planning is, or should be pursued with a predetermined social objective and provides a means to the attainment of that objective in so far as it is dependent upon the shaping of physical environment and services. extent that planning is done with such an objective it is likely to be successful and worthwhile. Without social purpose and without clear vision of social involvements planning may be negative, even detrimental. It may justly be said that much planning in the past, perhaps unavoidably, has been done without sufficiently broad or clear objectives. Up to the present time most physical planning in this country has been conducted by individual cities and comparatively small regions, for which the formulation of broad and effective social objectives dependent upon national policy has been locally impossible. 

Improved living environment has been a primary goal of many city and regional plans. Service and convenience to business and industry have likewise been properly emphasized. But size and spread of city have been indeterminate. 

Improvements have been discarded and made without knowledge of, and frequently without consideration of their social and economic implications and involvements. Great metropolitan areas have been planned with a view to continued extreme concentration of population assuming the inevitability of such concentration even in the face of known social and economic undesirability and disadvantages of the population pattern so concentrated. It

Planning is not a panacea. It will not prevent all mistakes. offers no miraculous foresight into requirements and eventualities. It deals with ever-changing social desires and requirements and with sometimes rapidly altering economic possibilities and conditions. It rests upon the rather inconsistent and shifting ground of the relation between the rights and interests of the community and of individual citizens. Full realization of all plans, physical, social, and economic, probably cannot be attained except through community ownership and control of all real wealth including land and 5

other natural resources and except through a high degree of submergence of the individual citizen. Full realization of plans probably isn't worth the cost of this much of sacrifice of freedom, especially so since plans are not infallible. On the other hand, no planning and, for that matter, no satisfactory social existence is possible when all rights are vested in the individual and when there is no centralized or communized direction and control of the acts of the individual. Both conditions would seem to be equally abhorrent and intolerable. Somewhere between, lies the area of practicable application and effectiveness of what we call planning, in the 

United States.

CHAPTER II A PLAN FOR A PHYSICAL NATION PLAN 

National planning is essentially a pioneer venture. There have been forays in the field, narrow wedges of attack along one line or another, but it still remains for this counrty to formulate procedure for, and to advance broadly upon national planning as now conceived. There are no guides of precedent covering in any comprehensive degree the job that needs to be done, unless the sum total of more or less unrelated and limited-objective planning efforts scattered through the Nation's history can be considered such precedent.

It has been pointed out that the framing of the Federal Constitution, the formulation of successive land policies, and the creation of the Federal Reserve System are instances of national planning. There have, from time to time, been formulated more or less extensive plans for national defense, the improvement of waterways, the building of federal-aid roads, and the rendering of other national services, but while these many efforts, significant as they are in themselves, indicate recognition of the necessity and importance of planning, none of them, individually or collectively, represents the kind of broad, correlated approach to national development which now comes to be thought of as national planning. Neither is there available for guidance any directly parallel experience in other countries although many nations are engaged in what is called national planning.

Approach to planning must depend to considerable extent upon preconceived objectives, considerably controlled and in large part directed by form of government, land policies, traditions, living standards, resources, and the physical and economic condition of the nation concerned. Peculiar conditions drawn around these several factors prevail in each of the several countries which have thus far undertaken national planning and to large extent have determined their respective approaches and procedures. In no instance would the same procedure be in any large degree applicable to the United States. These countries too are still pioneering and might be said still to be in the experimental stage of application of their somewhat various planning principles.

In each of these countries there is a more or less dominant objective to color and to narrow the scope of approach. Germany undertakes national planning principally through decentralization of industry and peoples, primarily in attack upon unemployment. Its efforts are expedited through dictatorship. Italy sees in national planning a means to development and reclamation of its agricultural interests and to invigoration of its population. 

Land planning is emphasized. Progress again is expedited through dictatorship. Russia, armed with revolutionized land policies and social status of its people, approaches national planning through the portals of sociology and economics with the primary objective of raising the living and cultural standards of a people who have long suffered substandard opportunities. Progress is facilitated and procedures are determined by the existence of a socialized government free of the pressures of vested rights and privileges. In each instance the specialized conditions surrounding national planning in these ry

countries, governmental, economic, and social, coupled with the dictates of expediency and relative necessity, have directed national planning in more or less narrowed channels and in directions only partially applicable to the United States. In Russia, perhaps naturally and possibly of necessity, primary stress, as suggested above, has been placed upon social and economic planning. There is evidence of little progress to date in conception of the country as a unified physical structure with functional areas predetermined and interrelated upon the basis of physical facts and with coordinated service facilities such as highways, railways, and waterways, designed to integrate the national physical structure. In other words, physical national plan appears to lag and to have been left as a secondary consideration.

Determinants of national planning procedure and, to a lesser extent, of the scope of national planning are subject to change; change in governmental structure, in land policies and property rights, and in social objectives, but in approaching the national planning problem there need be no standing back because of unforeseeable changes of circumstances, however significant these changes may promise or threaten to be in the ways and means of planning accomplishment. Adaptation to such changes is an essential part of the dynamic function of planning itself. Therefore, even in the face of uncertainty as to what the future may hold for this country, it seems nevertheless entirely feasible and worth while to enter into the venture of national planning based upon the assumption of retention of the essential governmental characteristics of the United States with such social and economic adjustments as may be imposed by necessity and by evolving social requirements and objectives.

It has been indicated that national planning for the United States, as here discussed, is a new venture. A number of the several Federal departments have undertaken more or less extensive planning of development within their respective jurisdictions but without correlation and without common and clearly defined national objectives. Planning done by these departments and its relation to comprehensive national development will be discussed in a subsequent chapter. Other countries have undertaken and are in the midst of so-called national planning enterprises, but, in all instances, the plans of these countries appear to lack comprehensiveness in greater or less degree. National planning, therefore, while with fundamental principles est ablished through long and successful application, still represents a sort of jigsaw puzzle which no country has as yet completely assembled or developed to its full range of possibilities and potentialities for intelligent guidance of national development. 

This implied conception of national planning, as an agency full in operation and effectiveness, is perhaps in the nature of a somewhat visionary ideal which can be approached in actual practice only in degree dependent upon peculiar current limitations. It is maintained by some that so complicated a task as national planning cannot be approached simultaneously upon all fronts with equal emphasis upon all problems and that practicable · approach must be taking a hand-hold upon whatever phase or phases of the project may momentarily offer greatest promise of accomplishment. This, a sense, is planning by expediency. Circumstances, largely those growing in

out of inadequate knowledge or general unfamiliarity with the nature and advantages of national planning, may compel such an approach. But whatever may be the necessary path to accomplishment, it is important at the start to formulate, first, a clear objective and then, a full program of activities and procedure for obtaining that objective, taking into consideration all the conditions and all the problems that need to be dealt with. Compromise, compelled by necessity as to which problems shall be dealt with first and which allowed to lag until the way is cleared, should be left until insurmountable obstacles develop rather than anticipated. Consistent with this thought, therefore, it seems proper that this initial attempt to formulate an objective, a definition, and a method for national planning in the United States should represent a full and perhaps rather ideal picture of such endeavor, even at the risk of seeming theoretical and impracticable. easier to evolve an effective working program from a scheme of broad approach to a high goal, even though somewhat visionary, than it is to evolve a broad approach and high goal from a program shot through at the start with expediencies and compromises growing out of anticipated obstacles. 

It is

There will be those who fear in national planning a regimentation of both private and collective activities, and perhaps excessive standardization; both naturally distasteful to a freedom-accustomed and freedom-loving people. Others will doubt the practicability, even the possibility, of longrange planning of the development of so complicated an organism as a nation, with direction and trends so unpredictable. As suggested in the previous chapter, such fears and such doubts grow out of failure to comprehend the essential character of national planning and of a nation plan. It may help to say that national planning is, first and foremost, a method and a procedure and that a national plan is or should be a guide plan indicating direc-· tion rather than unalterable requirements. Within the pattern of a national plan, there may remain all proper freedom of individual initiative and action. Unsocial acts and enterprises whereby an individual or groups of individuals profit at disproportionate cost to other individuals and to the community may prove special retardants to the carrying out of a national plan and may need to be restricted in the interests of public welfare. But these acts and these enterprises remain unsocial and undesirable with or without national planning and should be curbed in any event to prevent social disruption. National planning represents merely comprehensive attack upon all national problems, social, economic, and physical. 

With respect to the doubters of the practicability of long-range planning there is but one answer. The alternative of planning is no planning, and of the results of no planning there is sufficient evidence. A national plan extending into a long future period is unavoidably more or less of a guess, but a guess based upon a wealth of well-considered information as to existing conditions and trends, and a guess representing, presumably, the maximum degree of foresight humanly possible. Development proceeds year by year with or without plan. It is logical to assume that less mistakes will be made and that a reasonable degree of order is more likely to be assured when developments are based upon knowledge and upon forethought than when undertaken as unrelated projects without vision. The safeguard in national plan is that it is elastic and adjustable and not an iron-clad mold unadaptable to changing conditions and changing requirements.

Planning is a concomitant of civilization. In some degree it is a measure of the advance of civilization. More than that, civilization is dependent upon planning for survival. A primitive race living in the midst of natural abundance may, with comparative safety, neglect thought for the future beyond meeting the seasonal requirements of its environment. So long as needs are simple and materials to meet these needs are plenty and continuous, there is neither necessity nor encouragement to plan for the future. 

In a sense the early settlers in this country, surrounded by apparently exhaustless resources, experienced circumstances parallel to the primitive race in a primordial wilderness. Necessity for planning was limited to the provision of shelter, to the storage of food against coming winters, to the clearing of forests for convenience of habitation and for growing of food, and to general defense against unfriendly nature and men. To our early forefathers the resources of this country seemed inexhaustible. Indeed to them many of our now most valued resources were not resources at all, but obstacles to existence and to use of the land. The first job was to destroy the forest in order to grow corn. Later on, when the existence of and uses for other resources were discovered, these resources were and continued to be as wastefully exploited as if they had no end. Great quantities of valuable and useful material have been thrown away or destroyed in order to obtain some immediately needed small portion. Great quantities of coal have been irretrievably wasted in order to get at those veins of highest grades, most easily procurable. Immeasurable quantities of gas have been and continue to be thrown away in over-eager exploitation of oil resources. 

The small and precious nutritive content of millions of acres of soil has been withdrawn without replenishment or has been permitted to dissipate through erosion without thought to prevention. Never have the people of the United 

States quite recovered from their early acquired tradition that they were favored by God with a country of inexhaustible wealth. Plans they have made, but, with few and more recent exceptions, these plans have been for the preservation of the social order, for the protection of the rights of the property and persons of citizens, and for the opening of the great breadth of this country and its resources for intensive human occupation and use. Plans have been those of exploitation rather than those of conservation. Whatever may have been the necessity and desirability of this rapid opening up of the country without regard for waste, there no longer remains an iota of justification for continuation of such profligacy. There are those observers who give the United States perhaps a hundred years of tolerable survival under a continuation of the old methods of exploitation of resources and of planless development. In the life of a nation, so calamitous a prediction implies nothing short of premature national senility. 

The ordering, location, and programming of service improvements including transportation facilities such as highways, waterways, railways, and airways, and the many other physical developments essential to social and economic life and interchange in the United States directly reflect this same conception of a country of exhaustless resources. They are, in fact, merely a physical indication or expression of a national objective centered, consciously or not,. upon exploitation, under the guise of necessity of conquering and making accessible to human use a great primitive area. 

In 1919, with opening to homesteading of the last of the public lands 10

adaptable to intensive use, the earlier motivation of encouraging occupation of the land ceased. It had in effect come. to an end decades earlier. Highways, waterways, then railroads and more highways have been laid down over the breadth of the country in chaotic pattern, without regard for correlation, duplication, or waste, in order that more land areas be made accessible to more people in the shortest possible space of time. Great blocks of national wealth were turned over to public carriers as subsidy for providing this accessibility. Whole thought was to open up the land. It did not very much matter how or at what cost in natural resources. Resources were exhaustless. Like a great tidal wave, this process of opening up and development rolled across the country taking the richest of resources and spreading seeds of waste and destruction; reached finally the barrier of the Pa cific; then turned back to sweep a little cleaner and more thoroughly the areas least developed in the first great westward flow. After repeated and ever weakening pulsations, there comes quiescence and relative calm. The flush of the excitement of primary pioneering, exploration, and exploitation passes and leaves vision clear for stock-taking of what has been done and of what is left, both, in the light of future needs.

This stock-taking is an essential foundation for national planning. 

Consciousness of its need is not new nor has it been entirely neglected. For many years and with repeated emphasis the Federal Government has been doing this work although in more or less sporadic fashion. The objective of first stock-taking was that of discovering the location, extent, and character of national wealth and resources but, again, largely as an aid to development of these resources and without adequate concern for insurance against their dissipation. More recently this effort has become more analytical of rates of exhaustion and of relation of the natural wealth to the long-range welfare of the Nation. It now remains to broaden the scope and to fill in the gaps of this stock-taking and to translate the findings in terms of a program of sane and orderly expansion with due consideration to conservation consistent with serving the essential current requirements and social progress..

Urging of conservation of energy and resources and the present-day struggle for adjustment under an economy of abundance may seen strange bedfellows. But, this so-called economy of abundance is itself largely an outgrowth of the rapid development of and unguided use of the science and machinery of exploitation. There is as yet no abundance of equitable distribution and good living. In these things, the great majority of the people of this and other countries still exist in a condition of dire scarcity. It is still important, and will remain so, that natural resources be used intelligently; that national wealth and energy be made to produce a maximum of public good per unit expended; and that thought be given to leaving a fair portion of national resources and wealth as heritage to coming generations. 

Following appraisal of national wealth and resources, comes scrutiny of existing development machinery for adaptability to advancement upon whatever of coordinated national objective may be adopted. There are, under the 

Federal Government, three general classes of service machinery having concern with national development. These are: (1) Federal construction or service agencies directly engaged in and having full responsibility for prescribed 11

construction and service; (2) Agencies administering Federal aid to States and their political subdivisions, in the rendering of certain services, direction and responsibility for which remain largely with these sub-jurisdictions; and (3) Control agencies or Federal commissions which undertake to regulate services rendered by semi-public or private enterprise. 

Class One includes such agencies as the Army with its responsibility for land defence and for the development and maintenance of rivers and harbors; the Bureau of Reclamation, the Treasury Department, responsible for 

Federal court and post office buildings; the Forest Service and other divisions of the Department of Agriculture; the Division of Parks, Reservations, and Public Buildings, responsible for national parks and monuments; and numerous other similar divisions and bureaus, each responsible for some special phase of national development or public service. Class Two includes the Bureau of Public Roads and, more recently, several emergency relief agencies including the Public Works Administration. Class Three includes such agencies as the Interstate Commerce Commission, the Federal Radio Commission, the Department of Aeronautics, and the many special commissions or agencies that are created from time to time for the control of one or another group of private or semi-public activities. To this latter class might be added the Administration of the National Industrial Recovery Act. 

Significant among the circumstances surrounding the collective and individual administrations of these several agencies are: first, lack of a defined, comprehensive national objective to which each of these several agencies might adhere; second, lack of consistent and effective coordination of the plans and policies of these agencies either between classes or between agencies within a single class; and third, failure of the uncoordinated efforts of these many divisions to produce sufficient facts and fact analysis for either the proper definition of a national objective or for the framing of an intelligent program and plan for its accomplishment. Such correlation as has been accomplished seems largely limited to budgetary, executive, and congressional controls, largely financial and touching the problem near its apex rather than at its roots where most effective work can and must be done. 

The most obvious and, perhaps, the most spectacular example of the lack of coordination between the control agencies of the three different classes is in the field of transportation. The Army builds and improves waterways. The Bureau of Public Roads is indirectly responsible for the national highway system, and the Interstate Commerce Commission exercises control over railroads, their construction, and their rates. Through this triple, uncoordinated control of the development and administration of these three types of transportation facilities, requiring close coordination for full effectiveness, there have resulted: many unnecessary duplications of services; competitive waterways built and maintained at public expense to reduce railroad rates and to improve railroad service, both of which are presumably directly controllable by the Interstate Commerce Commission; and the frequent failure properly to relate water, rail, and highway facilities for convenient, economical interchange of services. The waste, duplication, and inconvenience involved are direct charges upon the public either through the public treasury or private purse. 12

Lack of coordination of agencies within a single class is best evidenced perhaps by the relationship between the several Federal construction and service divisions. The Army obtains an appropriation of twenty-seven millions of dollars to make an exhaustive survey of the navigable streams of the county, while the Department of Geological Survey, entrusted with the interior mapping operations, fails to obtain comparable funds to pursue its own mapping program, which undoubtedly would produce a considerable proportion of the data compiled separately by the Army Engineers. Authorization for the Army survey project extends to consideration of flood control, irrigation, and power resources of navigable streams, and yet there is no evidence that the Army's study of irrigation possibilities has been correlated with the plans of the Bureau of Reclamation. Quite definitely the Army plans do not take into account the problems of soil erosion and forestation, both of recognized significance, but falling under the jurisdiction of the 

Department of Agriculture and the Forest Service. The Forest Service in turn prepares an exhaustive survey of forest resources and an elaborate national plan for American forestry including consideration of national parks and other national recreation areas, but appears not to have cooperated with the National Park Service with respect to its national park recommendations. 

As a secondary, but perhaps an even more important result of this lack of initial cooperation between construction agencies in comprehensive plan making is that of competition for construction funds through Federal appropriation irrespective of the relative merit or relative urgency of projects individually or by class. Some slight approach to balance in expenditures for the different classes of Federal public works is obtained through budget framing and budgetary control, but in the lack of comprehensive and longrange plan, defining both objective and means to objective, the budget framers are in position to pass intelligent judgment neither upon individual projects nor upon gross expenditures, nor are the individual construction agencies in a position to support their claims for precedence against bloc interests promoting other uses of funds. 

These failures in coordination of national development and in concerted advance upon a common national objective become increasingly serious with increasing complexity of national development and service requirements. In the early stages of development the more important and more immediate needs were relatively obvious. It required no comprehensive plan and little of cooperation to determine that a trunk railway was needed to connect the centers of two or more rapidly developing, great sections of the country. Neither were the implications of a trunk highway, connecting two or more sizeable cities, too complicated for off-hand judgment as to merit. But as the primary requirements of such basic services as that of transportation are met, determination upon and choice between further developments become more difficult and complicated and dependent upon less discernible factors. То these complications in choice, growing out of relative plenty, are added necessity for consideration of new and changing conditions and of the limitations imposed by them upon growth and development requirements and probabilities. Such factors include: the social and economic implications of the automobile, the radio, the airplane; improved transmission of electric power; recent knowledge as to the rate of depreciation of natural resources such as 13

soil, timber, oil, and coal; and indication of national stabilization of population at a figure much lower than earlier anticipated. These many significant factors and their interplay carry the necessities of national development far beyond where they can be relegated safely to the inevitable cross hauling of numerous and unrelated policy-making and construction agencies.

The kind of administrative method used to bring about desirable coordi~ nation in objective and procedure is relatively unimportant. Presumably there are a number of equally effective ways in which this could be brought about.

Further, execution of plans is less important than the programming of projects. Greatest criticism of the operation of the several Federal construction and service agencies is not with the execution and administration of plans once determined upon, but with methods of planning and programming and with the character of source information and motivation. It is the sources of plan and program making and the foundations of plan and program for which new provision must be made.

Such mal-direction of Federal development as may have occurred or as may now be in process is not necessarily to be laid at the feet of Federal departments, individually or collectively. It is in large part an unavoidable result of the lack of centralized planning. There is needed, as a permanent fixture within the Federal administration, a fact-correlating, policy making, and coordinating body. For want of a better name, this body might be called the "National Planning Board".

Such a Board might be put effectively into operation with slight disturbance of the functions and duties of other existing departments and agen cies. These departments and agencies would logically continue as sources of needed factual data and other information. They would continue with their administrative functions under legislative and executive authority, as in the past, but, desirably, only within the broad national policy and plan established by the National Planning Board, through and by the authority of the President and the Congress.

Preliminary to delegation of development responsibilities to individual construction or administrative agencies, procedures and projects involved and all their attending factors should go through the following general processes: comprehensive fact-finding and analysis, policy making, and plan making to be conducted by the National Planning Board in some manner agreeable to the President and to Congress and, finally, budgetary determination and programming to be undertaken by the Division of the Budget with the benefit of consultation with the National Planning Board. 

Effective procedure in national planning must vary somewhat with the national objective. The formulation of such an objective should be among the first undertakings of a National Planning Board. For purposes of this dis- 

To provide and to insure cussion the following general objective is assumed: a minimum standard of living upon as high a plane as the wealth and natural resources of this country will permit, and, with this purpose in view, to dis cover the true potentialities of this country and its several parts, and to 14

direct subsequent growth and development in such a manner as to make full Social and economic use of these potentialities with due regard for the conservation and replenishment of all natural resources, and for the temper ment and desires of the American people. 

A plan for the accomplishment of this objective must include education; guidance and regulation of both public and private action, by policy and law; and a scheme for the guidance and control of physical improvements to comprise a so-called physical plan. It is with the third part of a national plan that this study is primarily concerned. 

As stated in the previous chapter, the physical plan, whether it be for a Nation, a State, or a city, is not a thing apart from economic and social plans with their involvements of social regulation, government, land policies, monetary systems, banking, public financing, etc., but rather, represents a translation of requirements, objectives, and of limitations imposed by them, in terms of service facilities. Social and economic plans have small reality except in so far as they are expressed in environment and in things made available for human use, for comfort, for enjoyment, and for furtherance of the functions of daily living. In a sense, physical plans represent just so much social and economic planning predigested for ready human assimilation. 

It becomes evident, therefore, that the scope of national planning cannot be limited to the mere unrelated planning for Federal public works but must be extended to all important phases of the national economy. Plarning for and programming the construction of public works represents but one of the stones in the arch of the national structure and one among the last to be replaced. Passing over, for a moment, the many service and regulatory ramifications of government because their bearing upon physical plan is one rather of degree of effectiveness than of form of plan, it is self-evident that public and private enterprise and service in the United States are so intermeshed that neither can be planned for or developed without close regard for the other. First approach to national planning should be without concern as to primary responsibility or jurisdiction, whether public or private or Federal or State. Responsibility for execution of the several parts of plan may be left to later apportionment, either along present lines of demarkation or with such variation as circumstances may require. So approached, national planning will include the following considerations:

GROUP A, 1 

BASIC AND CONTROLLING FACTORS 

1, Land Use Potentiality, 1 for Regions and for Localities: 

Land use potentiality is perhaps the most important basic consideration in national development. It comprises the primary limitation upon the population, commercial, and industrial pattern. Primary factors in land potentiality are: habitability, reclaimability, and productivity depending, in turn, upon temperature range, rainfall, topography, elevation, soil fertility, mineral wealth, relation to natural traffic channels and barriers, 15

relation to areas of other and similar utility including sources of raw materials and markets. Such factors as climate, topography, and natural resources are comparatively static and unchanging except as affected in minor degree by such inventions as air-cooling refrigeration and the airplane. Other factors are less stable and are subject to such swift and material change as quickly to alter the use and growth potentialities of areas concerned. Fertility of the soil, mineral wealth, and timber resources, upon which whole populations may depend, are subject to withdrawal.

The first step in national planning, therefore, is survey and determination of existing conditions with respect to all these factors, static and dynamic, including appraisal of rate of change, feasible extent of conservation and replenishment of those elements subject to withdrawal, and a study of continuing and future potentialitics. This will involve completion of the topographic map of the United States at appropriate scales; completion of the geological and soils surveys; extension of the soils survey to a study of present fertility of soils, rate of erosion, and range of suitable crops; and an appraisal of the changing and future use and adaptability of soils and areas in view of possible controls and developments already on the horizon. On the basis of these surveys and appraisals, and with consideration of other factors listed below, there should be prepared the first phase of national plan, "The National Land Use Plan." This plan would assign broad areas to intensive occupation for industry, commerce, and residence; for less intensive occupation by the several types of agriculture; for withdrawal from agricultural uses by reason of sub-marginal quality or condition or danger of serious erosion; for conservation of such resources as forest and timber and, possibly, such materials as oil, coal, and certain minerals and metals; for recreation; for sparse habitation because of insurmountable obstacles of climate and topography; and for such other specific uses as may be incidental or supplementary to the above. 2, 

Extension of Consideration of Land Potentialities to More Detailed Consideration of Natural Resources, Their Character, the Rate of 

Their Dissipation, Their Appropriate Use, and Their Replenishment and Conservation:- Natural resources, represent the real wealth of the Nation. 

Upon their extent, quantity, and continued availability depends very largely the standard of living possible for the people of the United States. 

In their profligate use and dissipation lies the greatest potential source of impoverishment. Some of those resources now deemed essential may sometime be thrown into the discard by invention and discovery, but until the essentiality of any given resource is definitely disproved, steps should be taken for preservation of its availability for as long a period as possible. In national planning for the use and conservation of wealth represented by natural resources, it is probable that the rate of consumption should be established at a point calculated to maintain an advancing standard of living, but without the wastes incident to unscientific and careless or exploitative methods of taking and/or of processing these resources due either to ignorance, to the pressure of unwise competition, or to the urge for excessive profit. In other words 16

there is probably little justification or need for withholding or for limiting the needful use of the natural resources of this country from the present generation in the interests of posterity. It should be sufficient to eliminate needless waste and to employ all practicable means for replenishment. Greater care in conservation and replenishment will need to be taken with respect to some resources than to others, It is for national planning to determine the extent of essential resources, their rate of dissipation, and the kind and extent of controls necessary in each instance to reasonable assurance of wisest use of these elements of national wealth. 

3, Social and Economic Trends: 

Development of the natural potentialities of a specific region or locality varies in character and intensity and from time to time in response to changes in or evolution of the social and economic structure of the nation. Stronger than the inherent natural potentialities of a region, at least for a given period, are the forces of human inclination and desire acting within the limitations imposed by the prevailing social and economic order. The movement and progress of regression of these forces, in the aggregate, represent social and economic trends. They determine somewhat the form and size of cities; varying degrees of concentration of population; the presence or absence of slums and blighted districts; the possession or lack of automobiles, radios, and electric refrigerators; the availability or absence of facili ties for education, recreation, sanitation, preservation of health, freedom or restriction of movement for the discharge of duties and pleasures; and the many other things and conditions comprising created environment and the circumstances of life.

These movements or trends are dependent upon education, social evolution, and invention. Progress, normally, is irregular and relatively unpredictable, but there are evident at all times certain marked trends which must be taken into consideration in national planning. Exhaustive surveys of both social trends and economic changes have recently been made. and reported upon by qualified observers. It remains in this field for the 

National Planning Board only to observe the findings of these surveys and to keep them up to date along with such periodic expansion as may seem important. 

4, Population and Industrial Trends: This country has wit nessed a series of population and industrial movements or migrations. 

The first great westward movement of population was simply a progressive Occupation and development of a virgin territory and, while interesting historically, has less significance to planning for the future development of the country than have more recent movements of the last half century which has witnessed strong flow from country to city with increasing concentration of population in the larger cities and metropolitan areas, due in large part to acceleration of industrialization and to higher efficiency in the process of agriculture, and, more recently, an apparent counterflow back to the rural areas. Whether or not this counterflow is explainable in whole or in considerable part by depressed economic conditions or has deeper roots is not yet clearly seen. 17

Closely related to this counterflow into rural areas i's apparent tendency toward the decentralization of industry, evidenced by removal of industrial 'plants from the larger to the smaller cities, by apparent favoring of smaller cities for location of certain new industries, and by the creation of branch factories frequently to be located in the smaller population centers. Further important movement of industry during the past decade or so has been that of extensive migration from one region to another, as illustrated by the wholesale transfer of certain of the cotton textile industries from New England to the South. 

Inasmuch as population is in large measure dependent in location upon means of subsistence, the location, movement, and concentration of people are in large part determined by the location, movement, and condi~ tion of industry. In rare instances industry goes to labor, as in the case of the cotton industries to the South and the silk mills of Patterson, New Jersey, to the mining regions of Pennsylvania. Prediction of population trends, therefore, and of future areas of population concentration is inter woven closely with, if not more or less dependent upon, prediction of future developments and movements in industry.

The location and distribution of industry in turn are dependent upon a complexity of factors in addition to availability of labor and include relation to markets, sources of raw materials, availability of power, transportation facilities and transportation rates, taxation, and the extent and character of legislative regulation. 

Distribution of industry, therefore, is a large determinant of population pattern. As suggested above, there are certain factors more or less controlling to the location of industry, and yet industrial distribution is susceptible of a much greater degree of planning than is fully appreciated. 

In this connection the National Planning Board should serve the dual function of predicting trends and of guiding distribution through planning. Prediction of both population trends and industrial distribution should be greatly aided by the land utilization surveys and studies suggested above under Item One.

The discussion above is concerned with the internal movement and relative concentration of population irrespective of rate of growth or ultimate total population of the country. Until recent years there had been no expectation but that the population of this country would continue to increase at a rapid rate for an indefinite period of years. Students of population growth now claim to have convincing evidence that stabilization of the popula tion of the United States is within sight and that it is likely to be reached at a point considerably under two hundred million. It is possible that little known or understood factors may enter materially to affect these calculations. Whether or not the population of the United States stabilizes at one hundred and seventy-five millions or at two hundred and fifty millions in the year 1960, 1965, or in the year 2500 A.D., however, is not of suffi cient significance to the immediate course of planning for the development 18

of this country materially to alter immediate policy or procedure. Enough land and enough natural resources and no more should be used to meet the current needs of the population. Population should be distributed and land and other natural resources should be utilized to best social and economic advantage as determined by consideration of factors listed under Item One. Service facilities should be supplied in adequate and elastic amount based upon the population pattern, determined largely by Item One. The essential character of this pattern would not be greatly changed by a maximum population of one degree or another. other words, trends and direction of growth and of expansion are much more important than rate of expansion and the ultimate population that must be provided for in this country. In 5, Governmental Trends: Inasmuch as government is merely one expression of the social order, this factor and this discussion might perhaps more properly have come under Item Two above. However, government, in its legislative and jurisdictional phases, is of special significance to planning and to the carrying out of plans. Though the country has operated within the same Constitution throughout its history, there yet appear to the student of government a great many fundamental changes with respect to processes and social liberalization, especially perhaps with respect to the interrelation of States' rights and the functions of the Federal Government and to the social and economic responsibilities of government. Such changes or evolution may not materially affect the form of a proper national plan, but they do have bearing upon the ease with which any national plan may be carried out and will have further effect upon the apportionment of responsibility for planning and plan execution between the Federal Government and its National Planning Board and the several States and their planning administrations. 

Many elements of national plan, both in policy and in physical structure, must depend in large part upon execution by States, counties, and municipalities. Planning and execution of plans by these political subdivisions of the Federal Government will in most instances be severely handicapped by illogical boundary lines and by the excessive number of county, township, and municipal units. The effect of improved transportation and of improved means of communication has been to remove most of the reasons for the existence of the small and sparsely populated township. 

Economy and facility in planning and in administration suggest the extensive consolidation of closely joined more populous municipalities. These same improvements in transportation and communication have rendered the small counties and, in some degree, the smaller States likewise obsolete, so far as extent of area is concerned. Leaving out of consideration tradition and political intrenchments, and from the point of view of governmental expediency, this country might logically be subdivided into ten or a dozen great States with boundaries laid down upon natural social, economic, and physiographic lines, with each State subdivided possibly into a number of large counties built up by extensive consolidation of existing county units. 

This represents an extreme step in reorganization and one which, for reasons of expediency, may not justify early consideration by a National Planning Board. Earlier and more feasible effort would be that of encouragement of the elimination of townships and of the extensive consolidation of 19

municipalities and counties together with definite provision for regional cooperation between States of close common interests. 

6, General Service and Living Standards in the Several Component 

Parts of the Country: Living standards vary for the different sections of the country and within spe cific regions: witness, the differences in living standards of the farmers in farming communities of the short-grass districts of the far Middle West and of the hill sections of the Southeast, and those of the farmers in farming communities of the Middle West. For variation within regions there is no better evidence than the contrast between the smaller communities of metropolitan areas and the slums of the central cities. It is probable that a considerable degree of variation in minimum standards, between regions and within regions, is inevitable. It remains, however, that the wide variation now found in the United States is due in considerable measure to the laissez faire policy of development which has prevailed throughout the history of the country. Occupation and development of land unsuited for agriculture have been permitted and encouraged. 

Once productive farm land has been allowed to disintegrate and to carry. whole populations into equal disintegration. By less simple processes the slums and slum populations have come into being and have been tolerated. 

Beyond the local social and economic disorders caused by lowstandard living conditions, they have national significance in that these conditions and these areas represent disease spots which tend to spread and to drain and to weaken the national social structure, Some of these conditions can be remedied directly through Federal effort. Others probably must be left to local jurisdiction and initiative. But all are of national concern and properly subject for study and analysis by the National Planning Board.

GROUP B 

1 

PRIMARY ELEMENTS OF A NATIONAL PLAN 

Such factors as those included in Group A above largely supply the foundation for those service facilities usually thought of as com prising the physical plan. They are the direction indicators and the character, volume, and intensity gauges. In actuality there is no such sharp-cut line between foundation and structure as might be indicated by this grouping. Land use particularly verges closely upon physical plan and in many aspects becomes a direct and tangible part of the physical plan. 

However, there seems sufficient difference both in precedence and in nature of approach between these elements as grouped to justify this much of separation for purpose of clarification, The primary elements of the mapped physical plan are as follows: 

(1) Transportation: Highway, Rail, Air, and Water 

If land potentialities, as determined by climatic and physiographic characteristics, may be considered as comprising the skeleton framework of the Nation, then railways, waterways, highways, and airways become the arteries carrying the lifeflow of social and commercial inter change. As suggested above, such facilities should be provided and 20

arranged to serve the special requirements and potentialities of the several sections of the country in a manner designed both of facilitate and to guide growth and development in expected and proper channels. 

To serve this arterial function properly the several kinds of transportation agencies must be closely coordinated both in plan and structure and in operation. To expect effective results otherwise would seem little more intelligent than to hope for an equalized and satisfactory flow of blood in the human body from multitudinous unsynchronized hearts pumping through an uncoordinated maze of arteries. 

National planning is not necessarily concerned with the controversial question of public versus private ownership of railroads or of other transportion facilities. It is concerned with coordination between the several kinds of transportation media and with a proper degree of unification within each form of transportation. Divided ownership and operation may make proper coordination difficult but, with centralized control, is theoretically not impossible. Centralized responsibility for planning, however, especially in its primary and more basic phases is essential to continue satisfactory transportation service at reasonable cost. Approach to a national plan, therefore, cannot be limited to consideration of those forms of transportation normally regarded as public works but must extend to consideration of the major development problems of all the forms of transportation listed above. 

Other forms of transportation, including gas and water mains and electric high-tension lines which, while having large national sig nificance, are nevertheless relatively less important and subject to less difficult problems in effective distribution and coordination. Inasmuch, however, as the transportation of gas and electricity have important bearing upon the development of both localities and regions, they do require consideration and are to be discussed under a later heading. .

Among the transportation problems requiring special attention by some central planning agency such as a National Planning Board are: (1) 

Coordination of services and facilities as suggested above; (2) Coordination within individual forms of transportation now operating under divided management and control, including the unification of railways, of airways, of highways, and to a lesser extent of waterways; (3) Elimination of unnec essary duplication of facilities; (4) Further development and expansion of all transportation facilities in accordance with a unified plan and in accordance with the relative utility and cost of the several forms of transportation as measured by circumstances surrounding individual and competitive projects; (5) The formulation of a policy of control and regulation designed to accomplish the objective of unified plan and service. (2) Recreation Facilities Most types of recreation facilities are logically and of necessity supplied by the several grades of political subdivisions rather than by the Federal Government. However, from the point of view of national planning 21

there remain two major considerations: (1) General regard for recreation standards in the various localities and regions of the country from the point of view of indicating substandard conditions and of stimulating their corrections; (2) The provision, protection, and extension of such great natural recreation areas as the national parks, the national forests, and the protection of and maintenance of accessibility to rivers, lakes, and ocean fronts.

Plans for accomplishment of these purposed are closely interwoven with the policies and procedures discussed above with respect to the conservation and development of natural resources, especially those of forests and waters, 

(3) Power and Power Distribution 

The problems of power and its distribution in the form of both gas and electricity are again closely joined with those of the conservation and development of natural resources and, to a lesser extent, with protection and development of waterways, especially in the case of electricity. 

With modern invention and extension of both domestic and industrial use of electricity, the availability of cheap electric power becomes an increasing factor of influence in determining both population and industrial growth and development pattern. Among the primary elements of national plans for European countries, notably Russia, England, and Germany, are plans for national electrification comprised of high production power plants so distributed and so joined as to serve, with more or less unified facility, the areas and regions promising or designated for relatively intersive occupation and development. An approach to such a network of unification and uniformity of service has been attained in this country largely through the voluntary coordination of private production and distribution companies. It remains for national planning to determine the degree to which desirable coordination and service has thus been attained; to appraise the power potentialities of the several sections of the country; to stimulate and to devise methods whereby electric power may be made more extensively available to all areas likely to continue in intensive agricultural or urban use; and, finally, to determine upon those measures essential to extensive and cheap electric service, whether they be federal control over divided private ownership, federal control over privately owned monopoly, Government ownership and management, or some combination of these designed to supply the desirable checks and balances. (4) Conservation The relation of national planning to conservation is discussed in considerable part above. There are, however, certain applications of conservation policies which become more or less direct functions of the physical national plan. These include: (1) The designation of forest areas to be retained within or added to the public domain and serving one or more of the following purposes: timber conservation and replenishment, 22

recreation, preservation of wild life and natural scenery, and flood and erosion protection and control; (2) The protection and use of rivers and their waters for irrigation, power, and navigation; (3) Designation of areas for various kinds and degree of development; and (4) The use, development, and conservation of other resources as discussed above. 

Broadly speaking, Group A factors represent the foundation, and Group B factors represent the structure of national plan. The principles set forth by the foundation factors apply equally to all planning, whether federal, state, county, or municipal, but the national physical plan is limited largely to major conservation, development, and improvement projects. The national plan provides the structural framework for development, within which the more detailed patterns of state, county, and municipal development must be woven. States, counties, and municipalities must in succession take up planning where the respective superior plan leaves off to the ultimate end that the individual citizen and property are supplied with full service requirements. In the interest of protection and accomplishment of national plan projects, and in furtherance of the national objective of a universally improved minimum standard of living, it becomes important and perhaps necessary for the National Planning Board to extend its efforts beyond national plan making to the stimulation of planning and planning procedure by all political subdivisions, especially by States, and further, to encourage and make possible cooperation between States and the coordination of individual state plans. 

A further function of the National Planning Board should be that of programming or advising in the programming of public works. Both for service and for economy, the sequence of improvements from the point of view of time and relative needfulness is almost as important as the determination of character and location of projects. In this connection it should be borne in mind, however, that the true measure of the effectiveness of any plan is rather one of how few things are done contrary to plan than one of how many and how rapidly plan projects are carried out. 

In other words, a national plan no more than a city plan is a scheme for the mere promotion of public works. Its function is not stimulation of construction so much as a guide for the more logical placing and programming of service facilities in normal volume and with normal concept of execution.

That the planning principle has not found more rapid and more general acceptance in public administration is due in large part to lack of general understanding of what planning is about. This confusion as to what planning is and how it operates is discussed in the previous chapter. 

It prevails generally through all strata of society and can be overcome only by extensive and presistent educational effort. Widespread acceptance of the plan idea and general observation of planning principles can be realized only through enlightened public opinion. Toward this end there are two general approaches, adult and child education. Of the two, child education through the public schools and colleges is much the more hopeful. Planning, of the breadth here discussed, is too new and, in a sense, too revolutionary to be accepted by the average and relatively 23

inert adult mind. The most that can be hoped for is a sufficient number of enlightened adults to keep the ball rolling with full acceptance left to future generations. Much of this needed education can be stimulated and in some instances conducted advantageously by the Federal Government and properly becomes an extended function of a National Planning Board. 

THE NATIONAL PLANNING BOARD - A PLAN OF ORGANIZATION AND PROCEDURE 

From the scope of the national planning job, as outlined briefly above, may be drawn suggestions as to the kind of organization required to prepare and administer the national plan. The details of this organization as to personnel and relationship to other departments and divisions of the Federal Government would probably be as various as the number of those students of planning and of government who might be called upon to devise organization plans. It is, therefore, for the purposes of discussion rather than with conviction that this is the only workable scheme, that the following suggestion for organization is offered: 

1.- Creation of a National Planning Board of five members holding no other office in the Federal Government and appointed by the President with approval of Congress. Members of such board to be selected for breadth of view and for knowledge of national conditions and requirements; to serve for terms of six years so arranged that not more than two members shall go out of office in any one year; to devote approximately full time to the work of the board; and to receive reasonable compensation. 

2. The National Planning Board to function as a coordinating and policy-making body with specialized surveys, studies, and plan making conducted by small committees appointed by the National Planning Board, upon each of which committees the National Planning Board is to be represented by one of its members serving as chairman. The personnel of these committees to be made up of especially qualified citizens, representatives of departments of the Federal Government and other federal officials with qualifications such as the function of the committee may demand. Among these committees might be the following: (1) 

A Committee on Social Conditions and Trends (2) A Committee on Industrial Distribution and Trends 

(3) 

A Committee on Land Use 

(4) A Committee on Conservation of Natural Resources (5) A Committee on Transportation Comprised of subcommittees for each of the principal forms of transportation, railway, waterway, highway, and air 

6) A Committee on Recreation 

(7) A Committee on Education (8) A Committee on the Stimulation and Correlation of State and Local Plans 

(9) A Committee on Government and Governmental Consolidation (10) A Committee on Power Regulation and Distribution 

(11) A Committee on Programming of Public Works 

(12) Such other similar committees as the evolving functions of the National Planning Board may require. 24

3. All committees to report to and through the National Planning 

Board and the National Planning Board, in turn, to report to the President and through the President to the Congress. 

4. The National Planning Board to be set up as a new arm or division of the Federal Administration, possibly under an executive Vice President and correlated with all other administrative departments and divisions of the Federal Government but not directly affiliated with any one of them. 

5. Operative relationship between the National Planning Board and the several construction and administrative departments and agencies of the Federal Government to be based upon dual initiation as follows: (a) The National Planning Board to initiate objectives and policy and the more basic plans such as the determination of the relative functions of the major land areas of the country; (b) The several departments each to initiate specific projects falling within their respective jurisdictions; (c) The several departments and agencies to refer these projects to the National Planning Board, this Board in turn, operating through the machinery suggested above, to evaluate, and to correlate individual projects so initiated, in the light of its predetermined policy and basic national plans. 

Such organization for national planning probably represents an advanced stage which can be approached only by successive steps. Procedure at any given point in progress toward development of such organization must, of course, depend upon circumstances. In the earliest stage of the idea, represented perhaps by the next step beyond the present temporary Board, much stress must be placed upon education of administrative and legislative officials toward acceptance of the more adequate approach, and upon such coordination of federal departments as can be brought about by their more or less voluntary cooperation. Further immediate effort should be toward the centralization of all current national planning work in and through the National Planning Board. Much of the progress toward winning recognition of national planning, as an essential and continuous element in federal administration, must be through demonstration of the indispensability of planning procedure to the development and maintenance of national life upon a plane consistent with the natural wealth and inherent potentialities of this country. 

The possible functioning of a National Planning Board as an agency for the formulation of emergency public works programs is omitted in the above suggestion for organization and operation because regarded as a quite secondary consideration. Whatever the degree of failure of public works during the past months to come up to expectations as a factor in employment relief and economic recovery, a large measure of this failure must be ascribed to lack of pre-prepared plans sufficiently well founded and sufficiently coordinated to carry conviction as to either relative or individual merit and justification. In other words, the United 

States was and still is unprepared to launch intelligently upon any extensive public works program, federal or local. The urgent need is for 25

planning and plans based upon the best of knowledge and foresight that is or can be made available. These, a National Planning Board can provide. With this accomplished and with plan in operation, any emer gency program of public works will fall easily and naturally into place. 

It will require little more than a speeding up of processes and of program. 26

CHAPTER III CRITERIA FOR THE SELECTION AND PROGRAMMING OF PUBLIC WORKS A GENERAL DISCUSSION 

A plan, whether for a State, for an apartment house, or for advanced education, is the product of applied criteria. It is the result of a reasoned choice and determination, based upon known factors and established standards. Plan, extended to construction program and to budget, involves further application of an extended body of criteria. Formulation of plan and of program, by applied criteria, may or may not be entirely by conscious processes. Criteria, assimilated and blended by experience, form the basis of judgment. Incorporated in the thought processes, they become a principal ingredient of common-sense. They color and shape point-of-view. 

But, it has been well said that: "A man's judgment is no better than his knowledge."

In the intelligent selection and programming of public works, essential knowledge includes: (1) A comprehensive plan comprised of properly related and properly weighted service facilities, as component parts; (2) Objectives and standards prerequisite to formulation of the comprehensive plan; and (3) The economic and social implications of carrying out the plan, in consideration of timeliness, relative need, and capacity to finance. 

The evidence of plan itself comprises the most important criterion, It is a sort of composite criterion derived from application of the numerous criteria of fact and of judgment included under Item (2) above. Item (3), dealing with programming and budgeting, brings into play a further set of related facts and factors comprising a third body of criteria. These several criteria are capable of more or less clear definition and of conscious application. Need for such definition, in the interests of widerspread use of criteria and better-informed judgment in the selection and programming of public works has been emphasized by the experience of the Federal Public Works Administration in its efforts of the past year to determine the relative merits of projects for which Federal funds have been requested.

It cannot be repeated too often or too strongly urged that the comprehensive plan represents the primary criterion. But, as suggested above, the comprehensive plan is the composite result of application of many separate criteria, each capable of individual analysis and each requiring more or less of formulation and definition. 

The formulation of adequate criteria is a complicated business involving many variables and, frequently, unavoidable resort to more or less empirical derivatives and the use of arbitrary and shifting standards. 

Criteria must be drawn around such general qualities as needfulness, social desirability, economic feasibility, fitness to purpose, and timeliness. 

These qualities respectively are variable in greater or less degree for each of the several classes of public works, for different parts of the country, 27

and according to economic conditions and to evolving or changing public opinion. It is impossible, therefore, to set up hard and fast criteria applicable to all kinds of public works at all times. ness.

Perhaps the most indefinite of criteria are those of needful- In practical application the quality of needfulness is relative. 

Needfulness, in the abstract, may be a measure of the capacity of mankind to assimilate and to consume improved environment and new things. Needfulness, in this sense, partakes something of speculation in Utopia. As a practical criterion needfulness must be determined by demonstrable deficiencies representing the difference between things possessed and things seeming reasonably possible of attainment. 

For practicability the top limit of needfulness is probably to be measured not by inherent capacity to improve living standard, but rather by the willingness of the public to provide collective services by expenditure from the public purse.

But this is an elastic and apparently evolving limitation. Since the inception of this country, and more rapidly during recent years, the breadth and scope of approved public services have been enlarging. The sequential introduction of free public schools and libraries, public parks, toll-less highways, and almost countless other community things and services are illustrations of the upward trend. 

Improvement of living standards through expansion of public responsibilities and services, however, has not been gained without struggle against reactionary public opinion. There are many who regard with fear and misgiving the rapid increase of the proportionate numbers of public employees and who visualize as an insupportable burden the carrying of an increasing proportion of public employees "upon the backs of other citizens". This is the product of shallow economic thinking. 

At the present stage of development in this country no man is sufficient unto himself. He lives by exchange of services. Usually he consumes only an infinitesimal part of the thing he produces by his own hands. 

The average result of one man's labor is the support of one man, usually ninety-nine hundredths other than himself, plus or minus a variable balance, which spells progress or regression. In last analysis, what matters it to economic stability whether a greater or lesser proportion of services are provided collectively or by private exchange.
