From accepted point of view there are many things that can be supplied and many services which can be rendered much more effectively and more economically under public or collective management than by private enterprise. Schools, highways, fire protection, and garbage collection are obvious instances. Growing recognition of the desirability of expanding the field of public service constantly lifts the range of possibility and with it there are lifted the limits of expectancy, by which needfulness, in considerable degree, is measured. This is true of both quantity and extent of individual services and of the range of services regarded as public responsibility. 

Public opinion, therefore, is an effective regulator of the public purse, determining within rather narrow bounds what proportion of the national income shall be spent in any given year or during a given period of years for public improvements and services. There is available 28

at all times, in effect, a definite and limited amount of money for apportionment among all public enterprises, which brings us to a second criterion, that of balance. 

There are certain basic necessities in community living, as there are for individual existence. As food, shelter, and clothing are to the individual, so are water supply, disposal of waste, passable streets, and fire protection to the community. But beyond such basic requirements are the many other perhaps less essential but, no less fundamental services which should be supplied, both quantitatively and qualitatively, in proportion to community need. Out of proportionate or relative needfulness and desirability evolves the criterion of balance. 

Through custom and through the expressed desires of the people there develops a more or less established, although changing, relationship between expenditures for, and the extent of, the several kinds of public services and improvements in the average community. This relationship may or may not be logical or the product of a true sense of values so far as human welfare is concerned. Usually it represents the service and environment that people think they want or are familiar with, rather than that which, from a more objective point of view, might appear to serve them better. Too frequently it represents facilities and conditions to which they submit or with which they are reasonably satisfied because they know of nothing better or are hopeless of attaining it.

Since any useful criterion of balance must be founded on a reasonable degree of probability, this lag of public knowledge and of public opinion, whatever its theoretical social and economic validity, must be taken into considération. A working criterion of balance, therefore, probably should be based in large part upon actual practice as evidenced by improvements and services now supplied by the average or selected communities plus a certain measure of known possibility of improvement. A standard of balance based entirely upon average existing conditions would in itself provide a worthwhile indicator of clearly sub-standard services or of overexpenditure in one direction or another for badly off-balance communities. 

Illustrations of bad balance of public improvements are numberless. 

They usually result from unenlightened administration or through the pulling and hauling of block interests, promoting one type of improvement or another to the exclusion of others with the frequent result that one or more kinds of improvement or service are extended disproportionately. Street paving and highway building may be carried to a high degree of perfection while parks and other recreation facilities are ignored. A supermodern sewage disposal plant may displace a needed high school, or a city hall or art museum may cripple a city's credit while streets go unpaved and antequated water pipes are bursting.

The logic and need of balance of public expenditures extend through all governmental units from federal to municipal, Inasmuch as there is no absolute method of determining human needs, it follows that there can be no absolute or scientifically founded standard of balance. Nor will any 29

standard of balance that may be derived be proof for long against changing public opinion. Proportionate emphasis upon one type of service or another fluctuates periodically in accordance with current demand growing out of new inventions or rapid social and economic changes. The automobile with its phenomenal development brought corollary demand for good roads and streets. 

Paving expenditures went out of bounds, usually without public demur, almost always with public sanction. Partly coincidental with this era of highway building, and encouraged by the ready funds of prosperity, the school bloc came into its own with "nothing too good for the child". 

Buildings, the tangible evidence of educational facilities, became the monuments and temples of countless municipalities, with public approval, but not always with due regard for proportionate provision of play areas and teaching staffs. As this is written, factors old and new are increasingly pressing toward accentuation of recreation. The automobile has at one and the same time made recreation areas more necessary and potentially more accessible. Increasing leisure both extends the use possibilities of recreational facilities and makes them increasingly necessary to community life and social stability. It

These broad fluctuations in public demand in one sense are evidences of evolution of this so-called balance in public services. is when the impetus of the single-pointed advance of any one kind of facility begins to usurp public funds to the exclusion of other equally needed services, that unbalance develops and adjustment of the capital improvement program becomes desirable. In some sections and in some communities highways and school buildings have been developed in such disproportion. In other States and communities other types of improvement have been overemphasized. This does not mean necessarily that any State or community has too many or too good highways or school buildings but, rather, that other needed services and facilities such as those of recreation, sanitation, culture, and education, have been allowed to lag, frequently because highways or school buildings or something else has absorbed too large a proportion of public funds. 

Standards of balance are variable for different parts of the country depending upon climatic conditions, intensity and character of development, and the readiness with which various facilities can be supplied. Sewers are not always needed to drain surface water in areas of low rainfall. The need for large municipal, county, and state parks is reduced by the near proximity of mountains or other wilderness areas. 

The need, however, for such things as schools, hospitals, and fire` protection is more constant. Variation in public works between poorer and richer States and communities is rather one of total expenditures than of balance. This is based upon assumption that, regardless of degree of community wealth, the spreading of funds over all essential services, even though resulting in low average standard of services, is better policy than concentrating limited funds upon a few services of disproportionately high standard.

Proportionate amounts of money expended upon the several kinds Actual services of improvements represent only one measure of balance. rendered are more important. Under certain conditions a certain quality 30

of service may cost a great deal more than under other conditions. Location of one city in relation to other cities within the same watershed may compel high-cost sewage treatment, while under other conditions disposal of waste may be accomplished at relatively small cost. Certain cities are required to go far afield for adequate water supply. Some communities are blessed with ready-made, low-cost parks, while others must carve their recreation areas out of despoiled and high-priced land. Standards of balance, therefore, must be compounded of perhaps one part sectional and environmental variation, three parts proportionate expenditures, and ten parts service. 

Standards of balance may be arrived at by joining together, as component parts, more or less arbitrary standards of service, or derived more directly from average practice for the several States and municipalities, as suggested above. The two may or may not coincide, depending upon the approach to standards of service. In practice it will be found that standards of certain services in the United States are uniformly low as measured by services known to be socially desirable and economically feasible. 

In setting up standards of service it would seem both desirable and reasonable that these recognized deficiencies be taken into consideration and that standards be established not at the average of accomplishment, but at or above the peak of accomplishment, as may seem reasonable and yet within the bounds of practicality. It has been pointed out that normal progress is made by long disproportionate leaps first in one direction and then in another. Highways, then school buildings, then parks or some other facility may in turn become far advanced beyond other needs. It is obviously unsound, therefore to base standards of service entirely upon current accomplishment in any special field of service unless it be perhaps by taking the scattered peaks of accomplishment as measures of possibility, even probability; upon the assumption that with municipalities and States as it has been with men in all past ages, there are those that represent high degree of advance as measured by the standards of any social order or any age and which are the forerunners or indicators of what the future has in store.

Furthermore, if standards are to serve as guide and stimulus toward improvement of services and average environment rather than act as depressants upon the more advanced communities, it is essential that they be established at or near the peaks of actual accomplishment plus a reasonable allowance for progress occasioned by current scientific and social advance. 

It follows that the best standards of balance are probably to be comprised of standards of service so derived. Both standards of balance and of individual services are necessarily subject to change and development, as suggested above. Their practical application will be discussed in subsequent chapters. 

It should be said perhaps that standards of balance and of service are less easily determined for the Federal Government than for states and municipalities. This is especially true of those fields in which the Federal 31

Government renders supplementary service, as in the case of highways, parks, port development, the advancement of agriculture, and the control of disease and insect pests. For any given State or locality services can be appraised properly only by taking into consideration the sum total of services of a given class provided by all agencies, federal, state, county, and municipal. 

Of all contributions to service rendered in these fields of joint endeavor, that of the Federal Government is usually most of a supplementary character and less subject to absolute determination as measured by actual requirements. The proper amount of such aid is more likely to be determined by peculiar local economic or physiographic conditions than from the point of view of the general welfare of the country at large. Proper proportion of federal expenditures in one direction or another is to be determined more logically in many instances by other criteria than those of standards of balance and of service.

The complexity of criteria for the selection and programming of public works increases with advance from a state of scarcity of needed services to one of relative plenty. In emergence from an era of dirt roads it was simple enough to select, for improvement, extensive highway mileage connecting principal population centers and special points of interest and concentration. Now, with primary highway systems largely constructed in certain localities, effective programming of further highway building must be with consideration beyond the satisfaction of primary needs to the perfection of system, to anticipated population and industrial trends, and to other factors in the continued utility of highways and prevention of premature obsolescence. With expanded need and demand for highways there comes greater necessity for care in the selection and designing of individual projects. It requires comparatively little discrim ination to select a useful park area for a city devoid of parks. But as a city approaches comparative saturation in recreational facilities, parks and playgrounds must be selected and designed with increasing view to proper distribution and to adaptation to future urban growth and development. 

Justifiable improvement of facilities adequate for basic needs, such as the tapping of distant and costly water sources to replace the cheap chlorinated supply near at hand, as cempared with correction of other de ficiencies is a more difficult problem than, for instance, provision of a public water supply where previous dependence has been upon individual wells or a nearby stream. This complexity of criteria is further aggravated by increasing complexity of the social and economic order with 

Under such evolving corresponding expanding demands for public services. conditions, the simple, off-hand method of approving individual projects upon their face value must give way to the process of comprehensive planning, whereby all the physical needs of a community or State are assembled in a composite picture wherein each project may be seen in its relation to all other projects and in its probable hearing upon future requirements within its own and all other classes.

Little less definite than the quality of needfulness as discussed earlier in this chapter is that of economic feasibility based upon public 32

capacity to pay the cost. With every billion dollars added to public expen~ ditures a new and louder cry goes up asking how and by whom the cost is to be met. Forces are divided between those who urge economic salvation through reduced public expenditures and those who look upon large expansion of public expenditures as an important factor in economic recovery and sta~ bility. Much depends upon point of view as to what comprises cost. In the last analysis, it would seem entirely demonstrable that for a relatively closed economic system such as the United States, the only real cost is that represented by irreplaceable natural resources consumed. Dollar cost, so far as the United States is concerned, to the extent that it does not involve an unfavorable balance of trade with other countries, has no significance other than the favorable one of increased circulation of dollar wealth among the people of this country. In other words dollar cost to the Federal 

Government is merely so much stimulation of the exchange of credit and currency among ourselves, a process which presumably might be repeated indefinitely and with high degree of acceleration with no economic result other than increase of apparent, and possibly actual, prosperity. More important to the Nation, coming from general recognition of the true factors in real cost, would be the almost inevitable by-product of improved public service and improved general living standards. Such by-product might well become the prime objective.

The same principle holds for every isolated and practically selfsufficient community regardless of size. It does not hold for any community which must go beyond its borders for any considerable proportion of required materials and labor, or, in other words, for any community wherein public works involve an unfavorable balance in exchange of supplies and service. 

This means that, for the United States, it is probable that no agency other than the Federal Government can safely proceed with public works and public expenditures with cost consideration limited to the rate of exhaustion of natural resources. From one legitimate point of view, however, the social problems of this country are national problems.. It is both socially and economically desirable that living standards be protected and raised on a national scale. The natural resources of the United States represent a large proportion of the national wealth desirably to be used and to be conserved in the national interest. It probably requires, therefore, only ingenuity in organization and in finance to extend this theory of obtaining public works and of improving public service through credit and currency circulation to all classes of public works under all jurisdictions.

Perhaps the very logic and simplicity of this theory as to real cost should be sufficient warning of possible impracticability, and yet stranger things have been observed than the obscurance of fundamentally sound and workable principles by the traditions and prejudices of established customs. It is quite possible that, in this particular instance, the only effectual barrier to an accelerated rate of circulation directed toward extensive public improvements is the accumulation of traditions surrounding the old established credit system of the United States. 33

This discussion of real cost of public works versus the popular conception of cost may seem somewhat far afield from the question criteria. 

In fact it has little bearing upon the selection of individual projects or in discrimination between classes of public works. It is an important fac tor in arbitrary limitations imposed by public opinion upon public expenditures, which, in its effect upon total funds available, tends to restrict practicable standards and to place arbitrary limits of feasibility upon the scope of certain classes of public works. Bee 

The same

Economic justification or feasibility, however, has other significance when applied to selection of individual projects. An assured self-liquidating project that is, a project whose cost, management, and maintenance are paid directly by the users - generally is held to be clearly justified. It is economically justified in the same sense that a successful grocery store or theater is economically justified. difficulty of predetermination of the probability of success applies to public works projects of this character as to private enterprise and depends largely upon the number of users and their willingness to pay the toll. It does not follow that all non-self-liquidating projects are not. economically justified. There are few public works projects whose value and benefits can be determined solely in terms of dollars and cents. 

Usually, even where the economic factor predominates, there are more or less intangible social implications that cannot be measured in dollars and cents and many widely disseminated factors, the dollar value of which cannot well be calculated or assigned. It should be possible, for instance, to estimate rather closely the economic justification of a canal designed solely for purposes of navigation. But the availability of other forms of transportation, both highway and rail, creates complications. 

Justification for the canal is to be measured, not by the service it might render if it were the only means of transportation, but by the difference between service it renders and service otherwise available. This differ- ence is not always easily calculated. Efforts to justify certain classes of public works on purely economic grounds frequently ascribe to individual projects or classes of public works economic effect or implications which properly should be more widely spread. For instance, it has been claimed that good highways provide the basis for the entire automobile industry from the production of gasoline to the manufacture of radiator caps. out good roads there would be little use for gasoline or automobiles, but the hundreds of thousands of workers who devote their lives to the refining of oil and to the making of tires have need for public services other than highways, and taxes upon the product of their labor must be spread to these other purposes.

With- Economic feasibility of an individual project in a particular State or city may depend entirely upon the credit status or taxing capaci ty of that State or municipality. The determination of the feasibility of a project on these grounds is entirely a matter of finance and is not a subject for determination by more generalized criteria. 34

The complexity of essential criteria, both as to number of factors. involved and as to difficulty of application, varies greatly with different kinds of public works. As suggested above, the needfulness of pure and adequate water supply and of facilities for sewage disposal is usually determinable simply upon the grounds of absence or presence of these facilities. 

Complexity with respect to determination of these public works is limited largely to questions such as the direction and extent of provision that sould be made for future growth and expansion and, in the case of water supply, to choice between sources offering water of varying degrees of natural potability. Determination of the relative needfulness and proper proportionate expenditures for street paving, grade-crossing elimination, school building, city halls, parks and playgrounds, port development, and most other kinds of public works is much less simple and involves many interrelated intangibles. A considerable degree of need may be easily demonstrable in every instance. Proportion or balance is determinable only by coordinated and well-considered judgment with guidance of standards as suggested above. Consciously or unconsciously, judgment must be passed upon many interrelated factors including those of growth probability, social and physical well-being, and probable limitation upon construction funds. Most to be avoided is the placing of the several public needs in separate cubicles, especially during the stage of planning and programming. 

In such cubicular planning and programming of public works lies the greatest source of unbalance in expenditures and service and of unwisely placed project. By planning thus in vacuo, the body of applied criteria becomes deceivingly simplified and dangerously reduced. Around each of the several classes of public works so planned and programmed there tends to develop a group of more or less single-minded supporters who, almost certainly, will carry the special class of public works concerned out of bounds in proportion to organized strength and enthusiasm. The Federal Government is especially prone to cubicle planning and programming because of its size and diversification.

The Federal Government is also especially subject to what might be termed pork-barrel balancing of expenditures, with concentration varying from time to time upon whatever class of public works may be foremost in the public mind or most readily subject to so-called equable distribution. The peculiar difficulties of applying board and adequate criteria in the selection and programming of federal public works are discussed in Chapter IV, but perhaps it should be said here by way of illustration that the tendency toward so-called pork-barrel distribution of federal funds has been perhaps most pronounced in connection with waterways and port and harbor development. 

Pork-barrel balance, in the sense of distribution of federal funds with view to equal or proportionate benefit of the several sections of the country from which these funds are derived is entirely justified. Evils come from attempts to provide distribution in kind. The spending of a million dollars for the improvement of navigable waterways in Ohio offers little basis for spending a proportionate amount for the same purpose in North Dakota or 35

Wisconsin. These States had better take their share in the form of highways, or reservoirs, or national parks. In applying such so-called porkbarrel balance, the economic unity of this country should be held in mind, and recognition should be had that equable distribution is not necessarily geographic. Such public works as the development of the port of New York, the building of the Panama Canal, the preservation of the great forests of the Northwestern States may have reverberations of benefit and utility extending throughout the country.

In summary there are the following several principal criteria or groups of criteria applicable to public works in the United States in degree varying considerably in applicability according to the kind of public work and the type of jurisdiction under which it falls. (1) The criterion of balance, including considerations of proper proportion between expenditures within a limited budget for the several kinds of public works and based primarily upon balance and proportion in the several services rendered. (2) The criterion of standards, whereby deficiencies are meas→ ured by arbitrary standards of service established at or somewhere near the peak of actual accomplishment, plus reasonable probability. (3) The criterion of essential services, applicable to such basic needs as those of water supply, sewage disposal, and fire protection, with the chief determinant simply that of whether physical conditions and degree of population concentration make these services essential to community well-being. (4) The criterion of cost, including the following factors: amount of total available income dependent upon community wealth, upon public opinion, and upon public view as to what represents the real cost of public improvements; funds available for a given class of public works as determined by balance; and value to be received and benefit to be de rived from a given project at a given cost, in consideration of all other needed improvements of the same class. (5) The criteria of relative need and relative benefit of individual projects in relation to and in consideration of all other needed improvements as determinable by coordinated and comprehensive plan. These criteria are the determinants of sequence of projects. (6) The criteria of trends and of growth and development potentialities, by which the extent and character of future required improvements and services may be measured. Such trends and potentialities in turn are determinable by exhaustive survey and analysis of conditions and trends and by comprehensive long-range planning. 36

(7) The criterion of social and economic desirability, which for practical application must be based largely upon such arbitrary standards as may be established under Item Two above. (Many conceivable public works are quite without economic utility or social desirability as simply to be determined by common sense. The economic and social desirability of expansion of accepted and useful services is relative and in the last analysis limited only by public opinion and upon limits set by public opinion upon public expenditure.) (8) The criterion of emergency, with application varied according to whether projects involved are those for the replacement of necessary public works destroyed by fire, flood, or other catastrophe, or whether these public works represent speeded-up execution of normal expansion or development of improvements and services. (The latter can be foreseen; the former cannot. In both instances the criteria listed above should be ap plied to whatever extent may be practicable in the same manner as under nor~ mal procedure. In the first instance the criteria of balance and cost may need to give way to necessity. In the case of simple speeding up of a program as relief for unemployment and economic depression, the only justification for failure in the orderly application of criteria listed above is that of lack of adequate plans prepared in advance. But for lack of advance plans, in view of the experience of recent years, there is no justification. 

Among the criteria of emergency is the criterion of proportion of dollars spent, going directly to labor. Such a basis of selection of projects becomes less important and probably fades into insignificance when advance upon public works is nation-wide, and when the force of secondary and terti~ ary circulation effectually equalizes distribution of the public works dollar, whether spent directly for labor, for raw or fabricated materials, or even perhaps for land.

This perhaps almost too lengthy discussion of the general problem of formulating criteria and standards as guides for the selection and programming of public works is offered partly as ground-work for and partly as explanation of the more detailed definition of specific criteria and of suggestions for their use and application contained in the following three chapters. While criteria of the sort here suggested are none of them entirely new, their terminology and their use may, in certain instances, be so unfamiliar as to suggest some thin air source. It has seemed necessary, therefore, that the reader be taken into the secret of their origin. 37

CHAPTER IV CRITERIA IN THE SELECTION AND PROGRAMMING OF FEDERAL PUBLIC WORKS 

The Federal Government includes ten departments and twenty or more independent offices, all of which with few exceptions have some degree of construction as direct responsibility. In the case of the independent offices, construction responsibility frequently is limited to a single project; a bridge, a monument, or a hospital. Some of the departments, on the other hand, are responsible for a great range and variety of construction and development projects and operate both in construction and in administration through numerous departmental divisions or bureaus. Work of the independent offices is, for the most part, so limited in scope and extent as to have little or no significance to this discussion. 

The ten major departments and their general construction and development responsibilities are as follows: 

1. Department of Agriculture. This Department is primarily a service agency created and administered for the advancement of agriculture, but there fall within its jurisdiction certain functions of more general national interest including the construction of public roads and the administering of Federal aid to State highway building; and the advancement of forestry, including operation of national forests. With the exception of the Bureau of Public Roads and the Forest Service, the construction and physical development responsibilities of the several bureaus and divisions of the Department Agriculture are limited largely to the housing of offices, laboratories, and other facilities, essential to internal operation and administration. 2. 

Department of Commerce.- The Department of Commerce, likewise, is in considerable part a service agency but has under its jurisdiction such development and construction responsibilities as: the development of airways, and the construction of lighthouses and of other aids to navigation. 

3. Department of the Interior.- Principal among the construction and physical development responsibilities of the Department of the Interior are: national parks, land reclamation, and improvements incidental to the

Indian Service. Reclamation projects are considered self-liquidating and therefore not to be regarded as much as objects of federal expenditure as of federal investment. In addition to the above there have been created in the Department of the Interior, by the National Industrial Recovery Act and through Executive Order, certain emergency operating divisions centered within the Public Works Administration. Under this organization, and in addition to its directing capacity in relation to federal-aid or federal-subsidized federal, state, and local public works, the Public Works Administration assumes direct responsibility, through specially 38

created public corporations, for the construction and operation of federal housing and subsistence homestead projects. 

4. Department of Justice.- The Department of Justice is charged with the location and construction of Federal prisons and indirectly concerned with housing of the Federal courts. 

5. Department of Labor.- Construction responsibilities of the 

Department of Labor are limited largely to the housing of administrative offices. 6.

Department of the Navy. Beyond the construction and maintenance of navy yards and a limited number of naval bases, hospitals, and so forth, the Navy Department is not concerned with internal or land construction or development. By far the greater part of its capital expenditures is directed toward fleet construction and equipment. 

7. Post Office Department.· 

The functions of the Post Office De partment are chiefly operative and only indirectly concerned with construction, chiefly in connection with post-office housing, which is the direct responsibility of the Treasury Department. 

8. Department of State.- The Department of State likewise is not a construction agency except with respect to the housing requirements of domestic and foreign offices. 9.

Treasury Department.- The Treasury Department has direct responsibility for the construction of post-office and Federal-court buildings and for other housing incidental to its administration, including customhouses and United States mints. 10. 

War Department. In addition to its natural responsibilities for the construction and maintenance of land defense facilities, hospitals, and other projects essential to its operation as an agency of defense, the War Department, through its Corps of Engineers is charged with the responsibility of river and harbor development; with the construction of Federal canals; and with flood control. In average volume the capital expenditures of the Corps of Engineers of the War Department far exceed those of any other department or division except the Bureau of Public Roads in the Department of Agriculture. It is also noteworthy that the disbursements of the 

Bureau of Public Roads are largely in the form of Federal aid to States, while expenditures of the Corps of Engineers are almost if not quite entirely for projects under its direct jurisdiction. 

A number of the divisions and bureaus of certain of the departments listed above perform services of direct and basic value and application to the construction and development projects carried on by other divisions of the same department and by other departments. Notable among these basic services the soil and land-planning studies of the Department of Agriculture, and the surveys and maps of the Coast and Geodetic Survey and of the Geological Survey. are: 39

Policies of and Criteria Used by the Several Federal Construction and 

Development Agencies 

For all public works, in their selection and programming there are two sets of policies and criteria: (1) Those comprising official policy as set forth in authorizing legislation, public documents, and official statements printed and oral; and (2) Those comprising applied policy and growing out of adjustment to conditions and circumstances and in adaptation to political expediency. The one is definite in statement but oftentimes obscure in application. The other is obscure and indefinite in statement but rather more obvious in result. The one is available to all who read. The other requires long and intimate observation to arrive at the facts. If, therefore, in the following analysis of the policies and procedures of the several Federal agencies a degree of incompleteness or discrepancy in fact should be apparent to those intimately familiar with the practices of one department or another, it is hoped it will be borne in mind that the writer has been dependent for information largely upon "official" statements coupled with observed results, without opportunity within the limited time at his disposal to trace the path between the two or to discover all mitigating circumstances.

Because of the intricacies of so wide a range of activities it is with no little trepidation, therefore, that the following discussion of procedures of the several principal Federal construction and development agencies is offered.

Department of Agriculture Bureau of Public Roads.- The chief function of the Bureau of 

Public Roads is administration of Federal-aid funds for state highway building, and the bulk of its disbursements is for this purpose. Prerequisite to grants of aid to States are: submission of a suggested coordinated system of Federal-aid highways by individual States to the Secretary of Agriculture and approval by him; and the submission of construction plans for individual projects, likewise to be approved by the Secretary of Agriculture. The Bureau of Public Roads undertakes to coordinate the 

Federal-aid highway plans of neighboring States in the interests of a unified national highway system but has made little progress with initiating a comprehensive national highway plan other than that built up by the joining of proposals made by individual States. 

The original authorization for Federal aid limited the Federalaid highway systems of individual States to primary highways with mileage not to exceed seven percent of the total state highway systems of respective States. Authorization has since been extended to include secondary roads. and extensions of Federal-aid roads through municipalities and permits, under certain conditions, extension of the seven percent limit. Limit of permissible aid has ranged during different periods from ten to twenty thousand dollars per mile. Larger grants are permissible for bridges and viaducts and, by recent enactment, full construction costs for grade-crossing improvements are allowable from Federal aid. Authorized use of Federal 40

funds has also been extended to the landscaping of Federal-aid highways. 

Federal funds are apportioned among the several. States as follows: one-third in the ratio which the area of each State has to the area of all the States; one-third in the ratio which the population of each State has to the total population of all States; and one-third in the ratio which the mileage of rural post roads and star routes in each State has to the total rural post road and star route mileage in all States.

Under the above procedure, with establishment and creation of the national highway system largely dependent upon State initiative, both composite plan and individual projects are subjected to criteria little better or worse than the criteria employed by individual States. Especially is this true of individual projects other than those comprising the most obvious of national trunk highways. In the virtually complete absence of comprehensive State plans it is obvious that the most important criterion, that of long-range comprehensive plan, has been and continues to be lacking as basis for the selection and programming of virtually all of the present Federal-aid highway system. The extent to which availability of such plans would have altered the present system is, of course, not determinable. It is probable that greater influence of plans up to the present time would have been expressed in altered location and design and sequence of individual projects than in their point-to-point service functions. 

Other than the requirement of factors of coordination and of appropriate design and construction specifications, the principal criterion applied by the Bureau of Public Roads in consideration of individual Stateaid projects is economic justification based largely upon estimates of present and potential traffic demand. Immediate traffic importance of an individual project can be determined fairly well by traffic counts covering regional traffic movement for the area of areas to be served. Estimates of potential traffic, including that of development over a considerable period of years can be arrived at in approximation, only upon the basis of comprehensive regional or State plans which, as pointed out above, are rarely available to the Bureau of Public Roads.

The narrowness of available and applied criteria in the selection of Federal-aid roads is of greatest significance in the more rapidly and more intensively developing sections of the country. Meeting of the primary highway deficiencies of less developed sections is properly regarded by the Bureau of Public Roads as still being a comparatively simple problem limited largely, in individual instances, to the selection and improvement of single well-designed, direct connections between di sconnected points of major interest or population concentration. 

The progressive difficulties of programming highway construction are described by E. W. James in Civil Engineering for March 1934, as follows: 

"During the first years that this Federal-aid improvement program was under way, it was relatively simple in most States to 41

select practically all this limited mileage by studying the distribution of population in urban centers and the total tonnage of agricultural produce by counties. These data indicated the points which should preferably be joined and the relative importance of the intervening territory. "No thought was given, or needed to be given under the circumstances, to speed of construction. Except in a few 

State systems, the mileage of connected highway in the United States was relatively small. The growing demand of the motoring public for improved roads and the general need for such roads made it unlikely in the early years of construction that any serious error would be made in selecting road projects. The funds to be spent each year were never known in advance, since the program was entirely dependent on the money made available in the several States. "From the start an effort was made to correlate construction so that the areas most advanced economically would be considered first and so that, as far as possible, continuous and connected routes would be improved. Under a definite budgetary policy, with the amount of the available funds definitely fixed each year and known in advance, it will be necessary to plan construction programs more carefully and with due regard to the requirements of the whole national plan. 

Under present procedure in designating the Federal-aid highway system there is general failure to coordinate the highway system with other forms of transportation. This also is an inevitable outgrowth of the lack of comprehensive plans, regional, State, and national. It is not necessarily or in any large part a fault of the Bureau of Public Roads but is inherent in the practice of what is termed in the previous chapter "cubicular planning" and is to be overcome only by long-range comprehensive planning approach.

From the point of view of developing an adequate national highway system the arbitrary limitation of Federal-aid roads to approximately seven percent of the State highway mileage of individual States and the limitation of funds in ratio of area, population, and mileage of rural post roads, are something of a handicap. It appears that the origin of the seven percent limitation had no basis other than that this percentage would provide certain of the low-mileage states such as Nevada and Arizona with roads extending across the breadth of these States in two directions. It is possible that, superimposed over the Federal-aid plan of national highway construction, there should be some provision for direct Federal construction of primary national highways. Certainly there should be a national highway plan initiated by the Federal Government as a structural part of the physical national plan; such national highway plan to become the basis for all 

Federal highway expenditures and for all Federal-aid grants to States.

It is assumed that both such Federal planning and construction would be carried on in close coordination with the work of individual States. 42

Factors in the determination of the relative merits and justification of an individual major highway project are: 1. 

First and by far the most important, the relation of project to comprehensive plan, which plan should indicate the potentialities of the region or regions served and all existing and proposed transportation facilities of all types. 

2.- Primary purpose to be served;- commercial or pleasure, as determinant of type, location, and gradient. 3.- 

Potential volume of traffic to be served as determined by traffic counts and growth potentialities of area to be served; - broken down for seasonal and all-year-round average and peak traffic. 

4.- Bearing of projected highway of favorable or unfavorable development of area traversed and area to be served. 

5. Relative importance of service. (The importance of providing long-distance travel facilities over wide stretches of territory lacking such service may outweigh considerations of actual traffic volume.) 

6. Relative prospective obsolescence of one route as compared with another by reason of threatened enveloping urban development. 

7.- For commercial routes especially, consideration for avoidance of duplication of routes of other adequate transportation facilities. 

8.- Relative extent of localized traffic congestion to result from one location as opposed to another. 9.- 

Probability of development of abutting territory and of increase in traffic demand upon the highway as factors in appropriate width of right-of-way. (A safe rule for major highways is to determine the proper width, as suggested above, and then double it in the interests of future improvement and protection.) 10.- 

Relative absence of traffic hazards and of cost of removal of hazards for one route as opposed to another. 

11. Relative need for the projected highway in view of other highway facilities existing as compared with other needed services; assuming necessity of choice in the disposal of limited construction funds.

It will be noted that, although the above factors are indicated as additional to the primary factor of comprehensive plan, they are almost without exception individually dependent upon comprehensive plan for determination. This is so uniform a characteristic of the programming of public works that it scarcely bears repetition. 43

Forest Service. The Forest Service has made an exhaustive survey and analysis of national forestry requirements as represented by its report to the Secretary of Agriculture dated March 30, 1933. As extensive as were the studies leading up to this report, however, no attempt was made to designate specific areas for public acquisition. The report is far-reaching in setting up principles and criteria for building up the national forest reserves. National forest acquisition up to the present time has been largely through transfer from the public domain and by exchange of public domain for private lands, under conditions set up by congressional action. There has been comparatively little direct purchase of national forest land (and most of this in the eastern states) requiring discrimination by the Forest Service in the selection of areas. Such discrimination as has been allowed the Forest Service has been more of the nature of designation of use and management of owned lands. 

The policy of acquisition of forest lands through exchange from the eminent domain has very largely controlled in the public domain (western) states since 1908 and has been operative through "56 laws of more or less general application and 13 laws specifically describing the lands subject to exchange. The net gain in national forest area through such exchange has been in excess of 800,000 acres. The only general criterion apparently used in the exchange process has been that the land acquired be better natural forest land than the area exchanged. Purchase of national forest land is considerably circumscribed by provision of the so-called 

Weeks Law that: 

"No deed or other instrument of conveyance shall be accepted or approved by the Secretary of Agriculture under this act until the legislature of the State in which the land lies shall have consented to the acquisition of such land by the United States." 

In its national plan for forestry, the Forest Service has set up a number of important objectives and criteria. The first objective is to conserve and develop the Nation's timber resources in scale commensurate with current timber use. They estimate that the current consumption of saw timber is five times that of growth rate and that total consumption of timber is twice that of the growth rate. The Forest Service estimates further that about four hundred and fifty million (450,000,000) acres of commercial timber land, with ownership and management divided, national, state, municipal, and private, will be needed to supply the timber requirements of a national population of one hundred and fifty million. A second objective is to turn to timber production or forest use, under either public or private management, all lands adapted to forest use and less adapted to or not needed for other uses.

Both objectives are founded on a number of considerations which in a sense comprise the more important criteria for the accumulation and selection of national forest land and which include the following: (1) Production of timber commensurate with present and probable future use requirements. 44

(2) Flood control and the prevention or reduction of land erosion. (Gained through reduction of the rate of run-off by more extensive forest coverage.) (3) The protection of watersheds from which public water supplies are drawn. (Both for equalization of flow and for prevention of pollution.) (4) The provision of recreation facilities. (There are more than thirty million visitors annually to the national forests attracted by some form of recreation, camping, fishing, sight-seeing, nature study, or other such opportunities freely offered.) (5) The protection and utilization of otherwise waste land threatening to become public liability or negative in the national economy. (6) The creation or perpetuation of employment within and the stabilization of populated areas dependent upon forest industries. (7) 

The preservation of wild life. (8) The preservation of aesthetic values in the landscape. 

In many instances single areas will serve all or most of the above purposes. Seldom will it be found necessary to acquire national forest areas specifically for the protection of wild life, for recreation, or for the protection of natural scenery. These things will be provided almost automatically in any large scale expansion of the national forest areas. They are, however, important secondary factors for consideration and, other things being equal, should be controlling. 

The above objectives and bases for the determination of relative utility of potential national forest areas are as now set up by the National Forest Service. They are still more or less theoretical in that the Service has as yet had little opportunity to apply them. Between this commendable policy and its application lie the problems of the national land policy and of national planning. For many decades the land policy of the 

United States was to rid itself of the public domain. Is that policy now to be aggressively reversed? The Forest Service is convinced that there should be such definite reversal of policy at least to the extent of early increase of the present national forest acreage by approximately a hundred and fifty percent (107,800,000 acres to 254,900,000 acres).

While general policies as to desirable volume of forest land acquisition and as to the proper bases of selection of projects can be established independently by the Forest Service, operation, including the distribution of forest lands and the actual selection of tracts, must or should depend in large part upon the broad, determination of the most advantageous potential 45

use of all the land area of the United States; in other words, the preparation of a national land-use plan. Many factors, as discussed elsewhere in this report, are involved in the making of such a land-use plan, not the least of which is that of the constant shifting of land use between forestry and agriculture. As measure of this problem, it is estimated that more than fifty million (50,000,000) acres of agricultural land have in recent years been abandoned or otherwise made available for afforestation. 

In his letter of transmittal of report to the President of the 

United States, the Secretary of Agriculture says: "A satisfactory solution of the forest problem will require the nearest possible approach to national planning. The laissez-faire and avowedly planless policy of private ownership is failing to meet the situation. The long-time character of forestry itself, the magnitude and cost of the undertaking, and the impossibility of doing immediately everything which must be done emphasizes the desirability of national planning," It is not entirely clear whether "national planning" is here used in the sense employed in this report or used to mean a national approach to forestry. There can be no effective national plan for forestry except as an integral part of a comprehensive national plan taking all national development conditions and requirements into consideration. 

Department of Commerce

Aeronautics Branch.- The Aeronautics Branch undertakes to guide the location and design of airports, but its chief construction responsibilities are those of developing and maintaining major airways and the provision of emergency landing fields along the lines of these airways. Το date the Branch has installed approximately twenty thousand miles of lighted airways equipped with radio communication stations, radio range beacons, and marker beacons.

The location and construction of airways are in that stage of development comparable to laying the first trunk railroads and to construction of the first primary highways, The initial and comparatively simple problem is one of connecting the major areas of concentrated population by the most direct feasible routes. Limiting conditions other than those of required directness are few and simple of conformity. They include the avoidance, so far as terrain and variation in climatic conditions will permit, of such hazards as mountain peaks, areas of high frequency of storms and fogs, and extensive areas affording hazardous landing conditions including large cities and large bodies of water. Location of terminal airports is a relatively small factor in the alignment of airways, involving usually only inconsiderable deflections from natural airway routes.

Location of such airway equipment as light beacons, radio range beacons, and emergency landing fields is naturally largely dependent upon airway alignment and/or topography. 46

Economically, so far as investment in construction is concerned, possible errors or bad judgment in the location of airways is not as serious as in the case of railroads and major highways because of the comparatively small cost of construction, because of the movability of much of the airway equipment, and because of the inconsiderable influence of airways upon the development of the area traversed. 

The location of airports for maximum service and for assurance of continued effectiveness is much more difficult. Ultimately, there will be need for a well-organized, close network of airways covering the entire country and coordinated with an international airway system. Built into and coordinated with the city structure, there will need to be numerous airports distributed and designed in conformity with the landing characteristics and manoeuverability of the future airplane. As aviation so expands, there will need to be increasing dependence upon comprehensive national and local plans which will indicate the direction, intensity, and general characteristics of future land development and population growth. 

Bureaus of Lighthouses and Navigation. Construction responsibili- ties of these Bureaus are limited largely to the location, construction, and maintenance of such aids to navigation as lighthouses and channel markers and buoys. Determinants of location of these structures are largely those of such navigation hazards as shoals, fogs, and narrow and tortuous channels. 

Groundings, navigation difficulties, and volume of water-borne traffic are the supplementary indicators. Superimposed upon these determinants as to location is the factor of judgment as to type of marker required and as to justified expense. Both these determinants and this factor of judgment are largely for departmental decision relatively independent of comprehensive national plan and of other departmental activities except for such obvious cooperation as is required for coordination with projects and activities of the Corps of Engineers of the War Department and except for use of information to be supplied by the Coast and Geodetic Survey. 

Department of the Interior 

Bureau of Reclamation. The United States has been more or less actively engaged in land reclamation through the Reclamation Service since passage of the Reclamation Act in 1902. This act was designed principally for the reclamation of land of the western states through irrigation. By the Acts of 1849, 1850, and 1860 all federal swamp lands had been granted to the states in which they were situated, thus relieving the Federal Government from any major responsibility for land reclamation through drainage. 

The object of the Reclamation Act was to make available for homestead use suitable irrigable lands which could be rendered usable for agricultural purposes at commensurate cost. The storage of water for irrigation 47

purposes and getting this water on the land to be reclaimed were and still are the chief construction objectives. Development of hydro-electric power has become increasingly a secondary objective. Flood control also is sometimes a factor.

Financing provisions of the act were based upon reimbursement of capital expenditures by homesteaders, without interest charges. 

Rate of advance upon the land irrigation program has varied somewhat, periodically, in accordance with public sentiment and with changing view as to the immediate and ultimate agricultural requirements of the Nation. The high price of land and the relative scarcity of food products prevailing during the war period produced a high point of interest in land reclamation. ?

The 1921 Year Book of the United States Department of Agriculture estimated that there were in the United States thirty million acres of irrigable land. There were at that time about nineteen million acres under irrigation, approximately one million two hundred thousand of which were in federal projects. To these there have since been added about four hundred thousand acres, three quarters of which are in federal projects. By natural selection the land reclaimed includes the more promising and least expensive projects. It follows that as work advances toward full reclamation of the irrigable acreage, as estimated above, it will be with increasing cost and, probably, with diminishing return per acre unit.

The land-reclamation program has been pursued without the benefit of a comprehensive land policy or land-use plan designating, from a broad national point of view, the trends of agricultural requirements, and providing for coordinated utilization of all the land areas of the country. Distribution of irrigation projects has been somewhat by political pressure brought by land owners and the representatives of individual states, but only upon determination of engineering feasibility and adequate water supply by the Reclamation Service. In the approval of individual projects, the 

Reclamation Service must take into consideration the amount of water available by storage or diversion; the engineering feasibility of getting this water on the land to be irrigated; and the agricultural potentialities of the land to be reclaimed.

These factors or criteria must attend any well-considered selection of individual irrigation projects. Coupled with them, may be consideration of the potentialities of an individual project for flood control, for public water supply, and for the manufacture of electricity. These factors, under certain conditions, may become the final determinants of feasibility because of contributions to financial return and public service as added justification of project cost. 

Especially in matters of flood control, the objectives of the Reclamation Service and of the Army Corps of Engineers are closely joined. 

There is, however, no evidence of joint operation of the two divisions in 148

this connection.

The Corps of Engineers includes the study of irrigation possibilities with its flood control investigations but apparently does not consult the. Reclamation Service to any appreciable extent in connection with its recommendations.

More important to the reclamation program at the present time than the selection and distribution of reclamation projects is the question of general land reclamation policy;- the question of whether or not, under present recognition of national surplus of agricultural land, there is justification in opening up new land for agricultural use either by irrigation or other means. The solution is not so obvious as it may seem. It may involve the choice, from the point of view of national economy, between producing needed food on comparatively few very fertile acres and upon many acres of outworn lands at proportionately much greater expenditure of energy. Further considerations which might, under certain conditions, justify extension of arid-land reclamation simultaneously with the withdrawal of submarginal areas from cultivation are the better adaptability of the reclaimed areas for the production of certain crops such as melons, potatoes, and many kinds of seed; unusually healthful living environment of project; and provision of perishable food for nearby communities.

National Park Service.- Principal among the responsibilities of of the National Park Service are the acceptance, development, and maintenance of.national parks and national monuments. The general policy of the National 

Park Service is to acquire national park and national monument areas by selection from the public domain or by gift, rather than by direct purchase. 

National parks are selected largely by reason of outstanding physical characteristics, principally those of natural phenomena and superlative natural scenery, for the purposes of preservation and perpetuation of these areas and for making them available and accessible to the people of the United States. There has been some effort to attain regional distribution of national parks, but equalized geographic spread of such areas has its distinct limitations. Both appropriate national parks and appropriate national monuments are where you find them. Unless the present national park standards are materially liberalized, and unless the Federal Government goes extensively into the business of providing regional or sectional recreational areas, now regarded more properly a function of the states, national parks need not be to any large extent dependent upon comprehensive national physical plan. They are more logically subject to independent selection with a view to broad national service.

Under the present policy of the National Park Service there will be little effort to extend national park holdings into new areas other than as is required for the protection and effective development of present national parks. A possible and commendable exception to this policy is that of the desirability of acquiring and preserving, with little structural development, one or more of the few remaining great wilderness areas of the United States. These areas might presumably partake of many of the characteristics of national forests, except that their prime purpose would be that 49

of preservation of wilderness qualities rather than that of the conservation of timber resources, and, for that reason, such areas would be given much more permanent status than is now true of national forests. Selection of such wilderness areas would likewise be dependent largely upon individual characteristics. They too are where you find them and cannot be selected from the point of view of geographical distribution. 

Federal Emergency Administration of Public Works. By far the most important agency in the Department of the Interior at the present time in the field of public works is the Public Works Administration, created under the National Industrial Recovery Act. The Public Works Administration was formed almost over night to serve a great emergency. It was necessary to formulate policies and procedures with almost equal speed and without benefit of comparable experience. This division started as an exclusively administrative agency set up to administer loans and grants or subsidies to already-established construction agencies including the several federal departments, states, counties, and municipalities. Subsequently its functions were extended, through specially formed public corporations, to direct responsibility for the construction and operation of housing projects and subsistence homesteads.

In its first statement of policy the Public Works Administration set up certain tests to be used in determining the eligibility of individual projects submitted for loan and grant. These tests were as follows: (1) The relation of the particular project to coordinated planning and its social desirability. (The Administrator and the State Board will consider ascertained trends as to changes in population, industries, and other social and economic factors.) (2) Economic desirability of the project; that is, its relation to unemployment and revival of industry. (3) The soundness of the project from an engineering and technical standpoint. (4) The financial ability of the applicant to complete the work and to reasonably secure any loans made by the United States. (5) The legal enforceability of the securities to be purchased by the United States or of any lease to be entered into between the applicant and the United States. 

In attempting to apply these tests the Public Works Administration and its Board of Review found themselves at once handicapped, with respect to individual localities, by insufficient compilation and analysis of pertinent 50

date and by the lack of comprehensive plans whereby the relative merit of individual projects might be demonstrated by the applicants or determined by the Administration. In practice, therefore, the Administration was, in considerable degree, compelled to fall back upon those types of projects whose social and economic justification could be most simply demonstrated; among them, water works and sewage-disposal systems. Further, by policy and by requirements of the act, Items Four and Five above became the principal tests of the eligibility of projects. 

A further unfortunate condition of loan, although one which may have been warranted by the requirements of emergency, has been that of restricting loans largely to those projects involving a maximum of direct labor employment and a minimum of land acquisition. While perhaps not serious for a short period of subnormal rate of construction, such a policy has a definite tendency to throw public works off balance for individual states and communities, especially in its effective discrimination against such facilities as parks and recreation areas in favor of public buildings and street and highway paving, of which in many localites there is relative abundance.

As stated in a previous chapter, the experience of the Public Works Administration and its comparative helplessness in determining the relative merits of many kinds of individual projects have brought home to the men concerned with this Administration and to other observers the widespread need for more adequate, comprehensive plans and for the formulation of practicable criteria for use in judging the merits of individual public works projects. 

Department of Justice

Considering the housing of federal courts, along with post offices, as a direct function of the Treasury Department, the planning and construc tion responsibilities of the Department of Justice become limited largely to the location and construction of federal prisons and other correctional institutions.

Criteria for the location of federal prisons are few and simple. 

They include the requirements of a reasonable degree of accessibility to population centers; isolation, to avoid disturbance of the normal functioning of the region in which they are located; and a proper degree of sanitation from point of view of environment and climate. 

Required total capacity of federal prisons depends upon the frequency of crime and the interpretation of what comprises a federal offense. 

Meeting of these requirements is again largely a matter for departmental determination by close observation of trends in crime and crime conviction rather than by national plan and by the use of criteria applicable to many other kinds of public works. Prisons or the lack of prisons may be the result of one kind or another of social planning, but they are of the 51

nature of an unsought appendage to be provided for as necessary and not to comprise an element in objective. 

Department of Labor 

Inasmuch as the construction responsibilities of the Department of Labor are limited to the housing of its own administrative offices, its administration policies do not come properly within this discussion. 

Department of the Navy

The primary capital improvement interests of the Navy are those of fleet construction and equipment but, in addition, considerable expenditures go to the construction and operation of navy yards and naval bases, hospitals and naval schools. Navy yards and naval bases are located with two principal objectives: first, to serve the purposes of naval manoeuvers both defensive and offensive; and second, especially with respect to navy yards, to provide regional distribution of such local or regional commercial advantages as may be derived from this activity. 

Much of the Navy's land construction is necessarily dependent primarily upon the technical requirements of war. To much less degree than in the case of the Army, purposes of naval warfare are equally served by normal and peace-time facilities and services. This is not true, however, to the same extent with such supplementary naval construction as that of hospitals. 

Criteria, if any, applied by the Navy Department in location of hospitals are not easily decipherable from observed location of such institutions as illustrated by a large hospital unit now under construction on a naturally unprepossessing site in proximity to the Philadelphia Navy Yard, where proximity of the navy yard appears to be the only reason for fixing upon this particular site. It would seem logical that considerations of pleasing and healthful environment would apply to naval hospitals as to other hospitals not closely tied to special localities by reason of requirements of accessibility. 

It is further observed that the Army and Navy maintain separate systems of hospitalization, when it would seem that this would be a logical place for coordination and extension to a completely unified plan for federa hospitalization with regional distribution of hospitals to serve all federal requirements.

Post Office Department 

The only construction requirements of the Post Office Department, other than housing in the City of Washington, are those of local post offices Post offices are constructed by the Treasury Department, and policies employed in their location and programming will be discussed under the functions of that Department. 52

Department of State 

The Department of State has no construction requirements other than those of housing its domestic and foreign offices, the locations of which are entirely dependent upon policy as to distribution and selection of embassies, consulates, and domestic branch offices. 

Treasury Department 

For direct administrative purposes the Treasury Department is responsible for housing its administrative offices, custom-houses and United States mints. The national distribution of custom-houses is determined by ports of entry; and, location of United States mints, by convenience of the Department. Except for administrative convenience, the location of these projects is not of large national significance. And their extent and number are directly dependent upon administrative requirements.

More important from the point of view of national service in the public works program is the distribution and progamming of post offices and local federal-court and federal-office buildings as directed through the office of the Supervising Architect. 

The distribution and construction of post offices and federal buildings have perhaps been more subject to pork-barrel distribution than any other single class of federal public works. They are among the first classes of public works to be thought of in connection with an emergency public works program largely because of their adaptability to general distribution. They are seized upon with peculiar eagerness by localities because they involve so little local responsibility. Because of this latter factor they are especially prone to over-construction, and a sane program of post office expansion requires close federal scrutiny rather than too much dependence upon local recommendations. This fact has been recognized by the creation of a special Post Office Committee under the Public Works Administration.

This Committee is undertaking to determine the merits of new postoffice and federal-office projects solely upon the grounds of economic justification which, in this instance, appears to be a sufficient and entirely applicable criterion. The policy of this Committee is to divide post-office buildings into two categories, "first" and "least". Post offices doing a gross business of twenty thousand dollars or less per year are included in the least category; all others in the first category. Present post-office facilities are examined for adequacy of quarters and as to carrying or rental and maintenance costs. If adequate quarters can be rented for less than the carrying charges upon the proposed new building, new construction is postponed until conditions are reversed. The carrying charges and capacity of proposed new structures are weighed against the volume of business done as proven by experience, If upon this basis and after allowance for 53

the requirements of reasonable growth, the proposed structure does not seem warranted, it is either reduced in size and cost or construction is postponed. In this way the present programming Committee proposes to apply the criterion of economic justification. The above relates to federal offices constructed in conjunction with post offices as well as to post-office buildings.

From the point of view of service and specific location post offices are primarily of local concern but are discussed here because in the aggregate they comprise a federal function. Their specific location is best determined by local comprehensive plan. 

General procedure in the initation of public buildings projects through the office of the Supervising Architect of the Treasury Department has been materially altered and improved within the past ten years. The old and present procedures are well described by the National Bureau of Economic Research in a 1930 Report on "Planning and Control of Public Works", as follows: 

"Prior to the passage of the Public Buildings Act of May 25, 1926, the initiation of federal building projects both individually and collectively had rested entirely with the Congress. Under this procedure bills would be introduced by members of Congress for buildings in localities in which they were interested; these in most cases involving acquisition of land. Such bills were then referred to the office of the Supervising Architect for report and estimate of cost. 

With the approval of the Public Buildings Act of May 25, 1926, a totally different line of approach was inaugurated. This act, while fixing the total gross outlay to which the Government might be obligated for public building construction within its purview, for the states, territories, and possessions of the United States;- confines itself as regards selection of locations to defining in a general way only the procedure to be followed; and except as regards, principal authorizations held over from previous legislation, makes no attempt to specify individual projects." 

War Department

The War Department performs two principal functions, each involv ing extensive national planning and construction; two functions not very logically or closely related. The one has to do with the problems of war and defense, and the other with the domestic problems of the development and maintenance of ports and harbors and navigable waterways. 

As in the case of the Navy, the location, character of construction and programming of such implements of war and defense as fortifications, arsenals, proving grounds, and concentration camps are largely to be determined upon technical considerations exclusive to the purposes of war and in accordance with current national policies and necessities. Many equally or 54.

even more important facilities of war, including adequate highways, railways, and air landing fields, have service requirements so nearly identical with those of peace-time activities that practically the same criteria are applicable to them, whichever the objective in view. 

Corps of Engineers. More subject to analysis from the point of view of this discussion are the policies and procedures of the Corps of Engineers in connection with waterway development and maintenance. As stated earlier in this chapter, this Division has a higher average annual expenditure for capital improvements than any other federal department or division except, for very recent years, the Bureau of Public Roads through its administration of federal-aid funds. Expenditures for river and harbor improvement during the past fifty years, principally through the Corps of Engineers has been in excess of two billions of dollars. 

General and official policies of this Division, in the selection of projects, are determined largely by legislative instructions contained in the River and Harbor Act of June 13, 1902 and by subsequent acts and amendments. The Act of 1902 requires the Division to take into consideration for any proposed waterway improvement the extent of potential waterborne commerce and the benefit to commerce represented by savings to be realized through water transportation. A subsequent Act of March 4, 1932 requires the Division to take into further consideration the extent of terminal facilities, either existing or to be provided by local interests and, in addition, the water power potentialities of the proposed improvement. 

Specified considerations are again extended by the Act of June 5, 1320, which requires consideration of the further factors, of general regional and local benefits and extent of local participation in construction cost. 

Section three of the 1902 act is amended by an Act of February 10, 1934, to require. that consideration be given to pleasure traffic service.
