By general policy derived largely from the above legislative in-` structions the Division undertakes to approve projects upon the grounds of economic justification which, however, in fact, has considerable variation and complexity in application, accompanied by the significant circumstance that the carrying costs are not borne by the users of individual waterways but by the Federal Government and that economic justification with respect to dollars-and-cents return is based entirely upon estimated savings to traffic operating upon theoretically cost-free thoroughfares. 

Further factors in economic justification of waterway improvements prescribed by law as suggested above include the prevention of damages incidental to floods and possible returns and services deriving from incidental water power development. By policy of the Division, the factor of savings to users is extended to benefits actually or presumed to be derived from supplying service, competitive to other carriers, principally railroads; competition designed to insure reasonable rates and good service by these privately owned although publicly regulated other carriers. All factors in potential economic return from waterway improvements, except possibly that f potential hydro-electric power, are rather indefinite in character and 55

difficult to determine. proximated. It is considerably dependent upon economic conditions. The only key to preventable flood damage is flood records for a past period, and these records usually are not very satisfactory so far as damage estimates are concerned. Prevention of flood damage brings in many questions of justification including the relative value of property to be protected and that of placing of responsibility for original occupation of lands known to be subject to flood.

Potential traffic volume can be only roughly ap- The Corps of Engineers is now in the process of completing an exhaustive survey of the development and improvement requirements and possibilities of practically all the navigable waterways of the United States. 

An appropriation of six million dollars ($6,000,000) was placed at the disposal of the Division for this purpose. The result of these surveys is being presented in a series of reports commonly known as the "308-Reports". 

These reports cover virtually all navigable streams in the United States and are two hundred and eight (208) in number. At this writing one hundred and eighty-five (185) of these reports have been completed, and one hundred and sixty-two (162) have been submitted to Congress.

As suggested above, the 308-Reports are not extended to nonnavigable streams, but do in appropriate instances extend to the headwaters and to the non-navigable sections of streams in part navigable. In addition to problems of navigation, these reports give consideration to flood control, potential water power development, and to irrigation. Flood control studies, however, are not extended to the corollary problems of afforestation and the prevention of soil erosion. Consideration of irrigation possibilities appears to be incomplete and not coordinated with the studies and program of the Reclamation Service. canals,

Except for the instances noted above and except for projected the 308-Reports appear to be comprehensive so far as the development and control of navigable waterways is concerned. They are not, however, related to any broad plan or policy of national development, nor have improvement proposals been made with any considerable degree of attention to coordination with present and probable other transportation facilities. 

These studies and these reports comprise a sort of blanket coverage of waterway possibilities without translation in terms of relative need and relative economic justification. Such analysis remains for further determination.

The first attempt at programming upon the basis of the 308-Reports is represented by the recent studies of the President's Waterflow Committee; the report of conclusions of which Committee is not yet available. This Committee approached the problem of programming waterway improvements, by dividing the country into six regions or sections and by proceeding to selection of ten favored projects from each region making a total of sixty projects. From these sixty, ten projects have been selected as of greatest immediate importance and merit. Procedure of this special Waterflow Committee does not seem to be especially promising for a number of reasons; 56

some of them quite unavoidable. First of all a program of this sort cannot be prepared with any large degree of assurance without having more of a foundation of comprehensive national plan than has been available to the Committee. The plan of procedure designating an equal mumber of projects for arbitrarily determined regions. with wide variation of waterway improvement requirement's smacks considerably of the old pork-barrel tactics especially familiar in the field of waterway development. Furthermore, inasmuch as the sixty and ten projects are individually in terms of entire river basins, there still remains the selection of individual projects within respective basins before a practicable working program is produced. The development of most of the river basins of the country naturally includes for each of them a considerable number of more or less separate projects of widely varying merit and immediacy of need. As illustration, the two-hundredodd 308-Reports include considerably more than two thousand individual projects.

In summary, the present policies and procedures of the Federal Government in the selection and programming of waterway improvement projects appear to be inadequate in these respects: 

1. The lack of a broad national development objective and plan makes close determination of the relative merit of individual projects impossible and a proper degree of coordination with other transportation facilities difficult if not equally impossible. 

2.- While certain rather intangible social and economic benefits to be derived from waterway improvements cannot well be measured in dollar return, it would seem that purely economic factors should be determined · more upon economic facts than is now the case. For instance, in estimating savings accruing to waterway users, these savings should be calculated as net savings after deduction of interest charges upon the capital investment and maintenance and operating cost. In the case of prevention of flood-damage control savings to property owners, as demonstrated by longperiod flood records, should likewise be figured as the net savings after deduction of capital maintenance and operating cost. 

3. The provision of competitive service for the purpose of controlling local or regional railroad rates and services is, or should be made unnecessary through powers of control of rates and service vested in, or to be vested in, the Interstate Commerce Commission. There are obvious inconsistencies in purported direct control of railroad operation and in federal support and virtual subsidy of railroads and in the spending of millions of federal funds for such indirect persuasion and enforcement as may be derived from competitive service. 

4.- Projects such as the Fort Peck development of the Upper 

Missouri River should be given closer scrutiny in accordance with Items Two and Three above and other factors. (This project is cited merely as illustration.) This development is designed principally to equalize the flow of the upper Missouri River to provide a longer season of navigability, 57

chiefly for the purposes of transportation of wheat. It is estimated that the difference in cost between transportation by water and by rail in this particular instance; while not especially important while wheat prices are reasonably high, will represent the difference between profit and loss under seriously depressed market conditions. These savings to shippers should be estimated as net savings, as suggested under Item Two, and should take into consideration corollary losses to other carriers. Whether privately or publicly owned and controlled, railroads are essentially as much of a national responsibility as is the agricultural industry. Furthermore, justification for such projects as the Fort Peck development is in considerable degree dependent upon national policy and plan with respect to marginal and submarginal agricultural areas, neither of which policy or plan was available to the proponents of this project. 

Central Control Agencies. In addition to such limitations as may be imposed by the President and by Congress, all capital expenditures are subjected to two central controls, one positive and the other advisory. The one is through the Director of the Budget, and the other is through the Federal Employment Stabilization Board. Both are principally concerned with total expenditures by individual departments and divisions and for classes of projects rather than for individual projects. Neither has the benefit of guidance of comprehensive national policy and plan whereby assignment of funds might be made more closely to approximate actual and demonstratable national needs.

Previous to creation of the office of Director of the Budget under the Federal Budget Act of 1921 and as described by the National Bureau of Economic Research in its 1930 Report upon "Planning and Control of Public 

Works": - 

"Expenditures for various purposes were handled by separate Congressional committees, each of which had the recommendations of only one or a few bureaus before it, but none of which saw all the requirements in relation to one another. There was relatively little coordination between the departments in submitting estimates, and contacts between Congressional committees for the purpose of securing a proper balance between the various needs were admittedly inadequate." 

But through the 1921 act:- "Under the budget system the estimates of the different federal departments are brought together by the Director of the Budget, who, as the President's agent, consults with the heads of the departments and individual establishments. The President submits estimates for all the departments at one time in the annual budget, which also shows estimated receipts. The budget estimates are reviewed first by the Committee on Appropriations of the House of Representatives; all appropriation measures must according to the Constitution originate in the House. After action by the House, the various departmental 58

appropriation bills are enacted upon by the Senate, and when the two agree, the measures go to the President for approval before becoming effective."

The Federal Employment Stabilization Board was originally comprised of Cabinet members working through an Executive Director. It has since become a separate advisory agency, advisory to the President and to the Director of the Budget. The original primary function of the Board was the formulation of a federal public works program, balanced principally from the point of view of employment stabilization. It offered, further, a means of comparison and a degree of centralized analysis and coordination, of the expenditures of the several federal agencies. Incidental to these functions the Board compiles and keeps up to date the expenditures of the several federal departments and divisions annually for a period of several years past and proposed expenditures annually for a period of six years in advance. By this effort the Board has been instrumental in stimulating long-range programming by the several federal construction agencies. 

Comparative expenditures by the several departments and divisions, as between these departments and divisions and as for the past and proposed expenditures within individual departments, are scrutinized and analyzed from the point of view of bringing these expenditures into better interrelation and balance. Unemployment trends and trends in the various industries which might be affected one way or another by one kind or another of federal improvement and expenditure are likewise observed and analyzed with object of directing federal expenditures in adjustment to these trends to whatever extent may be practicable.

The work of the Stabilization Board is a step in the right direction, but it does not and cannot go nearly far enough, in the absence of pre-formulated policies and criteria for application to both, classes of projects and individual projects, largely to be determined by and to be contained within the national plan. Balance and equalization of expenditures, whether as means to economy or as tools for unemployment relief, offer little of potential advantage until directly related to a defined national objective and to the comparative needs of the country. 

Procedures with Private and Semi-public Activities of National Importance 

Equally significant, from a national point of view, to development activities carried on directly by the Federal Government, are a number of private and semi-public activities under varying degree of federal control and including, principally, the construction and operation of railroads and the use and development of such natural resources as privately owned forests, hydro-electric power, coal, oil, gas, land, and certain of the more important mineral resources. As stated in Chapter III, and from a point of view of national welfare, many of the same criteria of national plan and policy are applicable to these activities as to the selection and programming of direct federal projects. And furthermore, the effective application of these same criteria to these private and semi-public works is 59

essential to the effective carrying out of any comprehensive federal public works program.

Most, if not all, of these private and semi-public activities are so diversified in management and operation that current policies and procedures in the programming of their respective activities can scarcely be stated in specific terms. Generally speaking, immediate economic gain to the owners or managers concerned is the chief and only criterion applied in determination as to rate and character of expansion. This varies of course inversely with the extent and effectiveness of federal control. specific conditions are as follows: 

More

Railroads. There has been created within the operation of the 

Interstate Commerce Commission the position of a Federal Coordinator of Railroads whose function it is to bring about such coordination and unification of the physical plants and operation of the railroads of the United States as may be possible under diversified ownership and management. Objectives of the Coordinator are primarily to bring about such efficiencies and economies of operation as can be accomplished through unification and coordination of individual railroad systems and through improved equipment and improved terminal facilities. Quite contrary to conditions surrounding most, if not all, of the classes of federal public works discussed above, the railroads of the United States are in most respects definitely over-expanded. Their problem is much more that of contraction and of consolidation of existing plant than of extension of primary trackage. Other than extension of facilities to meet the expanding requirements of a few rapidly developing regions, there remains little or no need for the laying of new railroads. On the contrary, it is estimated that, under pre-depression conditions, more than seventy thousand (70,000) miles of existing railroads are obsolete and subject to abandonment from the point of view of profitable operation. This estimate is based on presumption of continued individual management and operation of railroads and does not take into account such duplications of service as might be eliminated under a unified operation of a national railway system. There are a number of principal factors contributing to such obsolescense of railroads including the exhaustion of local natural resources or the removal or failure of local projects; and competition of motor vehicles and other forms of transport. 

Contraction of railroad trackage has been taking place gradually during the last decade or more. Up until 1916 or thereabouts it had been increasing. First-track mileage owned by American roads increased from 

193,346 miles in 1900 to 254,037 miles in 1916, and by 1930 had declined to 249,052. 

The existing railroad system of the United States has been developed to considerable extent with objectives almost diametrically opposed to the present objectives, unification and elimination of duplicate services, now being forced by circumstances. The system, in its entirety, has been developed through the independent planning and operation of almost innumerable companies each actuated by the prospects of individual profit with frequent emphasis upon direct competition involving duplication of services or the exclusion of services of rival companies for individual gain and oftentimes at public loss. It follows, therefore, that the railroad systems 60

of the country are confronted with a considerable job in coordination and unification of lines, especially at principal terminal and concentration points, and that such unification, together with modernization of service, represent the principal capital improvement requirements of the near future. 

Allocation of further improvements, whether those of expanded terminal facilities or of modernization, must depend largely upon the growth and development potentialities of the regions concerned. These in turn are determinable in degree only by national plan, designating, so far as possible, the future industrial and general use characteristics of various regions and localities. 

A further indefinite factor as to the future part to be played by railroads in the national economy and as to requirements in railroad service is the problematic one of extent to which the use of electrical power, the transportation of oil and gas by pipe lines, and other such modern developments will continue to encroach upon railroad business. 

The economic justification of individual railroads is no less difficult of determination than that of individual waterways. Direct comparison of transportation rates and revenues with those of either highways or waterways is not possible so long as users of highways and waterway's are not called upon to pay the full cost of service provided. Furthermore, below the point of profitable operation of individual railroads, there are certain important, if not almost indispensable, services to be provided and not otherwise available, such as long-distance freight hauling. This is an important consideration from point of view of public interests. 

In conclusion, there is probably no national service facility in the United States more in need of comprehensive planning or more dependent upon comprehensive planning for survival of profitable operation than railroads. It remains to be seen whether or not this can be accomplished under private ownership and divided management, under the maximum of centralized control that can be exerted by the Federal Government. 

Electricity.- Availability of and development of electric power and character and rate of industrial and population growth and development are becoming increasingly interdependent. The development of electricity, along with that of the automobile, has revolutionized distribution and dis-: tribution possibilities of industry, carrying with it equal effect upon population trends and population pattern potentialities. It is estimated that not less than seventy-five per cent of factory machines are now driven by electric motor. Home and farm occupations and their comfort and convenience are becoming increasingly dependent upon electricity. In other words, widespread availability of cheap electric power is highly important, and becoming increasingly so, to general national prosperity and improved living standards.

A primary objective of the national plans of a number of other countries is national electrification to be gained through unified power development making electricity reasonably available throughout the countries 61

concerned. The availability of cheap electricity depends principally upon power sources and maximum distance of economic transportation of electricity. 

For practical purposes the power sources of the United States are relatively fixed and determined. The distance of economical transportation has increased rapidly during recent years and undoubtedly is subject to much further development through invention. The .: 

The two prime sources of electric power are water power and coal. 

It has been estimated that approximately two-thirds of the potential water power of the United States lies within or west of the Rocky Mountains. better grades of coal, in much the greater quantity, are found in the eastern section of the United States. Both east and west, the principal population centers of the country can be served economically by electricity developed at and transported directly from the sources of power. There are large sections of the country, however, which are still dependent upon transportation of fuel for the production of electricity for local use. These areas can be drawn into the range of cheaper electric power by further unification of the major units of electricity production. and by improvements in the means of transportation of electric current. 

Factors in the expansion of electric power service, therefore, are: first, knowledge of extent and character of electric power sources (already fairly well determined by surveys of coal deposits, by the surveys and studies of the Corps of Engineers of the Army, and by numerous other private and public investigations); and secondly, determination of the relative potential needs of the various sections and localities of the country as to be suggested by national plan. 

The accomplishment of generalized availability of cheap electric power calls for a high degree of centralized planning and management. A great deal has already been accomplished in coordination of power sources through the more or less voluntary cooperation of major power companies, under private ownership and management. As with the railroads, it remains to be seen whether or not the objectives of widespread cheap electric power service can be adequately attained under a much divided management, whether public or private. Contrary to the case of the railroads, electric service is faced with the necessity of extensive expansion and development, especially in rural areas and toward cheaper rates. With respect to this particular natural resource there is comparatively little danger of exhaustion. Water will continue to flow down hill and, in many instances, electricity can be produced at and transported from the mine mouth at less cost and with less consumption of coal than is possible through the transportation of coal to point of electricity use.

The special criteria to be applied to the expansion or development of power plants are those of the potential needs of the area within economical transportation distance, as to be determined by comprehensive national plan and based upon potential consumption at reasonable rates rather than 62

at high rates which may already prevail; and the relative cost of electric power production per unit as compared with that of other power sources in the same regions. Variations in these criteria occur in connection with electric power production as a by-product of projects with other primary purposes, such as irrigation, flood control, erosion prevention, and the improvement of navigation. 

Coal. Approximately thirty per cent of the known coal resources of the World are contained within the United States. The 1925 Report of the United States Coal Commission states that less than one per cent of the coal reserves of the United States have been mined. This, however, pertains to all grades of coal, both bituminous and anthracite. twenty-five per cent of the workable anthracite deposite have been consumed. The proportionate consumption of coal is being reduced by the development of other power sources and by more efficient use of fuel. Approximately

It would appear on the face of these statistics that imminent need for caution in, and planning of, coal production and consumption is negligible. So far as total tonnage and rate of consumption are concerned, this is probably true. On the other hand, there is a wide range in the quality of coal and in its availability by practicable mining methods. It is important that high-grade coals continue to be readily accessible at reasonable rates to large consumption areas, and it is to insure this condition that care needs to be given. Natural mining practices have been to concentrate production upon the most easily and most economically mined of the higher-grade coals, with considerable disregard for waste. It is principally in the prevention of unnecessary waste and in the economical transportation of coal to points of consumption that national plan and coal production control should be directed.

The situation with respect to coal is well set forth in the 1922 

Report of the President's Research Committee on Social Trends: 

"The problem of conservation is not to prepare for days centuries hence when all the coal and metal shall be gone, but to minimize the readjustment to a stage of increasing cost which in some of the older lands has already arrived and in the United States is only a matter of time. The prospect is clear enough to make the prevention of needles waste a major social responsibility." 

Oil and Natural Gas. With respect to the extent and rate of exhaustion of the oil and natural gas reserves of the United States the situation is quite different. Because of the character of these resources, it has been much more difficult and virtually impossible to make close estimate of the volume of reserves. New and important discoveries continue to be made. However, the rate of exhaustion of these resources cannot but be alarming, and their production is peculiarly subject to waste due largely to highly competitive production. Natural gas, frequently a highly valuable by-product of petroleum production, is more often than not completely wasted in the process of taking oil from the ground cheaply and quickly. Another 63

economically and socially important phenomenon of the present highly competitive methods of taking oil is the development of boom cities and towns soon to be left stranded by the too rapid exhaustion of the local petroleum fields. More cautious and less hasty withdrawal of oil in some localities would serve to reduce the wastes of both boom building and premature obsolescence of oil-producing communities.

The 

As suggested above, the total quantity of available oil and natural gas in this country appears not to be even approximately known. 

Report of the President's Research Committee referring to above states that: "Were is not for the discovery of new pools, the supply of petroleum would collapse, for the bulk of the output at any time comes from the flush production of new fields."

It is likewise difficult, if not quite impossible to estimate the petroleum requirements of future years. The proportionate future use of petroleum for power production, in time, may be materially reduced or outmoded by further development of the use of electricity and by the invention of gasoline substitutes. Such development and such inventions might in fact be forced by a dwindling supply of petroleum in order to meet the requirements of motor transportation and other modern virtual necessities. But these possibilities of development and invention are in the future and it remains to make most efficient use possible of those facilities already known and at hand. It

The need for control of petroleum production and of conservation of petroleum reserves has been recognized by the Federal Government through the creation of such coordinating agencies as the Oil Conservation Board and by the controls now being exerted by the Department of the Interior. has likewise been recognized in certain of the principal oil-producing states, notably California, Oklahoma, Texas, and New Mexico, through the enactment of control legislation. This generally is a problem more of federal policy than of national physical plan. And there are involved the questions of control of wasteful competition; control of wasteful production methods; perhaps, regulation of maximum production; and, possibly, attention. to the regulation of the uses of petroleum in relation to uses of other more assured resources such as coal, in the production of power and heat. 

Forests. The problem of conservation and development of the forest resources of the United States, so far as direct federal responsibilities are concerned, is discussed earlier in this chapter in connection with the federal Forest Service plan for American forestry. There remains, perhaps, need for some further consideration of the general forest resources of the Nation, including all forests, whether or not feasible of inclusion within the public domain as national forests. It would seem that a simple and yet adequate criterion for the determination of what lands should be retained or developed as forest might be that of relative useability. Beyond assurance of a perpetuated adequate timber supply and beyond the necessities of flood control and erosion prevention, there is the question of use of land 64

which has no agricultural, urban, or other economic or social utility beyond that of fcrest or wild. land. In other words, there remains the problem of utilization of what is commonly regarded as waste land. This waste land, completely disregarded and uncared for, tends to develop as a social and economic liability, while, under some form of forest management, it might usually be turned to some economic and social gain. It is suggested, therefore, that, regardless of direct economic and recreational requirements, all such lands should be assigned to whatever forestry use they may respectively be most suited for.

Such a policy of waste-land utilization would probably involve extended public ownership and management of these lands. The Report of the Forest Service referred to above points out the unsatisfactory results of private ownership and management of forest and waste lands;unsatisfactory results occurring not always out of unwillingness of private owners to give proper care and treatment to these lands but of the inability of private owners to do so because of the nature of the responsibility, involving the necessity of fire protection, which cannot be provided individually; because of the long-time character of necessary investment; and because of the relatively greater cost of management and protection when falling upon small individual owners. 

Soil. Undoubtedly by far the most important natural resource of the United States is land, from the point of view of its agricultural productivity. It is reported by the United States Department of Agriculture in "Arable Land in the United States" (1918) that about one billion acres, or more than one-half of the land area of the United States, is unfitted for the profitable production of crops owing either to rough topography, deficient rainfall, low temperature, or infertile soil; and that about eight hundred and fifty million (850,000,000) acres of land, or fortyfive per cent of the total land area of the United States, are in crops or potentially available for production of crops. Area of land under the first classification, that unsuitable for agricultural purposes by reason of topography or climate, is constant, with the exception of such minor changes as may be accomplished through irrigation. The agricultural acreage is highly variable in degree of original fertility and is subject to material change in productivity by reason of unsuitable use or unwise treatment. 

The immediate agricultural land-use problem of national policy and national plan is less that of insuring an adequate supply of agricultural products for the immediate or near future than that of providing for the well-being of present farm occupants and of agricultural regions and of conserving an adequate productive capacity for future centuries. 

The formulation of a soil-conservation policy will involve the complicated process of determing: (1) Submarginal lands which definitely should be withdrawn from agricultural use; (2) Lands which may be continued in agricultural use under certain conditions as to treatment; and (3) Lands which can be continued in agricultural use presumably indefinitely without restriction in use other than that dictated by intelligent farming. Such 65

designation of use of agricultural lands will, as suggested above, comprise a primary element in the national physical plan, and the plan itself will comprise a principal criterion in the determination of policy with respect to specific areas.

The 

Carrying out such a plan of land utilization will involve further expansion in the direction of such public administration and control as is represented by the present federal program of submarginal land acquisition. 

With respect to Class Two areas it would seem entirely in the interest of both the public and individual land owners that a considerable degree of public control be exerted to insure the proper use of these lands which can be maintained in agriculture only by carefully regulated treatment. science of agriculture is developed to the point where such required treatment can be defined and indicated. By persuasion, or by compulsion, it may be necessary to require owners of this land to conform to proper farming practice. This in principal, while new to agriculture, does not greatly, if at all, exceed many of the regulations necessarily imposed upon private enterprise in urban areas, in the interests of individual and public welfare, Other and Miscellaneous Natural Resources of National Importance.- 

Factors to be considered in determining necessary guidance or control in the rate and method of use and production of other natural resources are uniformly those of:- 

1.- Importance of the resource concerned to the national economy and to social welfare. 

2.- Extent of resource; the rate of exhaustion; and probable future requirements. 

3.- Susceptibility to replenishment by known practicable means. 

4.- Extent to which waste can be avoided by improved treatment or improved methods of production or handling. 

5.- Extent to which the drain upon one resource, limited in quantity, can be reduced by substitution of more plentiful and less essential materials. 

Conclusion 

As indicated in Chapter III and elsewhere in this chapter, the relative merit and justification of individual public works are dependent in determination upon several underlying factors, principal among which are national policy with respect to general national development and with respect to the kind of public work or to the type of natural resource concerned; the relation of the specific project to parallel or supplementary services provided locally under other than federal jurisdiction; and the relation of the 66

project concerned to other parts and other phases of the physical national plan. There follows the justification of individual projects upon these bases, consideration of the more specific criteria relating to the fitness or adaptability of individual projects to intended purposes. These secondary criteria are more directly concerned with specific location, design, and cost. Without the primary bases of judgment, selection of projects must be by means of applying secondary criteria only, with all questions of broader relationship left to unfounded guess-work. 

In very large degree all Federal public works and most State and municipal public works have been limited in their determination to judgment founded on these secondary criteria. This is not to say that all or even any great proportion of public works constructed have been unsound, either in extent, location, cost, or intended purpose.

A great many improvements are obvious in justification and in solution of problems involved. In many instances, availability of national policy and comprehensive plan would suggest alteration only in degree, if at all. That many serious errors have occurred by reason of lack of coordination and plan and defined objective, however, is without question. 

Furthermore, at the present stage of development of this country with its social and economic complexities and with its extensive demands for and requirements of service, the proportion of development problems with obvious solution is much less than it was a hundred, or even twenty or ten years ago. There can be no further assurance of a reasonably rational development of this country except upon the basis of thorough-going coordination and comprehensive plan.

It is implied above that there is a considerable relationship‍ between criteria to be applied in the selection of Federal and of local public works. This is true especially with respect to those services and these facilities for the provision of which responsibility is divided between Federal, State, and local Governments, and particularly for such things as highways, parks, public forests, and certain correctional, educational, and eleemosynary institutions. These criteria are especially those relating to the total and relative quantity and quality of service rendered. These interrelated criteria can be applied effectively only through completion of the plan pattern down through the scale from national to local plan.

Throughout the above discussion, emphasis frequently has been placed upon the isolated workings of individual departments and divisions. 

Emphasis likewise has been placed upon the physical elements in national development. It is assumed that the suggestions contained in this chapter are to be operative through some such planning and coordinating machinery as is indicated in Chapter II and that the scope of the comprehensive national plan, to serve as the primary criterion in programming public works, also, is to be somewhat as defined in Chapters I and II. 67

CHAPTER V CRITERIA AND STANDARDS FOR STATE PUBLIC WORKS 

Development and service responsibilities delegated wholly or in part to states customarily include provision for major highways and bridges, state parks and state forests, penal and correctional institutions, hospitals for the infirm and for the insane, flood control, education, and the protection of wild life and game. Some states are further concerned with the protection and development of fisheries, the prevention of beach erosion, port development, land reclamation, and other improvements or services pęculiar to local requirements: Scarcely without exception, and perhaps entirely so for many states, these facilities and services are supplementary to or supplemented by facilities and services of like categories supplied by the Federal Government and/or by counties and municipalities. There is, however, considerable of logical differentiation between the established responsibilities of the Federal Government, the states, counties, and municipalities, based principally upon scale and relative importance and extent of projects. The operation of such division of responsibility may be illustrated by policies and procedures with respect to parks and recreation areas. 

The Federal Government is charged with preservation and maintenance of those few large superlative areas which by character are of interest to the Nation as a whole and which usually are too extensive to be within the means of or essential to the service of the states in which they are found. To individual states is delegated responsibility for providing needed large natural park areas primarily for state use and too large and too expensive to be handled by the individual county or municipality. Beyond these national and state park requirements is need for smaller and more intensively developed parks and playgrounds distributed throughout all urban areas. Such parks and playgrounds are primarily for local benefit and use and fall properly to local responsibility. .

The same logical basis of division of public service responsibilities follows generally through all public works. And it becomes possible thereby to prepare relatively independent plans respectively for individual political units of the several categories, Federal, State, County, and Municipal, by means of a gradual building-up process; a process using the 

National and/or Regional Plans as skeleton framework, state plans as reinforcement, and community plans as the flesh wherewith the rounded-out body is molded. Each successive plan must include the features of the plan next above it and must be drawn in consideration of the conditions and requirements of all the component parts of the area planned. 

A comprehensive state plan so constructed and so inter-related with national, regional, and local requirements and potentialities becomes, as is true of the national plan, the most important and the primarily essential criterion for the determination of the relative merits and needfulness of individual state public-works projects. Such State plans will show existing improvements and facilities side by side with proposals for extended services and will indicate the part to be played by each proposed project or extension of project in supplementing existing facilities. The plan likewise should indicate population-growth trends and probabilities and the adaptability of 68

individual projects to future requirements occasioned by these trends and probabilities,

As suggested in earlier chapters, state boundaries have seldom. been drawn on logical lines. Furthermore, with social and economic hori zons broadened by improved transportation, state areas once relatively independent in development requirements, are no longer self-sufficient and, in the solution of many problems, must be regarded merely as parts of a larger region. With respect to such problems, therefore, isolated state plans do not in themselves afford sufficient criteria for well-considered judgment. In these cases resort must be had to the national and to regional plans; regional plans in this sense probably to be the result of cooperative planning between states or by groups of states. 

It is a sad fact that comprehensive state plans are virtually nonexistant. At this writing, however, due to stimulation by the present 

National Planning Board, some forty States have taken initial steps toward the preparation of state plans and several states are reported as having made considerable advance with planning studies. In a few instances special effort is being made toward the coordination of the planning efforts of states comprising natural regions. 

Standards of Balance as Criteria in the Programming of State Public Works 

Chapter III includes general discussion of the use of standards of balance as one guide for determination of the relative need and timeliness of individual projects. However useful such standards of balance may be in programming state public works, it goes without saying that such standards cannot be uniform for all parts of the United States in view of such sectional variation in requirements as is imposed by differences in climatic conditions, in intensity of occupation and development, in general terrain, and in relation to such great natural features as oceans, lakes, great rivers, mountains, and desert.

There are two principal measures of balance; service and cost. 

A standard of balance measured by service rendered is likely to be more uniform over larger areas and under more varied conditions than is a standard of balance measured by money spent. Certain services and facilities cost more per unit in some localities than in others because of variation in ease with which services may be supplied. In some states capital expenditures must be spread over a wider variety of improvements than in others. While, as suggested above, relative service requirements may be more uniform than unit cost, these too are attended by considerable regional variation. Minimum highway requirements, for instance, in sparsely populated states may be disproportionate to recreational or institutional demands as measured by the experience of more populous areas. 

Likewise, under certain conditions the importance of such things as irrigation and flood control may outweigh the requirements of institutional care or need for more highways, again, as measured by the findings of regions with different basic characteristics.

The State of Missouri offers illustration of the extent to which state construction and state service can be thrown out of balance by 69

over-emphasis upon a single class of public works. 

An analysis of the fiscal problem in Missouri by the National Industrial Conference Board dated 1930 indicates that from and including 1923, to and including 1930, the State of Missouri expended approximately one hundred and seventy millions (170,000,000) of dollars on highways and less than nine millions (9,000,000) of dollars for all other capital purposes, a ratio of about twenty to one. And further that for every dollar expended in 1928 for capital additions or improvements in buildings used for educational, penal, eleemo synary, general government, and other purposes, the State expended approximately fifty-six dollars ($56.) on capital outlays for highways. The average ratio for all fortyeight states between capital expenditures for highways and for other state services as indicated by the accompanying Table Two is 79.18 to 20.82 and for the District of which Missouri is a part, 86.47 to 13.53.

Some state services may properly be regarded as of more basic necessity than others and less subject to variation in balance ratio until extended beyond primary requirements to a stage of elaboration. Basic capacity of penal institutions, for instance, must be in direct proportion to the mumber of criminals to be housed. Hospitals and insane asylums must have sufficient capacity to care for all patients not privately or otherwise provided for. It is virtually essential that there be all-weather surface highway connections between all populous centers and some form of highway accessibility to all populated areas. The above are basic necessities to the state, as are water, sanitation, accessibility, and fire protection, necessities to the city. The extent of failure to meet these primary fundamental services is a measure of positive and primary deficiency. And any moneys that may be available for one or another of these purposes should not be scrutinized too closely from the point of view of any arbitrary standard of balance. It is when refinements of basic services are to be considered in competition with one another and with such other fundamental improvements as parks, extension of educational facilities, and flood control and irrigation, that careful attention becomes especially important to prevent the development of one or more facilities out of scale with others of similar importance. 

Proportionate expenditures over a period of past years for the various services rendered by the State have, necessarily considerable sig- Their nificance in the formulation of any arbitrary standard of balance. mean, for the forty-eight states and even more truly for states with comparable environmental conditions, represents in large measure the choice of the public during the current period of years. From a broader and more calculated social and economic point of view, the public choice so indicated may seem derived from a distorted sense of values. But mass enlightenment in social values must come through the slow process of education. Parks, forests, and good houses may offer proportionately greater real return than more miles of concrete highway; well qualified and well paid teachers may mean more to education than more monumental school buildings; but the public falls easily, by superficial judgment or by persuasion, into extreme of highway and school building construction. In the long run, money spent on preventives such as recreational facilities and sanitary and pleasant living environment may offer far greater values than money spent on correctives, hospitals and jails, but, momentarily at least, the public may not see it so. 70

Any practicable standard of balance must take these distortions of values into consideration as inherent obstacles to a more intelligent use of public funds. Lacking any scientific basis for absolute determination of the relative values of public services it may possibly be that, at this time it is impracticable to go farther with standards of balance than to base them upon the average of proportionate expenditures for the several classes of public works, as established by the practices of states, reserving the conviction without power of demonstration that disproportionate amounts are being devoted to one purpose or another.

and other local conditions have been instrumental in bringing about larger outlays for state parks and state forest than have been made in other districts.

There is a small general increase in proportion of capital outlays going for highway purposes from the period 1915-1919 to the period 1922-1931 but no noticeable trends one way or the other for the other classes of state public works. Standards either of quantity or quality of services, in relation to population, wealth, or other yardstick are not easily arrived at, whether for the Nation, a state, or municipality. This is true especially of those services which are rendered jointly by jurisdictions of two or more categories. A principal difficulty in formulation of state service standards results from variations in the practices of states with respect to division of public works responsibility. There is, for instanco, considerable variation among the states as to the extent of highway construction delegated to counties. And some states assume major responsibility for eleemosynary institutions, while other states leave much of this function to counties and municipalities.

Perhaps the most feasible approach to formulation of service stand ards is by some such means as suggested in Chapter III. But, to be closely applicable, standards based upon the accomplishment of individual states should be given the benefit of close study for discovery of and allowance for whatever special conditions may prevail in these states. For reasonably dependable standards, much closer scrutiny of individual state practices will need to be given than has been possible within the time limits of this study. However, although, admittedly, information at hand is not sufficient to warrant positive, suggestion as to desirable or practicable standards of service as based upon observed needs and accomplishments, there may be justification in drawing general conclusions from such data as are available, with the understanding that these conclusions are tentative and intended only as indication of method and of probability. 

Standards of Service 

Standards of service, presumably, are more directly applicable as measuring sticks of adequacy and more definitely determinable than standards of balance: Standards of service are useful in the degree to which they have been derived by the careful evaluation of service rendered per unit of service facility, weighed against demonstrable need. Again, few factors in the measurement of services are subject to scientific determination. Most of them are dependent upon established custom and judgment. As suggested earlier in this chapter, it is especially difficult to determine standards of service upon a state basis for state services which are supplemented by federal and/or municipal services. Perhaps, of the several services rendered by states, only roads are susceptible to this approach and even for roads there is such a wide variation in conditions and in serviceability of roads per mile by reason of differences in location, in planning, and in functions, that any arbitrary standard can be useful only as a general indicator of the relative advance of individual states in road building. 76

Accompanying Tables Three and Four and Five show, by states and by districts, the several measures of road service; percentage of roads improved; miles of improved highways per thousand registered motor vehicles; miles of improved highways per thousand population; miles of im proved highways per million dollars assessed valuation; miles of improved highways per square mile of land area; and average annual capital outlays per motor vehicle and per thousand dollars of assessed valuation. Aver age annual outlays per motor vehicle and per thousand dollars valuation, as shown in Table Three, are for all roads and streets, rural and urban. 

It will be observed that there is greater apparent regional variation in road service than in proportionate expenditures as shown by Table Two and that Districts IV and V appear to be especially delinquent in high-type-surface roadways. Districts IV and V, however, are comprised largely of states having either low rainfall or mild winter climate or both; under which climatic conditions low-type or even gravel surfaced roads are relatively adequate.

Greatest variation, among states, appears to be in average annual outlays per thousand dollars of total assessed valuation. Range in District 

I is from $2.36 to $15.32; in District II, from $4.52 to $22.48; in District 

III, from $1.13 to $56.89; in District IV, from $3.92 to $30.04; in District 

V, from $4.85 to $12.77; and in District VI, from $4.85 to $12.03.

In setting up service standards, as suggested in Chapter III, it is well to be guided by averages but not controlled by them. While standards, based upon averages, may offer a considerable goal to the more deficient states, they are recognizedly considerably below "desirability" and, usually, far below individual accomplishments. Since "standards" are too often regarded as "maximums", low standards are almost certain to act as retardants to the more progressive states. Because of the influence of peculiar local circumstances it is equally unsound, per se, to estab lish standards upon the basis of the highest of accomplishment. In the matter of reasonable capital cutlay per thousand dollars assessed valuation, for instance, it probably would be unwise to establish as goal the average outlays of the States of Delaware and Florida, where peculiar circumstances are known to have prevailed during the years of observation. 

It is probable, for this item, that such states as Pennsylvania, Illinois, North Carolina, Missouri, Oregon, and California offer better guide although for some of these states there must be allowance for the variable of greater or lesser proportionate wealth, due to relative population density and industrial development. No single factor should control. Each should be given its proper weight among the others. As suggested above, peculiar local circumstances are likely to affect the proper weighting of all factors of measurement. It has been impossible, within the limits of this study, to observe local conditions and unwise, therefore, to attempt to establish standards, upon the basis of data at hand, more definitely than is suggested roughly by Tables Three and Four. 77
