Such state-wide services as those of education and recreation are discussed in Chapter VI, under municipal public works. Other state services, as remarked below, are not especially susceptible to standardization. They are more directly controlled by circumstances. No very definite limitations, for instance can be placed upon the desirability of expansion of state park and state forest holdings. It can be said, perhaps, that if one state spends a considerable amount of money on such services then there is no reason why other states shouldn't do so, in proportion to wealth, population, and opportunity. But it is quite impractical to fix any definite goal of so many acres of state forest or state park per thousand of population or per million dollars of assessable wealth. 

Applied Criteria

When applied to the programming of public works, "standards of balance" should provide general indication of those classes of public works most neglected and requiring greatest emphasis as well as of those whose rate of advancement may well be reduced in the broader interests of the general public. "Standards of service" will indicate the extent to which individual classes of service have attained reasonable expectancy as measured by the accomplishments of other states, in proportion to demonstrated need and wealth. Both standards of balance and standards of service deal principally with totals. There still remains the problem of selection and programming of individual projects within the several classes of public works.

Criteria to be applied in the selection and programming of individual state public-works projects in many instances are not greatly different in principle from those applicable to federal projects. Particularly is this true of highways. Except for the superimposed criteria of national plan and national policy, however, the state problem is somewhat simpler, if for no other reason than that the area and its development problems and limitations are more readily comprehensible. The State Highways.- As suggested above, much of the discussion in 

Chapter IV pertaining to criteria for the selection and programming of national highways is almost equally applicable to state highways. chief difference is that of the relative importance of highways concerned. 

But, under the present practice of assembling such of a federal highway system as exists by selection of routes from state highway systems, much even of this difference is submerged. 

The public clamor of the last fifteen or twenty years for good roads to serve the almost miraculously increasing number of automobiles has served in most states, if not in all, to develop highway construction and highway department administration as a dominating activity, with a strength of organized support almost certain to throw highway construction out of balance with other services. There is every evidence that this has already occurred in many states. A further outgrowth of this departmental domination, in instances observed and probably of frequent occurrence, is over-emphasis upon the strictly engineering factors in highway location and construction, with comparative disregard for many of the other more general but equally important elements of state development. This is 84

sometimes reflected in comparative elaboration and excessive cost of individual highway routes but more frequently, in general disregard of future growth probabilities and of the long-time effect of individual highways upon adjacent areas and neighboring localities. 

A number of states have prepared so-called comprehensive state highway plans. A general criticism of these plans might be that they are too narrow in objective and based too much upon back-sight and current needs as indicated by such factors as current traffic volume, and too little upon broad foresight. In no observed instance are these plans comprehensive in the sense of including consideration for all state development requirements. For the most part, they are what might be termed point-to-point highway plans designed to move vehicular traffic from one place to another with maximum directness and speed, with a minimum of regard for other equally important factors including coordination with other forms of transportation, influence upon and harmony with population and industrial trends, coordination with parkways and service of prospective recreational areas; and preservation of amenities. 

In states observed, and presumably in many others, engineering considerations in locating state highways and in programming their construction are considerably dominated by politics, brought about through legislative designation of individual highway routes.. In one State observed a three-hundred-million-dollar ($300,000,000.) highway-construction program was comprised of approximately fifty per cent legislated projects; projects which were imposed upon the plan and program recommended by the State Highway Engineers and the State Highway Department ›f this State, through specific legislation. Legislative designation and programming of state highway projects are not conducive to economy, efficiency, or a high degree of effectiveness in state highway building. 

The danger and faultiness of this method was recognized by the Federal Government in its Act of 1926, confining congressional direction of postoffice distribution and construction to definition of principles and policies and to making lump-sum appropriations for construction. In

In many parts of the country the question of volume of traffic justifying one type of construction or another and a greater or lesser expenditure is complicated by the phenomenon of holiday peak traffic. 

Highways connecting summer resorts or other points of very specialized interest are subjected to abnormal traffic loads demanding high traffic capacity for possibly not more than eight or ten days service a year. many such instances, high-capacity and high-cost highways built to serve these peak loads are relatively deserted during the remaining three hundred and fifty odd days. From the point of view of general justification, as measured by the capacity of this country to provide an almost unlimited quantity of public works, such roads may be entirely justified. 

In specific instances, however, the needfulness of their construction should be evaluated in relation to other more general-purpose highway deficiencies.

Again, for further criteria applicable to the selection and programming of highways the reader is referred to Chapter IV. 85

State Parks.- The requirements in selection and programming of national and state parks are by no means as closely parallel as those of national and state highways. While state parks should serve the function of preserving areas of exceptional natural interest or scenic value, they are intended primarily to serve the all-year-round outdoor recreational needs of the people of the state and locality. They should be readily ac cessible to populous areas and should be as well. distributed geographically as availability of suitable areas and as general conditions will permit. 

Economic justification of state parks cannot well be measured in dollars and cents. Their benefits, while real enough, are largely intangible. Somewhere in the scheme of things they have dollar value but this value is too elusive for the bookkeeper. On the other hand, the economic justification of one park area as compared with another frequently can be rather closely determined upon the basis of the following factors: compar ative natural adaptability to park purposes of areas under consideration and comparative cost of their improvement; comparative proximity and accessibility of prospective users; and acquisition cost as compared with the cost of other areas of approximately identical merit. 

State parks generally should be chosen and developed upon the basis of providing for the more general outdoor recreational needs of the state, such as camping, picnicking, hiking, and fishing, leaving the parks and playgrounds needed for the more intensive and everyday uses of organized play and for the enjoyment of intensively landscaped areas to development by the counties and municipalities.

Too frequently state parks have been acquired by expediency rather than by plan and by considered judgment. Much state-park acreage has been made available to states through donation and has been accepted not always with sufficient thought to adaptability to state-park requirements. Park funds are frequently dissipated thereby without proportionate benefit. 

Whether to be acquired by purchase or by gift, prospective state-park lands should undergo equal scrutiny as to fitness, to insure utility commensurate to cost of development and maintenance. 

State Forests.- The Report upon a national plan for American forestry issued by the Federal Forest Service in 1933 indicates that present state forest holdings total in excess of sixteen million (16,000,000) acres. 

In its plan for distribution of ownership and management of the national forest reserves, the Forest Service suggests a total allocation to states of approximately one hundred and seven million (107,000,000) acres. 

By far the larger proportion of present state forest lands is found in the eastern states, presumably for several principal reasons, among which are: the policy of the Federal Government to select its forest lands largely from the public domain, thus turning acquisition automatically to western public domain states and leaving the eastern states without exten sive federal provision; the greater wealth and the greater forest need of the eastern states; and the higher frequency of problem areas in the eastern states growing out of heavily cut-over lands and farm abandonment. 86

Criteria for the selection of state forest areas are not appreciably different from the criteria set forth in Chapter IV in discussion of national forests. The state, through its local agricultural and forestry departments has the advantage, or may have the advantage, of more intimate knowledge of local forest and agricultural conditions and requirements including closer designation of submarginal agricultural areas, local require ments of forest management and protection, and the relative use adaptability of all lands of the state. Local state administrations are likewise in a better position to judge the administrative savings and the social advantages to be gained by converting to state forest use those sparsely populated areas requiring school and highway service out of proportion to revenue producing power of these areas. 

The latter consideration comprises perhaps one of the most impor tant criteria in allocating land to state forests. The cost of servicing the shrinking population of submarginal agricultural areas is frequently considerably greater than that of public purchase and maintenance. In this connection the Report upon a National plan for American forestry has this to say: "A study in one state showed that in number of school districts the tax receipts for school purposes from sources other than the local farms were sufficient, if capitalized, to purchase all the farms in the district, and in some districts they would leave a balance of two thousand dollars per farm. Another state is paying to some of its townships out of the state treasury annually sums for schools alone equal to twenty per cent of the assessed value of the townships. This constitutes the net payments above the total of the town share of state taxes. Of the school cost of nearly three thousand dollars in one year in a town of still another state, the local residents paid only two hundred and thirty-six dollars."

Not only might such lands be acquired and maintained as state forests with less drain upon the state treasury than if left in private ownership, but such action would be of distinct social advantage to the population affected through providing more profitable employment in the state forests or through removal to more favorable environment.

State forests offer the same supplementary advantages as do national forests, including provision for recreation, the preservation of natural scenery and wild life, the prevention of excessive erosion, flood control, and the protection of public water supply sources. There is the same blanket criterion applicable to the theoretical limits of forestland acquisition and development, that limit being fixed only by the extent of land suitable for forest use and not needed for other and higher purposes. Care in total of investment in state forests need be given only to keep this service in reasonable balance with all other state requirements. The most important general guide for the selection of specific state-forest areas is the state land-use plan prepared in consideration of all pertinent factors including inherent productivity of the land, its relative adaptability to uses other than forestry, and probable requirements for the higher uses of urban development. 87

Eleemo synary Institutions. As suggested above, and for many states at least, primary responsibility for care of tubércular dependents and of the insane and other defectives falls upon the state. The programming of capital improvements incidental to these services involves two principal factors for consideration. The first is the proper extent of public responsibility, and the other is suitable location for individual institutions of the several types. 

Extent of public responsibility is determined by public opinion and increasing recognition of this responsibility is an attribute of presentday society. Demand upon eleemosynary institutions for the above purposes has been increasing rapidly in this country for decades, due both to this increasing recognition of responsibility and to an apparent increase in the proportionate number of mental defectives.

Basic capacity requirements are to be determined through study and analysis of the incidence and the trends of incidence of the types of infirmity customarily given public care and attention. Beyond supplying capacity requirements, however, there is the question of degree of elaboration of buildings and grounds, to be determined by judgment and with consideration for proper balance in public expenditures.

For the most part, individual state institutions of this character, while needing to be reasonably accessible to population centers, may well be placed in outlying areas with primary attention to sanitation, to attractive surroundings, and to a minimum of interference with or by other types of development and outside activities. Except for those institutions involving the incidental operation of farms and requiring good agricultural land, most suitable sites will probably be found in low-grade or submarginal agricultural areas. In many instances it should be possible to relate certain types of institution sites to such other public reservations as state forests and the larger state parks.

In the two or three states coming under the close observation of the author in this connection the above principles as to location appear generally to have been well observed. This is less true, however, of supplementary county institutions, partly at least, because of less freedom in the geographical selection of sites because of more closely circumscribed areas.

Miscellaneous State Public Works.- The miscellaneous responsi bilities of states including state aid to or state development of irrigation, prevention of beach erosion, flood control, national guard facilities, etc., are so various among the states both as to extent and character and as to peculiarity of local problems as not to be very susceptible to general discussion. In many instances these projects represent secondary responsibilities. Their merits must be individually determined, as must also the relative justification of their cost. In most instances the only dependable criterion for determination of the relative need and justification of these projects is again the comprehensive state plan and the long-range capitalimprovement budget based on comprehensive plan. 88

Development and Conservation of Natural Resources. Except for the states' participation in forest conservation and development, and except for the states' responsibility for the protection of agricultural land and for guidance in its use, the primary responsibility for control of the development of natural resources is, or probably should be, that of the Federal Government. Certainly, policies with respect to natural resources of national importance should be nationally determined. Some phases of administration of these policies might presumably remain with, or be delegated to state governments, but only to the extent that the national plan and policy are not endangered thereby. 89

CHAPTER VI CRITERIA AND STANDARDS FOR LOCAL AND MUNICIPAL PUBLIC WORKS The 

Municipal public works and responsibilities are perhaps more comprehendible than Federal or State activities, certainly so for most people. 

They are more directly and more intimately connected with everyday living. 

They can, so to speak, be seen with the naked eye. Adequacy and fitness to purpose of many municipal or urban public works, nevertheless, either individually or in the aggregate, are not subject to exact determination. maximum of needful public service, as suggested in Chapter III, is quite dependent upon current public opinion as motivated by or limited by knowledge or ignorance of possibilities, by available wealth, and by social conscience. To establish service standards or to render judgment as to justification of urban public works upon the basis of present-day accomplishment, even of the most advanced cities, is merely to make the best of present-day traditions and practices, albeit heavily weighed down by impoverished imagination. No city in the United States, and probably no city in the world, affords a living environment for all or even for the average of its citizens approaching known possibilities. The construction and improvement of cities has lagged far behind the general advance of the arts and sciences. This may be merely another way of saying that the arts and sciences of mechanics have far outstripped the art and science of community life. 

Objectively, so far as ill-adaptability of urban development to present-day living conditions is concerned, and in view of the real wealth and available energy of this country, the tearing down and rebuilding of the major parts of most American cities is not entirely beyond reason and justification. Certainly the objective of raising the average standard of living up to that point permissible, by reason of available natural resources, and possible, by reason of present advancement of the engineering sciences, will not be attained otherwise. Such wholesale reconstruction of the cities of the United States is theoretically both socially desirable and economically possible. Its realization, however, is dependent upon tremendous strides in national organization, upon much evolution of public opinion, and upon vast changes in prevailing principles of land economics and of taxation. For the purposes of this discussion such perhaps Utopian suggestion may have no special value except to indicate the dynamic character of any limitations which may now be placed upon either the quantity or quality of so-called needful public works. Shifting and uncertain, however, as arbitrary limitations upon prospective public services (represented by "standards") may be, they afford almost the only basis for estimation of near future probability. "Possibility" is an approachable goal, with many intervening hurdles. 

Standards of service and standards of balance are useful as crude measuring sticks for determining the relative advance of cities and the relative adequacy. of one type of service or another; -more useful perhaps when applied to municipal public works than to Federal and State projects. But, such standards, after all, are merely direction indicators. They do not represent the ultimate goal or fix the ultimate limit of public services or of possibilities in improvement of living environment. 90

From the point

Municipalities are the catch-all of public works. of view of national operation they are the "moppers-up" of public service, performing the more detailed and more intimate service functions essential to everyday living and rounding out services begun on a broader scale by the Federal Government and by States. The range of municipal public works is almost too generally well-known to require enumeration. It includes streets, parks and parkways, playgrounds, water, sewage and waste removal and disposal, schools, protection of life and property, and many special improvements and services including port development, airports, flood control, provision of public gathering places, libraries, and art museums, and in frequent instances, the operation of public utilities including gas and electric service and rapid transit and street railways. 

There are certain basic facilities which must be supplied to every city in proportion to its population. These include an adequate supply of potable water, the removal of sewage and waste, accessibility to places of work and of residence, and some degree of fire protection and policing. 

These things are to the city what a roof, clothing, and food are to the individual citizen. The primary criterion in determining basic justification of these public works and services respectively is the simple one of their absence or availability. But adequacy, design, and elaboration of the physical facilities intended to provide these more basic services are variable, with relative fitness dependent upon respective adaptability to city plan and to present and probable future requirements as largely to be determined by city plan. This is especially true of streets, which are the media of accessibility. Economy and efficiency in provision of other such basic services as water supply, sewage disposal, fire protection, and policing, are in considerable degree dependent upon street arrangement and street design. It is impossible, therefore, to enter upon discussion of standards of service or of other criteria to be applied in determining the relative merits and proper timing of municipal public-works projects without first considering the general effects of good and bad city planning upon the ex-, tent and quality of service rendered per dollar expended, The subject of good and bad planning and of present-day requirements as to form of city structure is too involved and too extensive for detailed treatment here. It has been adequately treated in other available writings. The planned development of cities of less than a hundred thousand population, with principles generally applicable to cities of all sizes, is discussed at considerable length in "Planning for the Small American City", written by the author and published by the Public Administration Service, Chicago, Illinois. It is sufficient, perhaps, to say here that effective service and quality of living environment provided per dollar expended may vary almost infinitely according to character of street arrangement and general city plan. It is not uncommon to find a variation of seventy-five or a hundred per cent or more in street costs for essentially the same degree of adequacy and convenience of accessibility. In most instances the higher the unit cost of accessibility, the less satisfactory is the living environment of the city or neighborhood concerned. Excessive cost of accessibility is usually due to inefficient and bad street arrangement and design. Inefficiencies in street arrangement and street design produce like inefficiencies and like excessive cost for many other services, especially 91

water service and sewage disposal, with cost for actual services rendered sometimes increased twenty to forty percent or more by bad planning. 

New development, naturally, offers greatest opportunity for realization of the efficiences and of the improvements in living environment discussed above. Only extensive reconstruction will confer these benefits upon already developed urban areas. There is, however, the great middle ground of extensive land areas platted but not yet actually improved or occupied, where the criterion of better planning can be applied effectively by throwing away the old plats and starting over again. In the aggregate there are hundreds of thousands of acres of such inefficiently and badly platted land lying unoccupied and without considerable improvement, within the close environs of American cities. There is considerable question as to whether any public works should be allocated to these low-standard and embryonic developments except after re-subdivision in accordance with present-day neighborhood requirements and with a view to ironing out all possible inefficiencies and waste.

The primary criterion for the selection and programming of municipal public works, therefore, as with Federal and State public works, is the comprehensive plan. But the comprehensive plan for a municipal area must itself be subjected to certain criteria. These criteria include conformity to the national policy; adjustment to national, state, and regional plan projects; and, in addition, adjustment to the less definite criteria of growth probabilities. Growth probabilities can be determined to some extent by national policy and through the findings of rational and state plans, particularly with respect to the potentialities of land utilizations. For assurance of long life and continued appropriateness of a specific civic design, however, definite limitation must be placed upon the directions of growth and upon the ultimate size of cities. Such limitation is not now in sight. Cities continue to be regarded as dynamic organisms with direction and extent of growth limited only by more or less uncontrolled and uncontrollable circumstances. In every present-day city there are germs of "elephantiasis" ready to flourish without public hindrance, should conditions permit. Until there is change of policy as to the placing of more or less arbitrary limits upon the size of cities, the criteria of growth potentialities and requirements must be largely those growing out of guess work and speculation based upon available facts and observable trends. 

Direction and probable character of urban expansion can be determined more closely than can rate of growth or ultimate population. City plans, therefore, must be based upon an assumed direction of growth and must remain subject to constant adjustment, especially in the central areas and with respect to arterial facilities of access, in accordance with changing conditions and in accordance with constantly varying rates of growth. 

The comprehensive city plan should be formulated upon the basis of certain more or less definite principles and arbitrary standards as to dis tribution, area, and arrangement, of the several functional and component parts of the present-day city. The mapped city plan is two-dimensional and is restricted to designation of the general use of land-areas. The twodimensional plan, however, is not an end in itself, but the means to servicing 92

the uses of land. Determination of many of these uses, such as those of business, residence, and industry, are delegated to private initiative under a varying degree of public control as to distribution, height and bulk of buildings, and as to provision of certain open spaces. 

Areas set aside for private occupation by zoning, for one type of use or another, may be based on rather general standards as to area requirements in proportion to population, but such standards are highly variable for different communities under different conditions. Certain classes of public works are relatively susceptible to standardization, so far as designation of areas in the city plan are concerned. Such works include the proportion of space to be devoted respectively to streets, parks and playgrounds, schools and school grounds. 

In addition to proportionate space requirements, plan standards can likewise be applied to the distribution or spacing of such facilities as schools, fire houses, and neighborhood parks and playgrounds. Spacing and distribu tion requirements for these facilities are only in slight degree, if at all, dependent upon community wealth or density of population. Proportionate area to be devoted to these uses, however, does vary considerably with prospective density of population, but again does not vary with the wealth of the community. Extent and character of improvements placed upon these public properties are the features most responsive to limitations upon public funds. This distinction, however, does not apply in the same degree to public areas which must be acquired by purchase, after development has taken place, in the process of replanning of old cities.

There have been many efforts to state or to fix area and distribution standards for the improvements listed above. Some authorities have approached standardization of park and playground areas from the point of view of proportionate acreage and have stated that ten percent of an urban area should be devoted to park and playground purposes. Others have rated park and playground standards in proportion to population, and these standards have been placed all the way from one acre to every fifty, to one acre for every two hundred of population. Majority opinion seems to be in favor of about one acre of park and playground for every one hundred people. 

Attempts have been made likewise to establish standards for the size of school grounds upon the basis of so many square feet per child, and upon the basis of arbitrary size of grounds respectively for elementary schools and for junior and senior high schools. On the basis of the square footage of playground-space per child, suggested standards have ranged around one hundred square feet per child in elementary schools, two hundred square feet per pupil in junior high schools and two hundred and fifty square feet per pupil in senior high schools. Upon the acreage basis, standards range around from five to eight acres for elementary schools and up to twenty to twenty-five acres or more for senior high schools. Suggested standards as to distribution of schools and school playgrounds are fairly consistent in requiring spacing at intervals of about one mile, with individual projects serving a radius of approximately one-half mile. The same spacing is probably applicable to neighborhood parks and playgrounds. Primary schools and small children's playgrounds are suggested to be placed at closer inter vals. Junior high schools and senior high schools and the larger city parks, generally, are conceded to depend more upon individual accessibility and suitability than upon uniformity of spacing. So far as street space is 93

concerned, standards are more commonly applied to right of way and paved widths as related to street function than to the proportion of gross area devoted to street space. Proportion of gross area given over to streets in the average land subdivision varies from twenty to forty percent. Proportion of street space properly varies according to the intended use of the land subdivided; certain business and commercial uses requiring proportionately greater street space than do residential areas. Standards for street paving are perhaps the most definite of public-works standards and the most easily arrived at, assuming the availability of comprehensive plan. However even paving-width standards have undergone considerable change within the past fifteen or twenty years, due largely to developments in the size and speed of motor vehicles. Street-paving standards are commonly derived from multiples of individual traffic lanes with widths calculated respectively to serve the requirements of the different classes of traffic. Some years ago, standard traffic-lane widths were placed uniformly at eight feet. 

Later, moving-traffic-lane widths were increased to nine feet, and, within recent years, traffic-lane widths for heavy traffic and for rapidly moving traffic have been increased to ten feet, and some suggestions have been made toward standard widths of eleven and twelve feet. Most commonly accepted standards, however, at the present time remain at eight feet for parked vehicles, nine feet for slow-moving or average city traffic, and ten feet for heavy commercial and high-speed-traffic movement. Standard paving widths are comprised of multiples of such traffic lanes in proportion to the functional requirements of the street concerned as determined by functional classification shown on the comprehensive city plan. 

Such standardization as has been undertaken for library service has been based upon number of books per capita. Standards for public water supply are based upon quantity, upon pressure, and upon potability including both taste and purity. Proper quantity usually involves: sufficient supply to serve daily per capita needs, which may range from fifty to a hundred and fifty gallons or more per capita; immediate reserve of possibly one day's supply; and an available reserve to carry through probable periods of drought, Pressure standards are usually based upon the requirements of both fire fighting and domestic use in carrying water to the tops of all buildings except those using supplementary pressure systems. 

Standards for sewage disposal beyond the efficient transportation and removal of sewage without nuisance are difficult if not impossible of definition. There are standards in sewer pipes and in other construction materials as in connection with all public construction projects, but these are beyond the scope of this discussion. Standards as to sewage disposal, stated in terms of degree of sewage treatment, are usually of greater importance to other communities within the same water-shed than to the city immediately concerned and are subject rather to determination by some superior jurisdiction, such as a sanitation district or the State than by the individual municipality.

The other miscellaneous municipal public works, including airports, fire houses, police stations, jails, hospitals, art museums, port development, etc., are even less readily subject to satisfactory standardization. They should be supplied in accordance with peculiar local 94

requirements, in location as indicated by comprehensive plan, and at cost proportionate to municipal expenditures for other public needs and services. 

Except possibly those for street-paving widths, the several more or less definite standards suggested above are the product of judgment often without very much basis of scientific fact. In some instances, judgment is probably more or less tinged by known local limitations. 

The sug gested standard of square-footage of playground per child, for instance, was devised more specifically for application in one or more of the largest cities of the country. Undoubtedly it is somewhat the product of expediency and aimed at local probability rather than uniform desirability. In almost all respects, except possibly street paving and sometimes water supply and sewage disposal, cities generally have fallen so far below known possibilities of service and environment that all efforts to formulate standards tend to assume something of compromise from the very start. 

Standards of service are full of variables and difficulties in application. Assuming a standard of one acre of park land for every hundred people, it is conceivable that this standard can be met literally by an individual city without providing any considerable degree of park service to any large proportion of citizens. Parks may be badly located; they may be badly improved. Mere acreage without good distribution and proper development may contribute little to effective service. Dollars spent on park acquisition and improvement are not necessarily a measure of service rendered; 

It follows, therefore, that service standards, measured by area, by distribution, or by capital expenditures, either in proportion to population or community wealth or gross area of community, represent a very crude measuring stick effective only when applied with knowledge of local conditions and with judgment.

It goes without saying, however, that the value of service standards is in direct proportion to the dependability of their derivation. As suggested in Chapter III, standards of service, to be effective in improving service in substandard communities without acting as deterrents to more advanced cities, probably should be based upon the demonstrated accomplishment of the more advanced municipalities, plus a reasonable surplus as allowance for current social advance and community progress. The formulation of such standards, if to be any more reliable than standards already available and as cited above, should be derived through close study of a considerable number of cities reputed to be offering exceptionally high-standard services of one kind or another. Such study should be directed toward discovering in individual instances what is implied by so-called "high-standard" services; toward discovering what these services mean to, and how they are looked upon, by citizens of varying responsibility and in the several walks of life in the communities concerned; toward discovering what, if any, peculiar conditions have been attached to rendering individual services; and toward ascertaining per capita cost of these services in proportion to per capita wealth. 

It is probable, as suggested above, that, by carrying such an analysis much farther than has been possible within the time limits of this study, interesting and valuable conclusions might be reached as to extent of improvement in public services reasonably within reach of the average American city. 95

Certain general indications are ascertainable with respect to both standards of service and proper balance in public expenditures from available statistics of cities including capital expenditures of municipalities over a period of years and physical extent of service facilities. Several tables relating to such statistics of cities are included below. 

Proportionate Capital Expenditures of Municipalities for the Several Classes of Public Works 

Tables Seven and Eight show the proportionate capital outlays of municipalities by States and by districts for the years 1921-1933 as indicated by municipal bond sales. A variable proportion of capital improvements of individual municipalities are paid for out of current revenue. It is assumed, however, that the proportion of funds so derived is likely to be sufficiently uniform for whole States, certainly so for whole groups of States, not materially to vary proportions based alone upon bond sales. It will be observed by the above tables that there is great variation in the proportionate outlays for the several municipal services, not only between the several groups of States, but between neighboring States presumably faced with similar conditions affecting municipal development. There appears to be some explanation for some of the regional or district variations, but none for others. For instance, it is obvious that by reason of climatic conditions the southwestern States are compelled to spend proportionately greater amounts for water than are the areas of greater rainfall. For the same reason these States are required to devote proportionately less of capital outlay to road and street improvement. It is understandable that the 

Rocky Mountain and Northwestern States (District V) with their smaller cities, scattered through an area of high natural recreational quality, should spend less for parks than do the municipalities of the North Central States (District II), but difference in environment scarcely accounts for eight times greater expenditure in the latter group of Staves, nor does there seem to be sufficient environmental explanation for the fact that the municipalities of this same group of States spend, on an average, twice as much as do the New England and Northeastern States (District I). It is no less difficult to explain, on the grounds of respective need, the fact that the Far-Western States, (Districts V and VI) devote proportionate amounts to Education almost twice that of Districts II and III. Some of District III States are relative ly poorer than many States of the other districts but comparison made is upon the basis of proportionate capital outlays and not upon the basis of expenditures per capita. There is, however, sufficient of regional differences in the requirements of municipalities to suggest that any attempt toward formulation of standards of balance in municipal capital outlays should be upon a regional or State basis. With the field of application of balance standards narrowed even to individual States, data available are not very encouraging.

Table Nine gives more detailed proportionate capital outlays for the several classes of public works for municipalities of the States of California and Massachusetts, including the proportionate outlays of a number of selected cities for periods of fourteen years and twelve years respective- Ely. of Tables Seven and Eight because of somewhat different grouping of outlays, but the two listings are close enough to show that these particular states are widely out of line, in most items, with their respective di-stricts. Data for individual cities within these states are still more various in proportionate outlays, in outlays per capita, and in outlays per $1000 assessed valuation. In California, proportionate outlays of individual cities vary as follows: Ceneral Government, 1.23 per cent to 7.32 per cent; Protection to Person and Property, 1.62 per cent to 9.55 per cent; Sanitation and Conservation of Health, 3.68 per cent to 12.87 per cent; Streets and Highways, 9.95 per cent to 65.13 per cent; Recreation, 3.23 per cent to 14.87 per cent; Waterworks, 17.01 per cent to 74.09 per cent.

In Massachusetts the range between two selected cities is as follows: General Government, .22 per cent to 1.83 per cent; Protection of Persons and Property, 2.06 per cent to 2.72 per cent; Health and Sanitation, 12.27 per cent to 38.42 per cent; Highways, 27.33 per cent to 47.78 per cent; Recreation, .93 per cent to 4.85 per cent; Water, 28.09 to 30.30 per cent; and Charities, .25 per cent to 2.60 per cent. There is closely corresponding variation between towns under and towns over 5,000 population. 

Whatever may be the local explanation of such wide differences in outlays among neighboring cities of comparable progressiveness, it seems obvious that any standard of balance based solely or in large part upon such superficial evidence as that of the actual outlays of cities can have little of value for direct application. Averages may have some use as general guides but, as indicated by Tables Seven, Eight, and Nine, are comprised of elements too various to serve as a well-founded mean of desirability, Possibly, close study of circumstances of a large number of representative cities would produce more applicable standards of balance but it is probable that standards of service offer more of promise. 

Capital Outlays of uni cipalities and Services Rendered Street Paving:- Table Ten shows percentage of streets paved and miles of street paving per capita for all cities of the United States, by districts. Table Eleven gives the same facts for ten selected cities. As to be expected, the states in areas of severe winter and high rainfall, generally, show a higher percentage of streets paved. The opposite is true with respect to miles paved per capita, largely explainable perhaps by average lower density of population in the southern and western states. 

With two exceptions, the percentage of streets paved in the ten selected cities is greater than the percentage paved for their respective districts, the average excess in the selected cities being approximately 17 per cent. With one exception, miles paved per 1000 population is greater for the selected cities than for their respective districts. Excess over the average ranges from 8 per cent to 65 per cent and, for the nine states showing excess, averages approximately 35 per cent. 102

As suggested elsewhere, a desirable standard of street service cannot very well stop short of paving, at least of some all-weather character, for all streets having a fair percentage of occupied frontage. A standard based upon actual performance, therefore, can be but a step toward the ultimate goal. Such standards are really less "standards" than they are "examples" of what certain cities have accomplished, to be used as stimulation to less advanced cities. Pursuing this thought, three goals successively superimposed upon the other might well be established for street paving: the attainment of the average for the district by the least progressive cities; matching the accomplishment of the most advanced cities by average cities; and attainment of complete street improvement by these cities now most advanced. 

Parks. Tables Twelve and Thirteen show the extent of park acreage and of capital outlays for municipalities of the United States, by states and by groups of states or districts. Table Fourteen gives the same data for twelve selected cities.

In some of the western states, especially, cities have acquired considerable park acreage outside of their corporate limits, usually mountain park reservations. These parks often partake of the character of state or county parks and, in most instances, serve a quite different function than do internal parks and playgrounds and have, therefore, been omitted from these tabulations directed toward analysis of municipal park and playground service.

No district has attained the general standard of one acre for every 100 of population, suggested earlier in this chapter. Such a standard is approached only by the western Districts IV and V. Five of the 48 states show, for reporting cities, an average of park acreage considerably over the above standard but all five are western states of relatively sparse population. Nineteen states show an average park acreage for reporting cities in excess of one acre for every 200 of population. But only one of these states is in District I and only four in District II, together comprising most of the more densely populated areas of the country. The average for District I, New England and the Northeastern 

States, for reporting cities is one acre for every 325 people with variation among the states ranging from one acre of park to every 133 people in Connecticut, to one acre for every 742 people in New Jersey. 

Eight of the 12 cities reported in Table Fourteen have park acreage in excess of their respective districts ranging from 65 to 234 people less per acre of park land than for the districts. Two of these cities, as widely separated as Springfield, Massachusetts and San Diego, California, have more than one acre of park land per hundred population and nine of the twelve have park land in excess of one acre per 200 population.

Following the earlier suggestion that standards, if not to act as deterrents, should be based upon the higher rather than upon the average of accomplishments, it seems reasonable, at least until more positive 105

Education (School Plants).- Table Fifteen shows the value of school properties and capital outlays for school purposes, by states, per capita of total population and per capita of children of school age, and per thousand assessed valuation.

Standards of education cannot well be measured solely by value of school plant. Capital outlays for school purposes are merely indicators of relative stress placed upon education by various communities. The more important factors such as the ability and effectiveness of teaching staffs can scarcely be reduced to figures and tables, certainly not upon the basis of available information.

As might be expected, there is greater regional variation in value of school property per capita than in value in proportion to assessed value ation.

Range of value per capita, for districts is $28.6 in District III to $74.4 in District VI. Range for districts in value per $1000 assessed valuation is from $37.7 in District I to $51.9 in District VI. But, excluding District VI, there is only $7.00 variation between the other five districts and the average value of school property for the whole country, as of 1930, is $39.6. Per capita outlays for children between the ages of five and seventeen for the years 1921 to 1930 varied greatly for states within districts. Range in District I was from $3.5 to $20.8; in District 

II, from $3.3 to $18.1; in District III, from $2.1 to $14.3; in District

IV, from $3.4 to $14.2; in District V, from $7.4 to $14.5; and in District 

VI, from $10.6 to $30.3. There is much greater uniformity among states in value of school property per capita of population of all ages and in value of school property per $1,000 assessed valuation. This is due undoubtedly, in considerable degree to the leveling effect of time. The figures for per capita outlays for children of school age cover a comparatively short period of ten years leaving out of consideration outlays upon plant made prior to 1921. 

General conclusions to be drawn from these tabulations are that communities have invested in schools about in proportion to their ability to pay for them, with inference that in some municipalities and in some states there is relative over-elaboration of school plants while, in many of the less wealthy communities and states, school buildings are still inadequate to need. And further, these figures offer little beyond their face value as foundation for formulating standards of school service.

treatment of sewage for all of the urban population which cannot be better or adequately served or protected by other means of sewage disposal. Ade quacy of individual plant is to be determined only by local conditions. 

Table Seventeen has value, in measuring service rendered, only in indicating that there is in all probability an extensive deficiency in sewerage facilities in this country, especially so with respect to sewage treatment plants. 

The sewage of 65.5 per cent of the urban population of this country cannot be satisfactorily and safely disposed of by dumping, raw, into the nation's rivers.

Selection and Programming of Individual Public Works Projects 

General needfulness of an individual public works project within any service class, may be indicated by the general deficiency in this class of public works as to be determined roughly by standards of service as discussed earlier in this chapter. Timeliness of a given project is to be determined in part by relative needfulness, as measured by the extent of deficiency in its class in comparison with other service deficiencies for the municipality concerned. Relative merit and timeliness of an individual project, in relation to other projects of the same class, is to be determined largely by the functional place and location of this project as indicated by the comprehensive city plan. Once again, therefore, no matter how circuitous the route nor how divers the paths to the selection and programming of public works, all efforts finally converge upon the comprehensive plan and its supporting data.

Other considerations are secondary and relatively specific and technical. Most of them should be incorporated in the comprehensive plan in the original instance. It is assumed, for instance, that the comprehensive plan will have indicated the proper spacing of neighborhood parks and playgrounds, and if developed in any degree of exactitude, will have shown the location and size of areas best adapted to these uses. Assuming general deficiency in park and playground provision and assuming that the neighborhood concerned is deficient in these facilities, there remain only two considerations in programming. The first is availability of funds for this purpose and the relative justification for use of these funds in this way in view of other municipal needs; and the second is designation of specific use for the area concerned, which is largely a matter of design and admini stration. In this latter connection, it might, for instance, be determined that a combined school, neighborhood park, and community playground offers the most effective and the most needed use for the area involved. To this extent such development of the project would presumably be more meritorious and more worthy of its cost than should the site be turned to more limited use; therefore more needful and more justified. 

The relative importance or relative justification of street-paving projects varies somewhat according to respective functions. Presumably the width of paving and strength of paving for arterial streets should be in direct proportion to traffic volume; present and prospective within anticipated life of the project. For minor residential streets, performing` 118

no function other than that of access to homes, there is no such logical distinction. All occupied residential streets should have all-weather surfaced roadways of most efficient width with elaboration of paving specifications approximately in proportion to the value of frontage affected and in proportion to the desires and financial capacity of the residents served. In most instances, paving of minor residential streets is paid for ultimately by frontage owners. The community at large, however, is concerned in the extent of paving widths of minor residential streets in whatever degree the community must ultimately assume responsibility for maintenance and repaving. In the interests of both private and public economy and in the interests of residential comfort and privacy, paving widths should not be in excess of functional requirements. This latter is a primary criterion in final determination of the merits of residential street-paving projects.

Further judgment needs to be applied to public improvements to be placed in new land-subdivision developments in advance of occupation. 

During the real-estate boom years, 1920 to 1930 especially, many municipalities undertook to finance the advance improvement of land subdivisions with anticipation of later reimbursement through special assessment upon frontage. This proved to be a highly speculative and hazardous undertaking. It might safely be said that at no time is such an expenditure or investment of municipal funds justified. The financing of private landsubdivision projects, for reasons too numerous to mention here, should be left entirely to the land developer. As suggested earlier in this chapter, the more or less extensive areas of badly platted but still unoccupied land to be found at the outskirts of most American cities should act as a red flag to municipal administrators when it comes to extending municipal public works into these areas. Liany of them should be replatted in accordance with better subdivision practice and in conformity with comprehensive plan, and seldom are public improvements in these areas to be considered justified otherwise.

As a further step in programming of public works and as a further check upon the timeliness of individual projects and groups of projects, there should be formulated for every city a capital improvement budget extended over a reasonable period, possibly from five to ten years. This capital improvement budget might desirably show the details of proposed expenditures for one year in advance; the grouping of projects and approximate respective costs annually for the succeeding five years; and a reservoir of projects of next relative importance and timeliness, for possible accomplishment during the subsequent five-year period. Such program and budget should be adjusted each year and maintained in the order suggested above: one year detailed, five-year period approximated, and a second five-year period generalized. Such a capital budget and program should be based upon and supported by: (1) The comprehensive city plan; (2) A statement of expenditures for capital improvements of the several classes over a past period of perhaps ten years; (3) A statement of service deficiencies as measured by accepted standards for the several classes of public works; (4) A financial statement indicating extent of indebtedness, current revenue, and prospective revenue; (5) Specifications and detailed 119

estimates for construction to be included in the first year's program; (6) Other pertinent data relating to specific projects or to local condi~ tions in explanation of or in support of the projected program, While changes in economic conditions may operate to speed up or retard such a program, thereby throwing the program out of gear so far as time designation of projects is concerned, sequence of projects based upon relative needfulness and merit should remain fairly constant except for current changes in service requirements.

It is assumed to be understood that capital expenditures as applied to the capital-improvement program are to include moneys derived either from current revenue or from public borrowing. It may or may not be pertinent to this discussion that, in principle and under normal economic circumstances, projects financed out of current revenue are presumably more meritorious than those involving the incurring of public debt. Borrowing capacity is better reserved for periods of depression. This consideration will be discussed further in Chapter VIII in connection with the use of public works as a factor in economic stabilization. 120

CHAPTER VII ESTIMATES OF POTENTIALLY NEEDFUL PUBLIC WORKS 

There are a number of possible approaches to estimating the magnitude of future and potentially needful public works. All of them are liable to unavoidable miscalculation. No one of them is so dependable as to permit elimination of the others. There is some advantage in approaching the problem from several directions using respective findings as some thing of check upon one another. This will be the method followed. 

In addition to satisfaction of present-day needs, an estimate of needful public works to be constructed over a future period of years must be made with consideration for rate of population growth, obsolescence and replacement, and possibilities of enlargement of the field of public works and public services.

Probable future rate of population growth in the United States is a moot question. Recognized authorities predict a rapid decrease in rate during the next few decades with the population of the United States soon to become stationary at a figure considerably less than two hundred million. 

But they are dealing with forces not yet clearly understood. It is quite possible that unforeseen factors may enter to disturb any such predictions. 

While ultimate population to be provided for in the United States, however, has bearing upon the ultimate magnitude of required public works and services, it is of little immediate concern to this discussion. The extent of public works needed by present population, with or without large increase in numbers, is so far beyond immediate probability based on past performance as not greatly to vary current estimates.

Obsolescence varies naturally with the kind of public work concerned and with the quality of construction. For the purpose of estimating replacement requirements, prospective life of respective classes of public works must be fixed more or less arbitrarily upon the basis of averages; such perhaps as twenty to thirty years for concrete highway paving, thirty years for the average house, and perhaps a hundred years for an irrigation reservoir.

There are three or four more obvious approaches to estimating the magnitude of future public works in the absence of precise and established program. They are: (1) By extension of the rate of public works for a period of years past at the same rate into a period of future years. Previous to 1900 an estimate founded thus on backsight was relatively dependable. 

The sporadic and rapid development in the public-works field of the past twenty years, however, suggests the probable existence of many powerful unknowns in the present situation, quite likely to upset seriously any estimate, especially any estimate arrived at by so simple a method. (2) By assuming optimistically that demonstrable deficiencies in public works and services, as measured by accepted standards, are probable 121

of being corrected within a reasonable period of time and by allowing the bulk of these deficiencies to serve as an estimate of future activities. 

Such an estimate is dependable to whatever extent standards established are sound and in whatever degree the estimator has succeeded in guessing the distance that the public frog will jump. (3) By estimating needful public works by the use of carefully prepared plans based upon thorough analysis of current requirements and future probabilities (closely related to and interdependent with Method (2)). (4) By the exercise of insight into reasonable possibilities for extension and expansion of public works and public services without restriction by tradition. By some, this might be called the approach of the optimistic dreamer. But, if anchored by sound judgment, such entrance into the field of possibility seems worthy of serious consideration, espe cially so in view of the developments of recent years, developments which less than a generation ago, in prospect, would themselves have been regarded as the wildest of hallucinations. 

Estimates of Future Federal Public Works 

Most available estimates of expenditures for future federal public works have been prepared independently by the several federal departments with little or no coordination and with little if any weighing of one group of projects against another to determine relative justification. Some of these departmental estimates have been based upon what are called comprehensive plans, but the plans are comprehensive only to the extent that they are presumed to cover all of the problems and possibilities of the particular class of public works under consideration. As pointed out in Chapter IV, these departmental plans have been prepared without the advantage of a defined national objective or real comprehensive national plan. Undoubtedly, if put through the sieve of such national objective and plan, these estimates of individual departments would undergo considerable change toward either reduction or increase of total expenditures. Since, however, no such sieve is available, present estimates by departments offer almost the only basis of arriving at any idea as to probable volume of federal public works for the next few years.

Federal Public Works 

The only presently available comprehensive estimate of future capital outlays by federal departments and other federal agencies is that compiled by the Federal Employment Stabilization Board, and which is comprised of individual estimates from each of the several departments and agencies extended through the year 1939. The Stabilization Board has likewise compiled the annual capital outlays of these departments and 122

agencies for a period extending back to 1920. The average annual expenditures by each of the several departments and agencies for the years 1920 to 1932 and estimated average annual expenditures for the years 1935 to 1939, as compiled by the Stabilization Board, are shown in accompanying Table 18. 

Because of the abnormal nature of expenditures during the years 1933 and - 1934, the figures for these years have been omitted. 

For the purpose of making crude estimate of probable capital outlays of the Federal Government during the next ten years, 1935 to 1945 inclusive, perhaps the most feasible method is by extending the average annual capital outlays, past and estimated future, to the ten-year period. instances estimated expenditures of the several departments and agencies are considerably greater than the expenditures of the years 1920 to 1932. The se estimates undoubtedly reflect somewhat their quality as the uncoordinated and not too well related ambitions of individual departments. Inclusion of the figures for the past outlays with those of estimated outlays for the next five years, while perhaps appearing at first to be ill-founded, will tend to add to these individualized estimates the leaven of probability, with resulting closer estimate than perhaps would be obtained by using either past or prospective expenditures as sole basis of estimate. 

Accompanying Table 18 indicates that, upon the above basis of estimate, federal expenditures for the next ten years may approximate three billions of dollars ($3,000,000,000).

Since date of supplying the prospective expenditures shown in Table 18 to the Stabilization Board (spring, 1933), the outlook of several if not of most of the federal departments has been considerably altered by changes in federal policy and/or by the speeding up or interruption of programs inci dental to the activities of the Federal Emergency Public Works Administra tion. The following changes in prospect have been noted. 

Forest Service. Since submitting its six-year program to the 

Stabilization Board in the spring of 1933, the Forest Service has recommended through its national plan for American forestry a program for land acquisition and for forest planting totalling in these two items alone an annual expenditure of approximately thirty-eight million, six hundred thousand dollars ($38,600,000) to be spent annually over a period of twenty years. 

Since this recommendation is undoubtedly conservative in view of the forestry needs and possibilities of this country, there is considerable justification in using it as basis for a revised estimate of capital outlays for forestry over the next ten-year period. This would raise the estimate as shown in 

Table 18 from approximately one hundred and thirty-one million dollars ($131,000,000) to three hundred and eighty-six million dollars ($386,000,000). 

National Park Service. Through emergency projects financed by the public works administration the National Park Service has been able to make considerable advance upon the capital improvement program, which the Service had expected would have to be extended over some future years. Nevertheless, the Park Service reports that it could spend to advantage considerably more money than was provided for in its 1933 estimate and now estimates that it 123

Office of Supervising Architect Post Offices.- The Post 

Office Committee of the Public Works Administration has reduced previously recommended expenditures for post offices from a hundred and fifty million, four hundred thousand dollars ($150,400,000) to seventy-six million, four hundred thousand dollars ($76,400,000), thereby reducing the estimate of capital outlays for the Office of the Supervising Architect as shown in Table 18 from two hundred and eight million, four hundred and thirty-one thousand dollars ($208,431,000) to one hundred and thirty-four million, four hundred and thirty-one thousand, one hundred dollars ($134,431,100). 1 War Department Corps of Engineers.- Reports and recommendations with respect to flood control and river and harbor improvements are divided into several categories. The estimated cost of carrying out the projects contained in these two categories is approximately $857,618,728. This is $135,291,738 in excess of the estimate for these purposes shown in Table 18 and would raise the total estimate of capital outlays for the War Department for the next ten-year period from $976,694,340 to $1,111,986,078.
