1 

Geological Survey. The average annual expenditures of the Geological Survey past and prospective as contained in Table 18 are approximately seventy-six thousand dollars ($76,000). 

If extended over a ten-year period, such annual expenditure would amount to something less than one million dollars. Approximately three quarters of the United States is satisfactorily mapped. Completion of a full mapping program for the United States at a cost of approximately one hundred millions of dollars has been strongly urged and recommended as an essential aid to the evolving policy of advance planning and of conservation of natural resources. It is further recommended that such a mapping program be extended over a period of approximately twenty years, presumably involving a cost of about fifty millions of dollars for the ten-year period. Because of the obvious need for this work the author feels warranted in adding the above figure to the estimated outlays of the Department of the Interior, increasing the total for that Department from $355,868,490 to approximately $400,000,000.

Upon the basis of these additions and deductions the net gain in estimate over that shown in Table 18 is approximately $394,000,000, giving a total estimate for federal public works for the next ten-year period in round numbers of $3,500,000,000. 

The above is estimate Number One for federal public works. It is based perhaps ninety percent on backsight and fails to take into 126

consideration the possibility of expansion of public works into such new fields as low-cost housing and the reconstruction and unification of railroads. Neither does this estimate take into account the possibility of expansion of established forms of public works, in accordance with evolving policies with respect to the expansion of public service with the incidental objective of reducing or stabilizing unemployment. These factors will be taken up later in this report in connection with a more liberal estimate of public-work possibilities for the country as a whole. 

It is probable that departments other than those listed above have made considerable changes in programs since submitting the figures shown in Table 18. But such changes, if any, have not been available to the author and probably would not materially affect the above estimate so far as the purposes of this study are concerned. 

Attention is further drawn to the fact that the above estimate is based almost entirely upon programs submitted by individual departments and divisions without. benefit of the process of coordination and balancing, which presumably might be brought about through comprehensive national planning and through the operation of a National Planning Board such as is recommended in 

Chapter II. 

State Public Works 

Estimate of the volume of future state public works may be approached only by estimating probable future outlays upon the basis of past expenditures with allowance for the more positive of demonstrable deficiencies. As indicated in Chapter V, past expenditures of state governments are in many instances badly out of balance with respect to proportion spent for the several essential services. Any estimate, therefore, based upon past performance should take into consideration the possibility of future re-direction of expenditures so far as individual classes of public works are concerned. Estimated totals for all state public works based upon past expenditures are likely to be more dependable inasmuch as adjustment of balance is likely to be made within a relatively constant total, a total variable with broad changes in policy, in public opinion, and in economic conditions. The se variables in total revenue available for capital expenditures are matters for speculation rather than close estimate. 

For simplification there are certain classes of public works such as roads and schools which, while extending into other jurisdictions, federal, county, and municipal, are to be most conveniently dealt with on a state basis as indicated in the following paragraphs. Inasmuch as county public works are comprised largely of roads, schools, and parks, and since county government is so various in the extent of its jurisdiction in the several states it likewise seems desirable to group county public works with those of the state for purposes of general estimate of volume. 

State and Rural Roads, Highways, and Bridges.- Over the past twenty years approximately seventy-five percent of the capital outlays of state 127

In governments has been for road building. The average annual investment in road construction and purchase of right of way for all state and rural roads in the United States during the years 1921 to 1922 has been approximately seven hundred and thirty millions of dollars ($730,000,000). some states and regions it is possible that highway building activities for the past several years have satisfied the more immediate primary highway requirements of these areas. In estimating future construction costs, however, and particularly for those intensively developed regions where presumably highway improvement has attained its highest level, it must be recognized that future work is likely to be more expensive per unit because of the greater costliness of the rights of way needed to complete existing highway systems and because of the large proportion of necessary costly widenings and corrections of the earlier and relatively poorly planned highways. Furthermore, adequacy of highway improvements is relative. Few, if any, states or regions have yet attained, or are likely to attain in the near future, the position of having too many highways or too good highway systems. States and regions may and do have too many and too good highways in proportion to other facilities provided and in proportion to other services rendered. But, as will be pointed. out later in this chapter, from an unrestricted view of need and justification and when considered abstractly in relation to this country's inherent economic capacity to make such improvements, it might be considered that the road-building job will not be finished until all necessary access roads as well as main thoroughfares are improved at least to the extent of all-weather surfacing.

It may well be, therefore, that a continuation of the roadbuilding program of the country at approximately the same rate as that of the past ten years represents a reasonable compromise with the ultimate of possibility and is as good a gauge as any of probability. Road construction, if extended into the next ten-year period at the same rate as carried on during the last ten years, will involve an expenditure of approximately $7,000,000,000. This figure is inclusive of the estimated ten-year capital outlay by the Federal Bureau of Public Roads of one billion dollars. 

Education. Public school construction in the United States, like construction of public highways, has undergone rapid acceleration during the past twenty years. Estimated value of all public school property as indicated by the records of the United States Office of Education increased approximately four hundred and sixty percent during the twenty years from 1910 to 1930. The number of pupils enrolled in public elementary and secondary schools during the same period increased approximately forty-three and a half percent. Enrollment in high schools during this same period increased much more rapidly than did enrollment in the elementary and secondary schools but not sufficiently so greatly to offset the disparagement between rate of increase of capital outlays for educational facilities and the increase of number of pupils served, as is indicated by the corollary fact that the increase of value of school property per pupil during this period was approximately three hundred percent. 128

It might perhaps be deduced from the above figures that the United States is now relatively well supplied with school buildings. This undoubtedly is true for some cities and perhaps for some regions, but as indicated by Table 15, the per capita investment in school facilities varies so widely for the several states that obviously there are still many areas with substandard provisions. Based upon the average value of school property per capita for the United States, there appears to be a deficiency of investment in school buildings of approximately five hundred million of dollars. In cities relatively well supplied with school facilities, especially in the older cities of the country, there are many obsolete buildings in use which undoubtedly should be replaced. A report of the National Conference on 

Financing of Education, issued September 10, 1933, recommends the junking of a hundred and fifty thousand one-room rural schools in favor of centralized school plants. In addition to normal rate of obsolescence of school buildings, there is the premature obsolescence resulting from population shifts from rural areas to the cities and from one part of a city or metropolitan area to another. Estimates of the cost of replacing obsolete school buildings and of carrying out such a program of centralization of schools are not available. Presumably during the course of the next ten years they might amount to as much as a billion dollars. Undoubtedly such a program would serve at the same time to correct in some measure the general deficiency indicated above.

A large proportion of outlays for school buildings during the past twenty years has been for public high schools. This perhaps is especially true for the last decade during which there has been proportionately greater increase in high school enrollment. Child labor laws, increased income per family, increased popularity of higher education, have all contributed to increase of high-school attendance. This increase has been further accentuated during the past three years by reduced opportunity for employment for children of high-school age. A further factor for consideration in estimating the volume of future school construction is the decreasing birth rate. 

The effect of this decrease is already becoming evident in elementary school enrollment but has not yet materially affected the high-school grades. 

As evidence of the several trends mentioned above, the Report of the President's Research Committee on Social Trends, published in 1933, indicates that during the year 1930 to 1931, while enrollment in public high schools increased 324,815, enrollment in public elementary schools decreased 68,675. It is obvious, therefore, that barring an unexpected rapid upturn of the birth rate in the United States, the rate of new construction to take care of additional child population during the next ten years is likely to be much less than that of the past twenty years. Primary new construction requirements will be those of satisfying the needs listed above and included within the $1,000,000,000 figure.

By far the greatest deficiency in school equipment would appear to be in school-play-ground provisions. This is especially true for the older schools in the older cities. According to report of the U. S. Bureau of 

Education published in 1929, at that time 20 percent of the elementary schools in cities of from 30,000 to 100,000 population had no playgrounds whatsoever and only about 50 percent of the high schools had playgrounds or 129

athletic fields. Cost of adequate school playgrounds, while relatively insignificant in new communities and usually so in connection with the building of new schools having freedom in location, becomes a considerable item for the older schools in built-up areas, because of high land costs. 

In many instances the provision of adequate school playgrounds now in the more intensively developed urban areas will be regarded as prohibitive, and such playgrounds as are afforded in these instances are more likely to be developed in connection with city park systems. However, much of expansion of school playgrounds is entirely feasible. There are no estimates available as to probable cost of such a program of expansion.. For purposes of general estimate, the figure may be placed at $350,000,000. So extensive a program of school playground provision, however, embodies a large degree of optimism as to the growth of recognition of the importance of this factor in child education and child welfare. An estimate of possibly $100,000,000 is likely much closer to probability. 

Total crude estimate of outlays for school and school playgrounds purposes for the next ten years, therefore, as arrived at above, amounts to approximately $1,100,000,000. Average annual capital outlays for school purposes since 1920 have been approximately $250,000,000. 

State Parks and State Forests. The average annual capital outlay for state park purposes for all the forty-eight states, for the years 1923 to 1930, was about $4,800,000. Average figures relating to state park expenditures, however, and applying to the entire United States, are not especially significant because the state-park idea is relatively new and has as yet extended in appreciable degree to only a few states, only 12 states having state park areas in excess of 10,000 acres. 

Among the more advanced of the states in state-park provisions are New York State, California and Massachusetts, with average annual capital outlay for the above period respectively of about $3,000,000, $79,000, and $300,000. 

Impelled by increasing leisure time, by the increasing mobility of people gained from the automobile, and by growing recognition of the value of outdoor recreation, the state-park idea is definitely on a steep upgrade. 

It is not at all impossible that the next ten years will see a quadrupling of the amount of capital outlays for state parks made during the past ten years. Upon this basis a ten-year estimate would amount to approximately $190,000,000.

County parks also have been making rapid progress during recent years and are likely to demand proportionately greater capital outlays during the coming ten-year period. The total capital investment in county parks to date is approximately one hundred millions of dollars. 

It may not be unreasonable to assume that this investment will be at least doubled during the coming ten years, placing the estimate of tenyear probability, therefore, at approximately $100,000,000. 

Closely related to state parks from the point of view of outdoor recreation are state forests. The average annual capital outlays for state forests, for the period 1923 to 1930 was about $1,200,000. 130

Projected into the next ten years at the same rate, the amount of capital outlays would be approximately $12,000,000. 

However, as with state parks, emphasis upon state forests is likewise definitely on the upgrade. This is occasioned by all of those factors in support of increased state parks and, further, by recognition of those benefits of increased state-forest acreage including the prevention of land erosion, the protection of water-sheds, flood control and the withdrawal of submarginal agricultural lands from the lesser social and economic use of farming. As with state parks, the rate of acquisition and development of state forests is likely to be greatly accelerated during the next ten years. 

It is difficult to foresee what this rate of acceleration may be, but the national plan for forestry prepared by the Federal Forest Service may offer some suggestion. This plan recommends increase of state-owned forests from 

16,100,000 acres to 106,800,000 acres, and, for the Federal Government, an increase in acreage from 107,800,000 acres to 254,900,000 acres, with relative proportionate increase as 90 to 140. The estimated cost of carrying out the federal program, as recommended by the Forest Service and as given earlier in this report, is $386,000,000, for the ten-year period. If it may be assumed that the cost of advancing upon the recommended state program at the same rate as that suggested for federal forests would be at about the same unit cost, then the state ten-year program would involve capital outlays of approximately $250,000,000.

State Hospitals and Correctional Institutions. The average annual capital outlays of states for hospitals and correctional institutions for the years 1922 to 1930 were about $35,700,000. Requirements for these purposes are more constant than are those of most other services rendered by the state. There is, therefore, in this connection perhaps more justification in basing estimate of future outlays upon the amount of past outlays than there is for other state activities. If the past rate of capital expenditures for these purposes is extended through the next ten-year period, it will amount to about $357,000,000.

As is true of schools, however, some states and perhaps some regions have attained much higher standards of institutional service than have others. Apparently some states and regions are faced with a varying degree of positive deficiency, which, if corrected, will involve an acceleration of rate of capital outlay over a period of future years. 

It is possible, however, that slacking off of construction in those states and regions relatively well equipped will tend to balance any acceleration of construction likely to be brought about in the more backward states. 

Some suggestion of general deficiencies in state mental hospital facilities is offered by the 1930 figures of the Federal Bureau of Census as to patient population in excess of normal hospital capacity. For the country as a whole this excess amounts to about 45,000 patients. The average value of state mental hospitals in the United States per inmate varies in the several districts from about a thousand dollars to two thousand dollars. additional capacity for the 45,000 excess patient population is to be provided at the prevailing rates in the respective districts, the total capital If 131

outlay to meet this immediate deficiency will amount to approximately $75,000,000.

There appear to be no definite figures available as to deficiencies for other state institutions. This more specific figure for the mental hospitals, therefore, must be considered as being included in the total estimate given above. 1 

Other Miscellaneous State Capital Outlays. As indicated in 

Chapter VI, state responsibility extends to a number of miscellaneous projects and services involving construction and capital expenditures varying widely in extent and character among individual states. The se miscellaneous projects include administration buildings, flood control projects, beach protection, irrigation, and reclamation, a certain amount of port development, some waterway improvements, and numerous other developments peculiar to local conditions and requirements. By their very nature and in the absence of long-range state plans and capital improvement programs, it seems impossible to make any definite estimate as to future volume of this group of state projects. There appears to be no better basis of estimate than that of projection of the past rate of expenditure into the next ten-year period.

The average annual capital outlay for such miscellaneous projects for the years 1922 to 1930 was about $67,200,000. At this rate capital expenditures for these purposes over the next ten years would amount to approximately $670,000,000.

The average annual capital outlays of states for all public works for the years 1922 to 1930 have been $517,619,965, as indicated by Table Nineteen. If it is assumed that the same rate of expenditure is to be extended through the next ten-year period, the total capital outlay involved will be approximately $5,000,000,000. Modified by the adjustments suggested above, and with outlays for schools, other rural roads and county parks added, the amount would be approximately $9,667,000,000. 

It should be noted, that the preceding figures relating directly to the capital expenditures of states do not include any considerable proportion of the capital outlays for schools discussed above and include only that portion of highway expenditures disbursed by states. Neither do they include the outlays of counties and townships for road improvement, both of which items are included in the over-all figure given above for "state highways and rural roads".

County Public Works 

Capital outlays by counties are limited largely to those for road improvement, parks, schools, charitable and correctional institutions, and administrative buildings. County capital outlays, past and probable future, for roads, schools, and parks are included in the overall figures listed above under state public works. There remains specifically for counties only one considerable item, that of outlays for 132

institutional and administrative buildings, and for this there appear to be no adequate figures. Whatever estimate is made as to probable outlay for these purposes must be purely speculative. There are approximately three thousand counties in the United States. It might be reasonable to assume that counties spend at least an annual average of $25,000 on capital improvements other than those included above in the state figure. 

This would indicate a ten-year capital outlay by counties for these additional purposes of approximately $750,000,000. 

Municipal Public Works 

There appear to be no adequate data covering all capital expenditures of cities of the United States for all classes of municipal public works. Whatever figure is given as to volume of past expenditures of all cities for all capital improvement purposes must be comprised in considerable part of estimates. Perhaps the list of municipal bond sales over a period of past years as compiled by the Commercial and Financial Chronicle offers one of the best of bases for making such an estimate. 

The annual average of municipal bond sales covering all municipal capital improvement projects for the years 1921 to 1933 amounts to $814,733,000. 

Coupling with this figure $400,000,000 as estimate of annual capital outlays from current revenue, as indicated by the estimated average percentage of capital outlays made from current revenue in the years 1923 and 

1929,- approximately 33 percent.

Deducting from this figure an estimated annual average outlay for schools of $250,000,000 (schools being included in the above tabulation for states) the average annual capital outlay of municipalities for several years past, comes to about $965,000,000,- and extended at the same rate over the next ten years, would amount to about $9,650,000,000 for the period; again exclusive of outlays for schools.

There is, however, another method of approach to probable municipal expenditures, which in some respects may be more reliable. This approach is by dealing more specifically with the several individual classes of public works and their respective deficiencies. 

By far the greater proportion of municipal capital expenditures go for the following purposes: 

1.- General government, including administrative buildings and miscellaneous projects. 

2.- Protection of persons and property, including structures incidental to policing and fire protection. 

3.- Health and sanitation, including sewerage, drainage, and waste disposal, and a certain amount of hospitalization. 

4.- Education, including schools and playgrounds. 136

5.- Roads, streets, and bridges.

An estimate of expenditures for schools and school playgrounds is included in the over-all figure given above under state public works. For general government, protection of persons and property, and for charities and miscellaneous construction, no specific data have been discovered covering demonstrable deficiencies. For these items there seems no choice but to assume a continuation of need and of development approximately as those of past years. Annual average of bond sales over the years 1921 to 1933 for these purposes amounted to about $65,000,000. This group of public works is assumed to draw proportionately more from current revenue than do many other improvements. It may be safe to assume that total outlays were at least double the amount of bond sales, or, approximately $130,000,000, as annual average; with estimate for the next ten-year period, at the same rate of outlay, amounting to about $1,300,000,000.

Needed and probable expenditures for sewerage, parks and playgrounds, waterworks, and libraries can in varying degree be approached more specifically and directly.

Streets and Roads.- Table Ten indicates that for a total of 379 cities reporting in 1928, representing approximately sixty percent of the total urban population of the country, only about 38,000, of a total of approximately 80,000 miles of streets, were paved. If two thousand miles of paving progress are allowed for intervening years, then it may be assumed for purposes of this discussion that approximately fifty percent, or 40,000 miles of streets in these reporting cities are still unimproved. If it may be further assumed that the remaining urban population of the country is served by about the same street mileage per thousand population as are the reporting cities, then there are in all about 70,000 miles of unimproved city streets. 

Presumably, reported street mileage includes a considerable proportion of streets which have been platted, but which are not yet occupied by buildings and for which there is not now, and may never be, need for improvement. 

There is no means of determining the extent of such questionable street mileage. For the purposes of estimate it may be safe to deduct twenty-five percent of the total of unpaved mileage as not being immediately needful, leaving a primary street-paving deficiency of about 50,000 miles. The life of he average street pavement is considered as about twenty years. If such a rate of obsolescence is applied to the seventy-thousand miles of paved streets, then there will develop, in the course of the next ten years, need for repaving of approximately 35,000 miles, which means a prospective deficiency to be met, at least in part, during the next ten years of about 85,000 miles of street paving. 139

From point of view of need, all actually occupied and used urban roads and streets should have some kind of all-weather surfacing. The character and costliness of surfacing varies greatly with volume and character of traffic, with climate, and with street grade. This variation in quality and cost of surfacing makes any over-all estimate of necessary capital outlay difficult, even though the above suggestion as to extent of need be accepted. Cost may vary presumably by as much as from fifty cents to three dollars or more per square yard. In fixing an average unit cost it may be assumed safely that the greater proportion of unpaved streets is in those localities where, by reason of climate, street paving is less necessary and at the same time less costly. There is some justification, therefore, in suggesting what may appear to be the rather low unit cost of one dollar and a half per square yard in estimating the cost of new paving. Possibly the unit cost of repaving would be higher, but by allowing for the value of already prepared subgrade, it may be that one dollar and a half per square yard for repaving also is not out of reason, although perhaps conservative.

If twenty-six feet is assumed as the average of street width, cost of paving per mile at one dollar and a half per square yard is $22,500. 

Curbs and gutters will probably add at least another $7,500 per mile, giving a total cost of improvement per mile of $30,000. Total cost for paving and repaving of the 85,000 miles of streets estimated above at this rate would amount to about $2,500,000,000. To this might be added another $500,000,000 for bridges and other construction. 

The above represents a conservative estimate of need. In view of past performance it is not probable that all street-paving deficiencies in the United States will be corrected in so short a period as ten years. 

However, there is no allowance in the above figures for street mileage added by normal expansion of cities. Allowing for a reasonable amount of expansion and in view of the conservativeness of the estimate of deficiency, there is considerable justification in placing the estimate of a possible future ten-year outlay for street paving at something in excess of $2,000,000,000. 

Parks and Playgrounds. According to Table Twelve, the average annual per-capita capital outlay for parks as reported by cities of the United States representing 78.8% of the total urban population of the country was $.78, for the years 1926 to 1930. This figure is derived from reports made to the Department of Labor by a large proportion of those cities having a population in excess of five thousand. Because of the probability that the cities not reporting include a disproportionate number of cities poorly equipped with parks, and because the normal park expenditures of cities of less than five thousand population is less per capita than the expenditures of the larger cities, extension of the above average outlay to all cities of the country is not justified. If this average per-capita figure is reduced to $.70, it may be estimated that the total of average annual capital outlay for parks for the years 1926 to 1930 amounted to approximately $48,000,000. These were admittedly years of comparatively large public expenditures for all purposes. It is 140

believed, however, that because of the increasing need for, and the growing sentiment in favor of, parks and recreation areas, park development is likely to proceed over a considerable period of years at an accelerated rather than at a diminished rate. It is probable that primary emphasis during the immediate future will be placed upon state and county parks. No matter how extensive rural recreation areas may become, however, there will remain the old and increasing need for local municipal parks and playgrounds. 

It is believed that any estimate of expenditures for park purposes during the next ten-year period should be that of past rate of expenditure projected, in this case amounting approximately to $500,000,000, plus a certain amount calculated to correct a part of present demonstrable deficiency. 

Table Twelve likewise indicates that the average park acreage for the country as a whole is somewhat less than one acre for every 244 of urban population. There are no precise or carefully evolved standards as to needful park acreage per capita, but such standards as there are indicate that there should be at least one acre of local park and playground, exclusive of the usual school playgrounds, for every one hundred population. Upon the basis of data contained in Table Twelve it seems probable that the total urban park acreage in the United States, exclusive of municipal park holdings outside of incorporated areas, is not in excess of 275,000 acres. As measured by the general standard of one acre for every one hundred people, there is an apparent deficiency in park acreage of about 400,000 acres. This deficiency extends in unknown degree to swimming pools, stadiums and auditoriums, and other park and playground equipment. The cost of correction would include the acquisition of land and its improvement together with the construction of such park facilities as listed above. Cost of both land and improvement for park purposes is widely variable, becoming excessively high in projects for the correction of park deficiencies in the older and larger cities. the purposes of estimate, however, an average total cost of five thousand dollars per acre is assumed. At this unit cost the correction of municipal park and playground deficiencies as derived above would be in the neighborhood of $2,000,000,000. Again, full correction of so extensive a deficiency in any ten-year period is improbable. It does seem reasonable in view of present trends to increase the above $500,000,000 item, arrived at by projection of past expenditures, by another $500,000,000 for the correction of deficiencies, making a total estimate of probability for the next ten years of $1,000,000,000.

Sanitation. As indicated by Table Twenty comprised of data from the Municipal Index for 1931, as reported by cities representing 57.25% of the total urban population of the country, approximately 15%, or 10,000,000, of the urban population of the United States is not served by sewerage collection systems. More than 75% of the total urban population, or more than 

45,000,000 people, have no sewage treatment plants. 

This actual and apparent deficiency in sewage collection and disposal facilities is comprised of both insufficiently expanded existing plants and of the complete absence of facilities. Estimating the cost of correction of sewerage deficiencies is complicated by variable local conditions. Under certain conditions, sewage disposal can be accomplished satisfactorily without 141

special treatment. In some cases of low-intensity development, the use of cesspools and septic tanks may be entirely satisfactory and less expensive than community disposal plants. 

There have been two compilations of needed sewerage projects which have come to the author's attention. One has been made by the National Committee for Trade Recovery and reported in Engineering Construction Markets for August-September, 1932, published by the Engineering News Record. This survey indicates that there are in the United States 

248 municipalities, with population in excess of 1,000, having no sewage collection system and 5,995 cities of this size without sewage disposal plants. It is estimated by this Committee that the cost of these projects, both those of sewage collection systems and sewage disposal plants, would amount to approximately $2,200,000,000.

The American Waterworks Association has made a compilation of immediately needed sewerage projects as of January 20, 1934, including 1,893 projects with a total estimated cost of $464,146,670. 

In many instances the projects included in the American Waterworks Association compilation represent the more immediately needed next steps in the building or improvement of sewerage plants. It is largely a compilation of the sort of things that might be done within the next two or three years assuming availability of funds.

According to figures compiled by the Division of Economic Research and Planning of the National Recovery Administration, expenditures for sewerage systems averaged $90,000,000 for the years 1929 and 1931. 

It becomes evident that in view of the obvious deficiencies in sewage disposal plants, and in view of the capital outlays of the years 1929 and 1931, which perhaps are not too abnormal with respect to these facilities, an estimate of probable program for the next ten years should be extended considerably beyond the projects compiled by the American Waterworks Association to include a fair proportion of the needed projects indicated by the compilation of the National Committee for Trade Recovery. 

Upon the basis of these several groups of available data, and considering the extensive inadequacies of many existing sewage collection systems and disposal plants, it is quite possible that complete modernization of sewerage service for the United States would cost somewhere between $3,000,000,000 and $4,000,000,000. Projecting the average capital outlays of the years 1929 and 1931 into the next ten-year period, capital outlays would amount to about $900,000,000. Adding to this an item of possibly $300,000,000 toward an enlargement of program for the purposes of making more rapid advance upon correction of deficiencies, the estimated total for the next ten-year period amounts to $1,200,000,000. 

This class of public works in addition to sewers and sewage disposal plants includes such projects as garbage disposal plants and incinerators. The above over-all figure, however, is probably sufficient to cover these additional items. 144

It should be said further that because of the close interrelation of the adequate handling of sewage and the provision of healthful recreation areas, the impetus now being given park development is likely to have the corollary effect of stimulating the construction of sewage disposal plants. 

Many much-needed natural park areas in the close proximity of densely populated areas are rendered useless for recreation purposes by stream pollution removable only by the better handling of sewage. 

Waterworks. Practically all sizeable communities have one form or another of collective water service. The estimated average of service for each of the forty-eight states indicates a surplus of water supply facilities averaging approximately twelve percent, with surplus shown for all states except the State of Washington and ranging from three to forty percent. 

For the most part the requirements of new waterworks construction are those of urban growth and expansion and of improved potability of supply. 

In the latter category are such situations as that of the City of Philadelphia, which takes its water from the rather heavily polluted Delaware River, depending upon heavy treatment to insure potability, with the result that the city's water supply is safe but not good. The need and justification in such instances for going to large expense in obtaining water from better sources are, of course, relative.

The National Recovery Committee for Waterworks Construction of the 

American Waterworks Association reports (January 1934) definitely needed and pending waterworks projects totalling in cost $555,847,070. As is true of the compilations of this same Committee with respect to pending sewerage projects, the waterworks projects included in this estimate appear to be those most immediately needed and are probably insufficient to meet the requirements of a ten-year period.

The average of bond sales over the period 1921 to 1933 for waterworks construction was about $120,000,000. Again, it may be assumed that waterworks construction is more likely to be paid for out of current revenue than are many other public works and that for purposes of estimating total outlays the above figure may be at least doubled; bringing the average of annual outlay for past years to $240,000,000. Upon this basis estimated outlays for waterworks during the next ten-year period would amount to something in excess of $2,000,000,000.

Public Libraries.- A public-library-service survey sponsored by the American Library Association and reported at the A.L.A. Conference of 

October 1933 indicates that, in forty-two states covered, and the District of Columbia, there exists a primary deficiency in library service as follows:approximately 44 million people out of a total population of 114 million surveyed (38%) are without library service and that 1,002 counties and 242

Presum cities of population greater than 5,000 have no public libraries. ably, this deficiency would be proportionately increased if the other six states were included in the survey. 

In addition to deficiencies represented by population not served, 145

there are the deficiencies of inadequate service by existing but crowded or under-equipped libraries.

Population not served in 1926, as estimated by the above survey, was 3,415,418 urban and 47,054,586 rural. Average per capita cost of library facilities in the United States has been $2.10. Assuming the goal should be the servicing of all population, urban and rural, the cost of meeting the above primary deficiency, at this cost per capita, would amount to about $100,000,000. There are no data as to the probably cost of making desirable improvements of existing library facilities. For the purposes of this study, the cost of such improvement together with the cost of providing for population increase and of replacing obsolete buildings is placed at $50,000,000.

Comparatively little progress has been made with servicing rural areas. It is too much to hope that the next ten years will see this deficiency in great measure corrected. A liberal figure for outlays upon public libraries during the next ten years, therefore, might be $100,000,000. 

Summary. Based upon the above figures, the estimate of total municipal capital outlays, exclusive of those for school purposes and included within the state summary above, amounts to $7,600,000,000, or approximately $2,000,000,000 less than the estimate arrived at above by projecting the estimated rate of capital outlays over the years 1921 to 

1933 into the next ten-year period.

This figure, however, does not include an item for municipal housing as discussed below.

Possible New Fields of Public Works and Old Fields Expanded 

Housing. There is evidence of fast-growing recognition that housing of low-income families is and must of necessity be in considerable part a public responsibility. This point of view has come to be augmented by the idea that large-scale housing offers a means to a considerable reduction of or stabilization of unemployment.

There is as yet, however, no agreement as to where public responsibility for housing should begin and leave off; no agreement as to what comprises sub-standard housing or housing below the point of reasonable toleration by a civilized community; and therefore no available estimate of the extent or volume of the real housing job. 

There have been two general approaches in making such estimates of deficiencies as are now available. One is that of estimating the housing shortage based upon indications as to actual vacancies, and the other going more deeply into the problen to discover how many of the dwellings in use 146

are actually unfit for occupancy. The one is directed primarily toward discovery of the potential volume of future residential construction of all classes. The other is aimed more directly at determination of the extent of deficiency in those grades of housing accommodations which presumably might fall within the range of public responsibility. It is with estimates of this latter type that this study is most concerned.

The U. S. Department of Commerce is undertaking a real property inventory of 64 representative cities, widely distributed over the country. 

At this date compilations are available for 48 of these cities and the findings with respect to these 48 cities are summarized in accompanying Table Twenty-one.

Cities included in this summary represent 4.9 percent of the urban population of the United States and 8.9 percent of the total population of cities within the population class groups of the cities covered. This is an insufficient base for estimating the deficiencies of all urban areas of the United States especially in view of the fact that none of the large cities of the country, with their more extreme housing problems, have been included in the compilation. However, an estimate based upon extension of the deficiency proportions of cities surveyed to all urban population may have some interest and value.

Such extension of proportions, as shown in Table Twenty-one, indicates a total deficiency of dwelling units of 1,496,323, assuming a normal vacancy of 5 percent. By similar extension of the number of dwelling units unfit for habitation found in the cities observed, the total of unfit dwelling units for all urban places amounts to 359,256. 

These deficiencies include dwellings of all cost ranges and are calculated without consideration for "sub-standard" houses as discussed below, except those houses judged positively unfit for human habitation. 

These estimates, therefore, no matter how true for the field covered, are not of great value in estimating the magnitude of the low-cost housing problem, should the elimination of all sub-standard housing become a national objective.

Estimates of definitely sub-standard family units vary from 

1,250,000 to 10,000,000, with all estimators conceding that their conclusions are largely guess-work. Much of the difference in estimates, however, is explainable by differences in definition of sub-standard housing. Definition of one recognized authority is as follows: "Housing is sub-standard that is structurally unsafe, dilapidated, or out of repair; housing which is damp or partly underground; housing which contains windowless rooms or which has insufficient light and air because of insufficient open spaces around it; housing which lacks running water, bathtub or shower, private indoor flush toilet, sewer connection where sewers exist, or adequate means of heating in winter; housing which has neither gas nor electricity. A house is sub-standard for a given family if it is too small for that family. Where all low-rate housing tends to be of one, two, or three rooms, this fact is an important part of the general inadequancy. 147

Total in U. S.

While admittedly defective in many respects, in the absence of more specific information, there seems no better approach to estimating the extent of sub-standard housing than that of assuming that under usual conditions standard facilities as measured by the above definition are not likely to be supplied at less than a certain average rental. It is assumed that such average rental for the country as a whole might be placed at $20 per month. 

Concerning such approach one authority has this to say: "When the rental and home value figures of the 1930 Census became available, they showed that thirty-four percent of all rented non-farm homes in the United States were renting for less than twenty dollars a month. Under average American conditions I believe it is sufficient to say that rentals under twenty dollars connote sub-standard housing. In some parts of the South a fifteen-dollar dwelling may be fairly good, but in at least as many parts of the North a twenty-fivedollar one is definitely obsolete. "Of the owner-occupied non-faim homes eighteen and one tenth percent were valued under two thousand dollars, which corresponds to a twenty-dollar rental. Combining these two groups, the conclusion would be that twenty-six and six tenths percent of all non-farm homes, or something over six million of them, are definitely sub-standard." 

By this same approach and as measured by the above definition of sub-standard housing, it is estimated further that possibly as many as five million farm homes are definitely sub-standard. Public responsibility for rural housing probably is more remote than for urban housing, although the Federal Government begins to touch upon the rural housing problem in its program for the withdrawal of sub-standard agricultural lands and in its program for the development of subsistence homesteads. 

There are three principal factors in estimating the volume of housing construction over a period of years. These are: (1) The extent of present housing deficiency represented by actual housing shortage and by sub-standard accommodations; (2) Current obsolescence based possibly upon an average building life of thirty years and involving a continuous replacement of buildings; (3) Expectancy of population increase. 

Since the estimate of six million sub-standard non-farm homes indicated by the above quotation seems to have as adequate foundation as appears to be available and since, at the same time, it represents an approximate mean between the two extremes represented by estimates in hand, this will be assumed as a very approximate estimate of present urban housing deficiency represented by sub-standard dwelling units. 

Sub-standard housing conditions are not static but may be assumed to expand somewhat in proportion to the increase of population. Population of the United States increased approximately sixteen percent during the decade 149

1920 to 1930. If it may be assumed that sub-standard housing, through obsolescence of structure and through normal increase of the low-income groups, increases by no more than one percent a year for the next thirty years, a thirty-year job of eliminating all sub-standard housing would involve the building of at least eight million low-cost dwelling units. 

At the end of thirty years it is presumed that a certain amount of replacement would need to be added to the requirements or normal expansion to comprise the continued low-cost housing program. Again the above figure does not include sub-standard rural housing. It is generally conceded that with present knowledge of construction and with present construction costs, new housing above sub-standard grade cannot be produced for much if any less than two or three thousand dollars per dwelling unit. At this rate the cost of the eight million units would be $24,000,000,000 and, if undertaken for accomplishment within a logical period of thirty years, would involve an expenditure of perhaps $800,000,000 a year. Such a program and such an expenditure is recognized as not a little visionary. It does, however, represent a crude measure of the extent of the real urban low-cost housing job and of what this country might do should it become really interested in exploring the possibilities of work that needs to be done. 

It has been suggested by a competent housing authority that an immediately practicable low-cost-housing program could be built up on an annual expenditure of $100,000,000. Should the above estimate of eight million sub-standard dwellings, existing and cumulative within the thirtyyear period, be anywhere near correct, then, at this small rate of outlay, it would require something like two hundred and seventy years to rid this country of low-standard houses, assuming a static population and a moratorium upon obsolescence. It is probable that a total expenditure of $100,000,000 a year on low-cost housing would be scarcely sufficient to absorb current increase in this type of accommodations. 

A ten-year program at a hundred million a year would amount to $1,000,000,000. It is not enough and would appear to be a pessimistic figure. There seems some justification for increasing this amount to $5,000,000,000 in the general estimate of probability of public works construction for the next ten-year period. This will include the outlays for low-cost housing purposes by municipal and state as well as federal authorities. An estimate of possibility, as indicated above, might be as much as $24,000,000,000.

Railroads. Whether or not the railroads of the United States become a direct federal responsibility and classed as public works, their maintenance and improvement are of great public interest and represent extensive possibilities in the way of new construction. It is probable that the maximum of desirable improvement in railroad service, as to be made possible through new construction, may not be attained except through centralized administration possibly involving public ownership. For this reason it seems proper at least to include consideration of extent of the 150

railroad rebuilding and improvement job along with housing and other potential public works; certainly so, for the purposes of speculation in the field of possibility.

It is estimated that the total investment in railroads in the United States is in excess of $26,000,000),000. A considerable proportion of this investment is now in jeopardy because of the failure of railroads to meet the competition of highway and air transportation and because of the inefficiencies in operation, resulting from: inadequate unification of railroads; unnecessary duplication of lines; general bad planning and obsolescence of trackage and equipment, from point of view of now apparent possibilities. Some suggestion of trends and possibilities in railway. passenger transportation is offered by the experimental run of the new lightweight, high-speed Burlington train from Chicago to Denver at an average speed of a hundred and twelve miles per hour,- a run requiring such precautions in the protection of grade-crossings, switches, and other hazards, as to indicate that such speed, however mechanically possible, is not generally feasible over any extensive amount of existing railway trackage. .

It has been estimated that if the passenger speed norm in the United States were increased to approximately ninety miles an hour, the cost of straightening curves and removing other hazards would amount to at least $2,000,000,000.

There is no estimate available of the cost involvements of extensive rebuilding of the railroad system of the United States with the object of attaining that efficiency possible through complete unification of railroads and providing the comforts and speed of service now apparently mechanically possible and necessary, to meet highway and air competition. Without slight basis of estimate, it is ventured that such improvement in reconstruction together with extensive electrification of lines might amount to as much as or more than a third of the total investment in railroads, or about $8,000,000,000.

The American Engineering Council, in a report dated April 7, 1933, suggests early elimination of five thousand selected railroad grade-crossings at an estimated cost of $500,000. It has been variously estimated that elimination of all railroad grade-crossings in the United States would cost from twelve to twenty billions of dollars. This is several times the estimated amount necessary to provide adequate parks and playgrounds for all. urban areas of the United States and from a half to three quarters of that needed to eliminate all sub-standard housing in the country, as estimated above. Although elimination of grade-crossings is important, it would seem quite out of proportion to devote twenty billion or even twelve billion dollars to this purpose, in the most blue-sky of construction programs, until many other human needs are met far beyond present wildest dreams. Increase of the $8,000,000,000 railroad reconstruction figure by $2,000,000,000 for grade-crossing eliminations would seem to make proportionate allowance for this purpose and bring the total for railroad reconstruction and crossing elimination to $10,000,000,000. 151

Expansion of the Road-Building Program.- According to the records of the Federal Bureau of Public Roads, there were in 1930 in this country 125,709 miles of highways with high-type surface, 572,106 miles with low-type surface, and 2,314,956 miles of non-surfaced rural roads. 

In speculating as to the utmost possibility in road improvement it might be concluded that all needful rural roads should be given one type or another of all-weather surface. There are, however, a certain proportion of rural roads which do not and are not likely to serve any useful function and which should be vacated. Some years ago it was estimated that there were approximately 10,000 miles of such roads in the State of Pennsylvania. This is about 11% of the entire rural-road mileage of the 

State. The percentage of excess roads in the State of Pennsylvania is probably higher than for the average of the country, but such difference is likely to be more than compensated for by future progress with the withdrawal of sub-marginal agricultural lands and with the expansion of national and state forest holdings. For the purposes of estimate, therefore, it may be safe to conclude that at least twenty percent of the total unsurfaced rural-road mileage of the country has or will have insufficient function to warrant capital outlays for their improvement. 

This leaves approximately 1,750,000 miles of rural roads which might be considered worthy of more or less extensive improvement. It can be assumed safely that a very large proportion of these remaining unimproved roads are of tertiary character, to be amply improved by relatively lowcost surfacing. The State of Pennsylvania is reported to have produced a satisfactory tertiary road surface at a cost of about $5,000 per mile. 

A considerable proportion of needed highway improvement is of high type and might very well increase the average cost to $7,500 or more per mile. 

Initial cost of paving all the needful roads of the country at this rate would appear to be approximately $13,000,000,000. This might be increased to $15,000,000,000 to allow for bridge construction and other incidental improvements. It is probable, however, that under average conditions the life of such low-cost surfacing would not exceed ten years, making the liberal allowance of fifty percent for salvage of the old road bed, there would be involved in such a program, upon basis of such obsolescence, the necessity of fifty percent reconstruction every ten years, involving the total capital outlay over a period of twenty years of about $22,500,000,000. There might be added to this figure a sum of $2,500,000,000 for reconstruction of existing improved highways to meet modern requirements and for repaving of already improved roads, making a total of possibly $25,000,000,000.

Expanding of the Forest Program. - There are in this country approximately 670 million acres of forest or potential forest land. Approximately 170 million acres of this are in public ownership, federal, state, county, and municipal. It is proposed, under the national plan for forestry prepared by the Federal Park Service, to increase public holdings of forest lands by about 223 million acres, giving over to public ownership approximately 393 million acres. This is about 100 million acres less than the total estimated extent of commercial forest land in the United States. 152

The cost of carrying out the federal part of the forestry program as outlined by the forest service and extended over a twenty-year period would be approximately $772,000,000 for land acquisition and for planting. 

An estimate of the proportionate outlays of states for the same period and as suggested by the national plan for forestry would amount to about $500,000,000, or a total for federal and state outlays of about $1,272,000,000. The national plan for forestry appears to have been conditioned somewhat upon the requirements of practicality both in acreage recommended to be acquired and in the extent of areas proposed to be reforested and, further, appears not to have taken into full consideration the possibilities of retiring submarginal agricultural land. It is probable that a capital outlay of as much as $5,000,000,000 over a period of twenty years would not exhaust the potentialities of public acquisition and development of the forest areas of the United States. This figure, however, probably should be sufficient to include further development of national and state parks. 

Expansion of Waterway. Improvement Program. In addition to the waterway projects included above under federal projects probable of construction within a ten-year period, the Corps of Engineers has made surveys and studies of additional projects totalling an estimated cost something in excess of $7,000,000,000, bringing the total estimated cost of projects recommended or surveyed by this Division to date to approximately $9,200,000,000. It is impossible at this time to adjudge the relative needfulness of all the projects included in this figure, but for purposes of estimating the maximum of potential public works, a full figure of $9,000,000,000 may be included. 

Summary.- The total for these five groups of works, waterways, forests, highways, railroads, and housing, expanded in accordance with the above estimates would amount to approximately $73,000,000,000. The estimated outlays for the several other classes of public works, federal, state, and municipal, as outlined above and extended into the second ten-year period would amount approximately to $17,000,000,000. If to this figure for rather more normal procedure with public works there is added an item of $15,000,000,000 to allow for proportionate expansion of these services, then the total volume of possible public works to be carried out over a period of perhaps twenty years might be concluded as ranging between $100,000,000,000 and $110,000,000,000.

Miscellaneous Estimates 

There are two further sources of estimates of public works which, while not used extensively in this study, nevertheless, have not been disregarded. The first is the unpublished program for the stimulation of the construction industries prepared by the Division of Economic Research and Planning of the National Recovery Administration. This study is concerned largely with evolving a two-year program of public works. Its estimates of volume of potential public works are based in considerable part upon estimated deficiencies caused by the lack of construction during the past three 153

or four years. Because of the limited scope and purpose of this program, it offers little of assistance to the sort of long-range estimate herein under taken. The 'other obvious source of estimate of the volume of public works is the records of the Federal Public Works Administration, showing projects for which loan or subsidy has been requested. 

According to a letter from the Administrator of Public Works dated February 15, 1934, the total of applications granted and applications pending as of December 31, 1934 was about $6,300, 000, 000, including projects amounting to approximately half a billion dollars definitely rejected, withdrawn, or rescinded. Inasmuch as the value of total of projects allotted and pending amounts to less than three times the total annual average of public works construction for the several years prior to 1933, it is evident that this list provides little measure of the volume of potential public works for a future period of from ten to twenty years.

Both these estimates made by the Division of Economic Research and Planning of the National Industrial Recovery Administration and the projects listed with the Public Works Administration are necessarily to be considered as included in the general estimates offered above. 

There are other special projects including the plans in preparation by the Mississippi Valley Commission for development of the Mississippi Valley and plans and work being projected by the Tennessee Valley Authority, and such possibilities as the extensive redistribution of the farm population of least favorable agricultural areas, which have not been made subject to special consideration. For the most part, work incidental to such projects will be included within some one of the more general items. Such of these projects as may be excluded above probably will not be of sufficient magnitude materially to affect the totals of so general an estimate.

Summary 

From the above estimates, two summarized totals are derived, including all classes of public works under all jurisdictions, federal, state, county, and municipal. The first total is a rough estimate of the probable magnitude of capital outlays for the next ten-year period. The other is a very general estimate of the potentialities of public works under a policy of greatly expanded and considerably extended national objectives, and including works which might be extended over a period of perhaps twenty years. 

Total Number One, estimated magnitude of probability, is $26,067,000,000. This figure has been reached by comparatively slight adjustment of the rate of capital outlays over a period of recent years to the more obvious of current trends with a degree of optimism in 154

allowance for correction of most glaring deficiencies. It includes the following items: federal public works, $3,500,000,000; state public works (including schools, county parks, and all rural roads), $9,667,000,000; miscellaneous county public works (exclusive of schools, roads, and parks), $300,000,000; municipal public works (exclusive of schools), $7,600,000,000; and low-cost housing (under all jurisdictions), $500,000,000. 

In the interests of the welfare of the country as a whole, there can be little question of the justification and needfulness of spending such a total amount for public works during the next ten years. In the absence of carefully evolved comprehensive plans, and of other supporting data and analysis, however, there is no assurance that the amounts allotted to individual classes of public works, either national, state or municipal, are in proper balance from point of view of best meeting the social and economic needs of the country.

Total Number Two, estimated magnitude of possibility, amounts to $105,000,000,000. This figure has been reached by disregarding for a moment past practices and policies to view the real public service job of this country, a job which the United States has the means, if not the organization, to accomplish. Until the major portion of the work included under this estimate, and more like it, is done, there can be no justification in extensive unemployment. For estimate Number Two there is even more probability of bad balance between individual items than for estimate Number One. 

Supporting plans and data are less available in proportion to the greater remoteness of so extensive a program of public works. 155

CHAPTER VIII GUIDANCE OF PUBLIC WORKS TOWARD EFFECTIVE PUBLIC SERVICE 

There is some obvious inconsistency between the content of the preceding chapter and the emphasis placed elsewhere in this report upon the importance of advance planning. The author has repeatedly stressed the view that pre-formed objective or policy and pre-prepared comprehensive plan are essential primary bases for determination of both character and quantity of needed public works, whether they be federal, state, or municipal. Under the present circumstances of no well-defined and coordinated national policy and plan, no comprehensive state plans, and few adequate city plans, it must appear both inconsistent and not a little footless to undertake estimation of the magnitude of potential public works. Certainly it must be recognized that in the absence of well-defined plans such an undertaking is especially. subject to wise error.

Possibility of error increases rapidly in movement from the general to the specific, in movement from estimated totals for the country at large to estimated potentialities for given classes of public works in specific areas.

Estimated totals for the country at large, including all potential public works regardless of jurisdiction, are less subject to error growing out of lack of comprehensive plans than to error introduced by unforeseeable future changes in public opinion and policy with respect to the kind and extent of public works properly an obligation of government. value of an estimate so full of potential miscalculation and error is merely that of giving a general idea of actual magnitude of this consumer of wealth, labor, and materials called public works; looked upon by some so hopefully and by others so slightingly, as a possible stabilizer of employment and of the national economy.

The 

Possibility of considerable miscalculation in estimates occurs to greater or less degree for all factors employed. By far the most important variable in estimate of totals is that of evolving public opinion and public policy, unknown as to possible rate of change; unknown even as to direction. 

There is every evidence that the extent of recognized public works is on the increase, both as to number and kind of services to be rendered collectively and as to their quantity and quality. But there is no positive assurance that one sort or another of social upheaval may not bring reaction. Public service directed toward the objective of raising general living standards is one measure of the progress of civilization. History shows clearly enough how slow and tortuous may be the path of social progress. Superficial evidence of evolving civilization, including the many popular products of invention, may be misleading. The enthusiasm of envisioning a goal, physically possible and dependent only upon development of public opinion, may lead to over-optimistic calculation of near future probabilities.

Estimates based upon deficiencies, measured by the difference between empirically-derived standards of service and actual service rendered, are heir to all the inaccuracies of insufficiently-founded judgment.inherent in the formulation of such standards. As suggested in earlier chapters, 156

virtually all standards of service must be arbitrarily fixed at some level determinable only by judgment, based upon probability as evidenced by demonstrated accomplishment and the more obvious trends. All such standards of service are subject to the variable of public opinion as suggested above. 

In particular instances the literal satisfaction of standards may bear no more relation to actual service rendered than may putting in the required number of hours for hoeing a field of corn when all time and effort may be squandered on the first dozen hills. Application of standards as a measure of work to be done assumes an efficiency of effort possible only by faithful conformity to comprehensive plans. Bad planning or no planning will cer tainly increase and may multiply the amount of construction necessary to attain a desired standard of service.

If, however, inevitability of miscalculation in estimates of volume of future public works is recognized so that these estimates may not be used too literally, they may have considerable value in analyzing the potential effectiveness of public works as a tool for stabilization. Even with miscalculation of twenty-five per cent or more, some idea should be afforded as to approximate magnitude of the consuming power of this field of national endeavor.

Potentialities of Public Works as a Stabilizer of Employment and of the 

National Economy 

Before entering upon speculation as to possible employment of public works as a stabilizing tool, it may be well to offer reminder that first, foremost, and always, public works are a means to public service and to improve living standards. If incidentally public works, through cal culated programming, can be used to flatten out the curve of unemployment and of business cycles, then that represents so much social good and national gain. But there is real question as to whether such gain justifies any considerable degree of inefficiency of effort, particularly in view of the fact that such inefficiency can be reduced almost to infinity by the simple process of advance planning.

Further, the conscious employment of public works as a means for long period reduction of technological unemployment, and superimposed upon the mass of public works normally regarded as needful, is highly justified in degree to which these superimposed public works are directed toward real social betterment. Such public works and such programming do not represent inefficiencies. Neither need they be a charge upon the expedients of emergency. They embody, or should embody, so much advance into that field of needful public works complete in justification but currently regarded as immediately improbable in view of retarded public opinion. 

Estimates of volume of potential public works as contained in Chapter VII include consideration of both probabilities and possibilities. 

Estimates of probabilities are based principally upon back-sight and with only a mild infusion of optimism as to rate of evolution of public opinion. 

Estimates of possibilities open the way to advancement in the field of these so-called superimposed public works mentioned above and to real 157

1 advancement toward that standard of living of which, by real wealth and scientific knowledge, this country is capable. 

Estimates of possibilities include all those seemingly visionary projects such as the extensive rebuilding of American cities; the redistribution of population according to a pattern more conductive than that of the present to general prosperity and good living; the giving to every man of a decent home with reasonable provision of the comforts and conveniences of living; the removal of all squalor from living environment; the adequate replenishment and protection of important natural resources; the remodelling of all transportation facilities to whatever extent may be necessary for accomplishment of the above purposes; and many other conceivable efforts conducive to material improvement of the conditions of life and entirely feasible except for the single obstacle of the prevailing embryonic organization of society.

While not retreating from the stand that such extensive remodelling of structural things and such improvement of physical environment is physically possible and socially desirable, the author, nevertheless, recognizes that public opinion and the deficiencies of present-day social organization may represent as effective an obstacle to accomplishment as if there were no cement to build new roads, no bricks to build new houses, and no abundance of coal and water power to produce cheap electricity. It is recognized that in the interests of practicable discussion of the magnitude of public works as a tool for unemployment relief and for economic stabilization, it is necessary to fall back very largely upon estimates of probability. 

From the figures contained in Chapter VII, it may be assumed for purposes of discussion that normal public works, exclusive of the implements of war and exclusive of extensive departure into new fields, and reasonably to be accomplished within a period of perhaps ten years, will approximate twenty-five to thirty billion dollars, optimistically, thirty billions.

This estimate, as to be seen from Chapter VII, is based upon assumption of widespread correction of demonstrable deficiencies as measured by current accomplishment; upon allowance for additional requirements occasioned by population growth; and upon allowance for current obsolescence of improvements. To this might be added a sum of perhaps five billion dollars for government participation in low-cost housing, the probabilities of which are supported by current evidence of growing recognition of public responsibility in this field. Additional public works occasioned by more extensive advance into the field of public utilities including gas, electricity, and railroads, are not included, partly because policies are undetermined, but more because, whether or not publicly owned and operated, employment and capital expenditures in these fields are likely to remain relatively unchanged except for the possibility of the wider spread of service which might be made possible under public non-profit operation. Assuming, therefore, a thirty-five billion dollar ten-year expenditure as a crude estimate of the magnitude of the public-works tool, some idea may be formed as to its potential effectiveness in absorbing the technologically unemployed and in stabilizing cyclical unemployment. 158

For so varied a mass of public works it seems all but impossible to determine with any exactitude the proportion of this expenditure to go directly to labor. Assuming, however, for purposes of discussion that fifty per cent goes directly to labor, and assuming a six-hour day and fiveday week at an average compensation of one dollar an hour, this amount of public works will provide approximately three billion man-days of labor, or about twelve million man-years of employment. Assuming again that total labor involved may be tripled by the secondary and tertiary effects of these public expenditures, the maximum of man-years of employment provided directly and indirectly by the construction of these public works would not exceed forty or fifty million. This would absorb the total of twelve million unemployed as estimated for the worst of the recent depression years, over a period of perhaps four years. This represents less than a forty per cent increase in capital expenditures over those of the past several years. A large portion of this volume of public works may have some force as a tool for stabilization. But only the increase may be regarded as a possible factor in reduction of technological unemployment. Employment occasioned by administration and maintenance of new public works has not been included but would undoubtedly absorb a considerable amount of additional man-power. 

Obviously, public works conducted on the scale of "probability", even though infused with a considerable degree of optimism, do not in themselves offer either means to anywhere-near-complete levelling of cyclical unemployment or of any considerable abatement of what may be regarded as more permanent technological unemployment. This remains true even though it were possible completely to impound public works in a reservoir of potential energy to be released at will and as occasion may demand. And such impounding of public works, while possible in some degree, cannot and should not be made completely operative. This is especially true of those public works representing basic necessities or intended to correct marked deficiencies. It is obviously impracticable and socially undesirable to delay construction of these works, pending an anticipated depression of uncertain date. A measure of control of public-works timing, to provide a reservoir of work for emergency use, is desirable and should be possible. Such tim ing probably is most feasible for federal and federal-aid projects. These expenditures are more or less elastic and directly controllable in accordance with national policy. Control of state and municipal expenditures is much more elusive. Tendency always is to expand capital improvements during periods of prosperity. Such expansion usually is made possible by more or less extensive borrowing, readily sanctioned by a currently prosperous citizenry.

It is suggested that one feasible means of building up a reserve of municipal and state public works and a reserve of municipal and state borrowing power might be through persuasion or by compulsion placed upon states and cities to adopt, much more extensively than at present, a policy of pay-as-you-go; whereby capital improvements are paid for out of current revenue during times of prosperity and whereby borrowing capacity is reserved for periods of depression and economic emergency. How this can best be brought about is a problem for governmental economists. Such a working arrangement might presumably have many refinements, such, perhaps, as com pulsory maintenance of a surplus of funds to be drawn from current revenue 159

to meet depression-period debt charges. Such procedure would involve the raising of taxes when times are good and taxes readily payable, and reduction of taxes during difficult years. Such tactics upon the part of public administrators might not represent good political practice. They would be good business and, except by improved business methods, the use of public works as a stabilizer of unemployment seems not to offer much promise. 

A reservoir of public works comprised of those "superimposed" projects mentioned above, drawn from the realm of possibility rather than from the field of much-restricted probability, should be much more effective as a stabilizer than the kind of public works referred to in the preceding paragraph. These "superimposed" public works are needful, are essentially justified, and serve a useful public purpose. But, for the most part, they are relatively less urgent. They are not as subject to current demand as are many of the more accepted public works likely to be attended by persistent encroachment upon any reserve held in abeyance to some future and uncertain emergency. Most of the public works of this second category are most logically to be undertaken by the Federal Government or to be directly stimulated by the Federal Government and, to that further extent, they are more easily manipulated, from the point of view of both volume and timing, than are those projects falling under more diverse jurisdictions.
