Public Works as a Means of Public Service and to Improved Standard of Living 

As stressed above and repeatedly, the only real justification of public works is that of thier contribution to public service and to improved standards of living. In the aggregate, public works include service of all those functions not delegated to private enterprise, contributing to general well-being, comfort, and convenience, and for which there is material and knowledge to produce. Such. definition of public works extends far beyond the accepted use of the term and to services which always have been foregone because they have not been readily adaptable to private enterprise conducted for profit and because, from limited social point of view, they have not seemed to come within public responsibility. 

The fact that a great people such as those of the United States should continue to tolerate services and living environment so disproportionate to national real wealth and to native ability to produce is without doubt due to failure to comprehend and to order the complexities of a social organism of such magni tude. It is scarcely conceivable that a community of a thousand intelligent people, thrown completely on its own resources in a land of virtually unlimited productivity, would cease its labors toward the common good until every individual and every family had at his and its disposal all the basic necessities and comforts that the aggregate genius and wealth of the community could provide. In this imaginary town, assuming it to be equipped with scientific knowledge and raw materials comparable to those of the United States, some individuals might presumably be more richly endowed than others with material things; a few might have bigger and more elaborate houses; some might have two or three automobiles, perhaps a half 160

dozen baths, and a radio in every room; but no family would be without a good house, good sanitary facilities, and electricity. Every street would be paved. There would be a reasonable number of books per capita. There would be park and playground space in full proportion to the desires of the people. Service wires for electricity and telephone would be placed under ground. Billboards and squalor would give place to order, shrubbery, lawns, and trees. In short, everything would be done that the community knew how to do for the advancement of good living. Organization would be simple, and both method and necessity would be easily seen and comprehendible. 

It may be that the human race, as represented by the people of the United States, has not yet attained that degree of social comprehension whereby the development and service problems of millions of people spread over a wide and diverse area can be reduced to a simplicity and to an ease of handling comparable with the problems of the small self-sufficient community. In principle, expansion of full comprehension to national problems and to national development does not seem impossible. Actual problems of the larger and more complex community are much the same as for the smaller, only bigger. A man with a shovel can remove a mountain by persisting with the same methods and movements that he may use to remove a molehill. The difference in practice is that, seeing neither the top nor the other side of the mountain, he may quit on the big job before he starts. 

Earlier in this chapter, reference has been made to the possibility of expenditure of funds and effort without proportionate return in service rendered. As size and complexity of community increases, the more probable becomes such wastage and the greater becomes the necessity of full comprehension, foresight, and plan. Again, the man with a shovel may attack either side of his molehill and throw the earth in almost any direction that may meet his fancy without greatly reducing the effectiveness of his work. In attacking the mountain without plan for disposal of earth removed he may succeed only in digging a hole, to build another mountain. 

Comprehensive planning for the Nation, for a region, a state, or a city, is a necessary part of the process of reducing complex problems to simple and comprehendible terms. The comprehensive plan is both a compass and a gyroscope pointing direction and maintaining balance. 161

CHAPTER IX BRIEFED FINDINGS AND SUGGESTIONS 

Planning is looking forward. It is a process peculiar to the human race but one relatively undeveloped in application to community or collective enterprise.

Plans may be expressed in blueprints or in terms of guiding principles. Plans designed to guide the development of dynamic organisms such as a city, a State, or a Nation, over a long period of years must of necessity take chiefly the form of policies and guiding principles with a minimum of dependence upon blueprints. Because most people are familiar with the blueprint type of plan, the general public does not readily grasp the idea of community, State or national planning. 

The term "planning" has caught the popular imagination during recent years more rapidly than has grown understanding of its proper meaning and application. Countless specialized groups talk of planning, having in mind quite different processes and things. Economists, sociologists, agriculturists, and engineers talk of national planning, each in his own language. There

City, State, or national planning cannot be so pigeonholed. can be no such thing as physical planning in isolation from social and economic planning. This is true in proportion to. the social and economic complexity of the thing planned. It is a condition more intensified for a 

Nation than for a city, as it is less true of an individual land development than for an entire community. 

Physical planning, the planning that deals with land, water, structures, and all the other material objects that comprise physical environment, is but the tangible or visual expression of social and economic desirability and necessity. Physical planning without social and economic planning is gainless. The effectiveness of social and economic planning, in turn, is largely to be measured by their effect upon living environment as expressed in physical things. 

The term "planning", therefore, as used throughout this report, even when applied to a specific physical thing, assumes all pertinent social and economic considerations.

Effective planning for the United States involves the weaving of a full network of municipal, county, and State plans bent upon the master pattern of a national plan. Sub-standard conditions resulting from failure to fill in any part of this network must inevitably result in proportionate structure of the national pattern and proportionate liability to be borne by the Nation as a whole. Planning is not a panacea, It will not prevent all 162

mistakes. It offers no miraculous foresight into requirements and eventualities. It is a guiding process necessarily subject to constant adaptation to new and unforeseen conditions and requirements. Planning may take the form of regimentation or of absolute control over initiative of the individual, but this is neither an essential quality of planning nor is it a likely expression of planning in the United States. Planning may be directed toward quite the contrary objective, absolute freedom of the individual. Somewhere between the two extremes probably lies the greater social and economic good and the greater likelihood. 

A Plan for a Physical National Plan 

There has been much talk during recent years about national planning. Many countries, notably Japan, Italy, Germany, and Russia, have entered upon the preparation of so-called national plans. In most, if not in all, instances these national plans have been limited in scope and in comprehensiveness by narrowed objectives. It may still be said that no nation has as yet completely assembled that jigsaw puzzle which is the national plan. National planning is still a pioneering venture. 

Planning is not new to the Federal Government of the United States. Planning of one sort or another has been in process periodically since the inception of the country. The difference between national planning that has preceded and the kind of national planning which of necessity is now forced upon this country is largely a difference of method and ob jectives.

Increasingly, during more recent years there has been an extensive federal departmental planning. The sum total of planning effort made by the several federal departments is probably much more than sufficient to have prepared the most thorough of national plans. Failure to produce a comprehensive national plan has been due to a general lack of coordination between the departments and due to the lack of a common objective and a common body of policy.

There are those who doubt the practicability of long-range planning. For these there is but one answer: the alternative of planning is no planning, and of the results of no planning there is sufficient evidence. 

A national plan extending into a long future period is unavoidably more or less of a guess, but a guess based upon a wealth of well-considered information as to existing conditions and trends, and a guess representing presumably the maximum degree of foresight humanly possible. Objective

A primary objective of planning in the United States should be the conservation and proper utilization and development of natural rem To this end a first step in national planning should be a thorough 163

stock-taking of national wealth.

Planning for the conservation and for the use and development of the natural resources of the United States might well be directed toward some such objective as follows: To provide and to insure a minimum standard of living upon as high a plane as the wealth and natural resources of this country will permit, and, with this purpose in view, to discover the true potentialities of this country and its several parts and to direct subsequent growth and development in such a manner as to make full social and economic use of these potentialities, with due regard for the conservation and replenishment of all natural resources. 

Studies to be included in planning toward such an objective will include those of: 

1. Land use potentiality for regions and for localities, 2. Character and rate of dissipation of natural resources, their appropriate use, and their replenishment and conservation. 

3. Social and economic trends. 

4. Population and industrial trends, 5. 

Governmental trends. 6.

General service and living standards in the several component parts of the country, include: 

The primary elements of the mapped physical national plan would 

1. Transportation facilities 

2. Recreation facilities, 

highway, rail, air and water. 

3, Power and power distribution, 4. Land use classification including provisions for carrying out an established policy of conservation, National Planning studies and the preparation of a national plan might most effectively be accomplished through some such planning and coordinating agency as a National Planning Board. 164

This National Planning Board should be set up as advisory to the President and to Congress. Preferably, its members should have overlapping terms to insure continuity of personnel. The National Planning Board should function as a coordinating and policy-making body, initiating surveys and planning studies to be carried forward either by its own staff or by the staffs of the several federal departments, with coordinating functions extended to the planning and development enterprises of states and other civil divisions so far as these concern the national welfare. And, finally, the Board should serve as a sieve through which the plans and projects initiated by individual departments may be drawn for evaluation and for correlation with respect to national policy and basic national plans. 

Criteria for the Selection and Programming of Public Works 

A plan is the product of applied criteria. The plan itself comprises the most important criterion in the selection and programming of public works.

A primary group of criteria governing both plan and project planning is that embodying needfulness. Needfulness of an individual project or of a class of projects is relative. It is to be measured only by arbitrary standards which may be ranged progressively higher or lower according to prevailing economic conditions and prevailing public opinion. 

Such standards may be those of service or those of balance of services. Standards of service have to do with the extent of facilities required to perform a given purpose. Standards of balance offer means of measuring the extent to which one or another kind of service facility is developed disproportionately or is tending to consume a disproportionate share of public funds.

Both standards of service and standards of balance are subject to so many local variables as to be incapable of reduction to universally ap plicable and specific terms. They are valuable only as general indicators of conditions and of direction.

As suggested above, few services have any positive top limit as measured by needfulness. Standards with respect to most services are but stepping stones toward a goal of perfection. In order that they may not act as deterrents to communities relatively high in the scale of accomplishment, standards are desirably to be set either at the peak of demonstrated accomplishment or somewhat above it. 

A second group of criteria includes those of economic justifica tion economic justification as based upon: first, public opinion as to the proper extent of collective enterprise and expenditures; and; second, upon the proportionate magnitude of real wealth. The former effectively controls the use of real wealth in providing public services and finances but does not alter its essential potentialities. 165

The real wealth of the United States including land, building materials, and all forms of energy, is sufficient to provide virtually all the services, comforts and conveniences readily conceivable. The fact that these things and services have not been provided is due in considerable part to confusion of thought as to what represents cost. Real cost for a comparatively closed economic system such as that of the United States is to be measured not by the internal exchange of the symbols of wealth, but by the extent of exhaustion of irreplaceable resources and by such unfavorable balance of international exchange as may result from any given program of construction. With intelligent use, the real wealth of the United States is sufficient to produce for all its people an extremely high standard of public service and living environment. Failure to accomplish such an objec tive is one of organization, not of physical means of accomplishment. 

The criteria of economic justification; therefore, are largely as determined by current public opinion as to how much of a collective service shall be provided by the public purse. In application to individual projects, economic justification is measurable principally by the standards of balance suggested above. 

The application of both standards of balance and of service is especially complicated for those services which are rendered jointly by the Federal Government and/or by the states and municipalities. The complexity of criteria for the selection and programming of public works increases with advance from a state of scarcity of needed services to one of relative plenty. 

The complexity of essential criteria also varies greatly with the different kinds of public works with basic need and desirable extent much more obvious for some than for others.

Lack of balance in public expenditures and services results largely from uncoordinated or cubicular planning and programming of public works whereby applied criteria become deceivingly simplified and dangerously reduced. Around each of the several classes of public works so planned and programmed in isolation there tends to develop a group of more or less single-minded supporters who will almost certainly carry out of bounds the special class of public works concerned. The Federal Government is especially prone to cubicular planning and programming. 

The Federal Government is also especially subject to what might be termed pork-barrel balancing of expenditures. Pork-barrel balance in the sense of distribution of federal funds with view to equal or proportionate benefit of the several sections of the country from which these funds are derived is justified. Evils arise mostly from attempts to provide distribution in kind.

In summary, there are the following principal criteria or groups of criteria applicable to public works in the United States in degree varying considerably in applicability according to the kind of public work and the type of jurisdication under which it falls: 166

1. The criterion of balance, including considerations of proper proportion between expenditures, within a limited budget, for the several kinds of public works and based primarily upon balance and proportion in the several services rendered. 

2. The criterion of service standards whereby deficiencies are measured by arbitrary standards of service established at or somewhere near the peak of actual accomplishment, plus reasonable probability. 

3. The criterion of essential services, applicable to such basic needs as those of water supply, sewage disposal, and fire protection, with the chief determinant simply that of whether physical conditions and degree of population concentration make these services essential to community wellbeing. 

4. The criterion of cost, including the following factors: amount of total available income (dependent upon community wealth, upon public opinion, and upon public view as to what represents the real cost of public improvements); funds available for a given class of public works as determined by balance; and value to be received and benefit to be derived from a given project at a given cost, in consideration of all other needed improvements of the same class. 

5. The criteria of relative need and relative benefit of individual projects in relation to and in consideration of all other needed improvements as determinable by coordinated and comprehensive plan. These criteria are the determinants of sequence of projects. 

6. The criteria of trends and of growth and development potentialities, by which the extent and character of future required improvements and services may be measured. Such trends and potentialities in turn are deter minable by exhaustive survey and analysis of conditions and trends and by comprehensive long-range planning. 

7. The criterion of social and economic desirability, which for practical application must be based largely upon such arbitrary standards as may be established under Item Two abovo. (Many conceivable public works are quite without economic utility or social desirability, as determinable by applied common sense. The economic and social desirability of expansion of accepted and useful services is relative and in the last analysis limited only by public opinion and by limits set upon public expenditure by public opinion). 

8. The criterion of emergency, with application varied according to whether projects involved are those for the replacement of necessary public works destroyed by fire, flood, or other catastrophe, or whether these public works represent speeded-up execution of normal expansion or development of improvements and services. (The latter can be foreseen; the former cannot. In both instances the criteria listed above should be applied to whatever extent may be practicable in the same manner as under normal procedure. In the first instance the criteria of balance and cost may need 167

to give way to necessity. In the case of simple speeding-up of a program as relief for unemployment and economic depression, the only justification for failure in the orderly application of criteria listed above is that of lack of adequate plans prepared in advance. But for lack of advance plans, in view of the experience of recent years, there is no justification. Among the criteria of emergency is the criterion of proportion of dollars spent, going directly to labor. Such a basis of selection of projects becomes less important and probably fades into insignificance when advance upon public works is nation-wide, and when the force of secondary and tertiary circulation effectually equalizes distribution of the public works dollar, whether spent directly for labor, for raw or fabricated materials, or even perhaps for land.)

Criteria in the Selection and Programming of Federal Public Works 

на The Federal Government includes ten departments and twenty or more independent offices, all of which, with some exceptions, have some degree of direct construction responsibility. In the case of the independent offices, construction responsibility frequently is limited to a single project bridge, a monument, or a hospital. Some of the departments, on the other hand, are responsible for a great range and variety of construction and development projects and operate both in construction and in administration through numerous departmental divisions or bureaus. The planning procedure followed and criteria applied by the several major federal departments and divisions are discussed in detail in Chapter IV. 

In general, it may be said with respect to federal public works that their relative merit and justification are dependent in determination upon several underlying factors, the principal among which are: 1. 

National policy with respect to general national development and with respect to the kind of public work or to the type of national resource concerned; 

2. The relation of the specific project to parallel or supplement ary services provided locally under other than federal jurisdiction; 

3. The relation of the project concerned to other parts and other phases of the physical national plan. 

There follows the justification of individual projects upon the bases of the more specific criteria relating to the fitness or adaptability of individual projects to intended purposes. These latter and perhaps secondary criteria are more directly concerned with specific location, design, and cost of projects. Without the primary bases of judgment, selection of projects must be by means of applying secondary criteria only, with all questions of broader relationship left to unfounded guess-work. In very large degree all federal public works and most state and municipal public works have been limited in their determination to judgment founded on these secondary criteria. This is not to say that all or even a majority of public works 168

constructed have been unsound, either in extent, location, cost, or intended purpose, but that the usual procedure in programming and selection of public works is not conducive to economy and balance in public expenditures nor to rendering the maximum of service per unit cost. 

A great many improvements are obvious as to justification and solution of attending problems. In many instances, availability of national policy and comprehensive plan would suggest alteration only in degree if at all. That many serious errors have occurred by reason of lack of coordination and plan and defined objective, however, is without question. 

Furthermore, at the present stage of development of this country, with its social and economic complexities and with its extensive demands for and requirements of service, the proportion of development problems with obvious solution is much less than it was a hundred, or even twenty or ten years ago.

There can be no further assurance of a reasonably rational national development except upon the basis of thorough-going coordination through comprehensive plan.

It is implied above that there is a considerable relationship between criteria to be applied in the selection of federal and of local public works. This is true especially with respect to those services and those facilities responsibility for which is divided between federal, state, and local governments, and, particularly, for such things as highways, parks, public forests, and certain correctional, educational, and eleemosynary institutions. These citeria are principally those relating to the total and relative quantity and quality of service rendered. These interrelated citeria can be applied effectively only through completion of the plan pattern down through the scale from national to local plan. 

Criteria and Standards for State Public Works 

While many state public works are supplementary to or supplemented by facilities and services of like categories supplied by the Federal Gov ernment and/or by counties and municipalities, there remains a sufficient allocation of public works responsibility to states to permit of the preparation and effective operation of state plans. 

A comprehensive state plan provides, in itself, the principal criterion for the selection and programming of state public works. Most state boundaries, however, are illogically drawn and many state plans to serve effectively must be coordinated with plans of adjoining states to form what might be called sectional or regional plans. Both state and regional plans in turn should be coordinated through and by the national plan. 

A number of state plans are now in the process of formulation but, to date, no comprehensive state plans have been available as criteria for public works programming.

Standards for State Public Works 

Effort has been made as indicated in Chapter V to arrive at a 169

standard of balance based in part upon the actual expenditures of the sever al states over a period of past years. Because of sectional differences in climatic and other environmental conditions, the states have been divided for this purpose into six groups. Expenditures and proportion of expenditures for the several principal classes of state public works have been tabulated for all states arranged in groups as suggested above. These facts as tabulated are so variable as to discourage hope of arriving at any dependable standard of balance, either for the Nation. as a whole or for groups of states, by reliance solely upon these figures. As they stand, they offer only the most general of guides and can be brought closer to actual desirability only by much more exhaustive study of conditions in individual states, including analysis of all peculiar local circumstances and of actual services and facilities provided under the local ratio of expenditures. The above tabulations, however, do offer evidence of the more marked deficiencies and of proportionate over-elaboration of certain classes of public works for individual states. 

Consideration is given likewise to standards of service, and several tabulations are offered showing the extent of the various types of state public services in proportion to population, wealth, area, motor vehicle registration, number of dependents, etc.

Standards of service presumably are more directly applicable as measuring sticks of adequacy and more definitely determinable than are standards of balance. Standards of service are useful in the degree to which they have been derived by careful evaluation of actual service rendered, per unit of service facility weighed against demonstrable need. But few factors in the measurement of services are, in the last analysis, subject to scientific determination. Most of them are dependent upon established custom and judgement. It is especially difficult to determine standards of service upon a state basis for state services which are supplemented by federal and/or municipal services.

Perhaps, of the several services rendered by states, only roads. are reasonably susceptible to this approach. And, even for roads, there is such a wide variation in conditions and in serviceability per mile, by reason of differences in location, in planning, and in functions, that any arbitrary standard can be useful only as general indicator of the relative advance of individual states in road building. 

In setting up service standards it is well to be guided by averages, but not controlled by them. While standards based upon averages may offer a considerable goal to the more deficient states, they are recognizedly consider ably below desirability and usually far below individual accomplishment. 

Since standards are too often regarded as maximums, low standards are almost certain to act as deterrents to the more progressive states. Because of the influence of peculiar local circumstances, it is equally unsound per se to establish standards upon the basis of the highest of accomplishment. No single factor should control. Each should be given its proper weight among the others. As suggested above, peculiar local circumstances are likely to affect the proper weighting of all factors of measurement. It has been im→→ possible within the limits of this study to observe local conditions and unwise, therefore, to attempt to establish standards upon the basis of data at 170

hand more definitely than is suggested roughly by tables contained in 

Chapter V. 

The general indication of data compiled is that state highway construction has frequently been advanced out of proportion to her state services. This does not mean necessarily that there are too many and too good state highways, but rather that there are proportionately too few other service facilities.

Criteria and Standards for Local and Municipal Public Works 

Municipal public works and responsibilities are perhaps more comprehensible than federal or state activities. They are more directly and more intimately connected with everyday living. Adequacy and fitness to purpose of many urban or municipal public works, nevertheless, either individually or in the aggregate, are not subject to exact determination. 

Maximum individual public service is quite dependent upon current public opinion as motivated by or limited by knowledge or ignorance of possibilities, by available wealth, and by social conscience. To establish service standards or to render judgment as to justification of urban public works upon the basis of present-day accomplishment of even the most advanced cities is merely to make the best of present-day traditions and practices, albeit heavily weighed down by impoverished imagination. No city in the United 

States, and probably no city in the workd, affords a living environment for all, or even for the average of its citizens, approaching known possibilities. The construction and improvement of cities has lagged far behind the general advance of the arts and sciences. 

Standards of service and standards of balance are useful as crude measuring sticks for determining the relative advance of cities and the relative adequacy of one type of service or another. They are more useful, perhaps, when applied to municipal public works than to federal and state projects, but such standards, after all, are merely direction indicators and do not represent the ultimate goal or fix the ultimate limit of public services or of possibilities in improvement of living environment. 

Variable Complexity of Applied Criteria

There are certain basic services which must be supplied to every city in proportion to its population, simple in the extent of applicable criteria as to need and justification. These include potable water, removal of sewage and waste, accessibility to places of work and of residence, and some degree of fire protection and policing. Other services, are subject to more varied and more complex criteria. Difficulty of applying service standards to municipal public works is greatly increased by the variable between good and bad planning. As with federal and state public works, the municipal plan is a primary criterion in the selection and programming 171

of municipal public works, and further, the effectiveness of individual projects, per unit cost, varies greatly with the skill and foresight that has gone into the plan making.

Comprehensive municipal plans must in turn be subjected to certain criteria. These criteria include conformity to the national policy; adjustment to the national, state, and regional plans; and adjustment to the less definite criteria of growth probabilities. The comprehensive city plan should be formulated upon the basis of certain more or less definite principles and arbitrary standards as to distribution, area, and arrangement of the several functional and component parts of the present-day city. Such facilities as parks and playgrounds, schools and schoolgrounds, and widths of street paving are more susceptible of standardization as to distribution, extent, and design than are other municipal public works. Even in these instances, however, with the possible exception of paving widths in relation to street function, no precise standards have been determined. In most instances such suggestions of standards as have been offered are the product of judgment without very much basis of scientific fact, and are frequently tinged by known or apparent local limitations. Cities generally have fallen so far be low known possibilities of service and environment that virtually all efforts to formulate standards tend to assume something of compromise from the very start.

Standards of service may be met literally without providing comparable service. Service standards measured by area, by distribution, or by capital expenditures, either in proportion to population or community wealth or gross area of community, represent very crude measuring sticks effective only when applied with knowledge of local conditions and with judgment. 

The value of service standards is in direct proportion to the de pendability of their derivation. Such standards, if to be more reliable than standards already available, must be derived through close study of a considerable number of cities study directed toward discovering in individual instances what is implied by so-called high-standard services, toward discovering what these services mean to and how they are looked upon by citizens of varying responsibility in the several walks of life; toward discovering what, if any, peculiar conditions are attached to rendering individual services; and toward ascertaining per capita cost of these services in propor tion to per capita wealth. 

In attempt to arrive at general conclusion as to proper balance in municipal expenditures for the several classes of public works and to determine the extent of actual services rendered in a number of selected cities, a considerable amount of financial statistics of cities have been compiled and are given in tabulations contained in Chapter VI. As is the case with state expenditures and state improvements, the range of expenditures and improvements for individual cities is so great as to caution against using these facts either separately or in the form of averages as basis for the formulation of standards except after considerable further study of local circumstances. 172

Certain general conclusions may be drawn from these data, such, for instance, as indication that, from the point of view of balance, schools generally have absorbed somewhat disproportionate amounts of public funds during recent years. Again, as with state highways, this may not mean that existing school facilities are over-extended in any considerable number of communities, but rather that other facilities apparently have been sacrificed to increased school construction. 

General needfulness of an individual project within any service class may be indicated by the general deficiency in this class as to be determined roughly by standards of service. Timeliness of a given project is to be determined in part by relative needfulness as measured by the extent of deficiency in its class in comparison with other deficiencies for the municipality concerned. Relative merit and timeliness of an individual project in relation to other projects of the same class is to be determined largely by the functional place and location of this project as indicated by the comprehensive city plan. 

As a further step in the programming of public works, and as a further check upon the timeliness of individual projects and groups of projects, there should be formulated for every city a capital improvement budget and program extended over a reasonable period, possibly from five to ten years.

Estimates of Potentially Needful Public Works 

In addition to satisfaction of present-day needs, an estimate of needful public works to be constructed over a future period of years must be made with consideration for: 1. 2. 3. 

Rate of population growth

Obsolescence and replacement 

Possibilities of enlargement of the field of public works and public services.

Probable future rate of population growth in the United States is a moot question, involving unforeseeable factors which may enter at any time to disturb predictions. While ultimate population is to be provided for in the United States has bearing upon ultimate magnitude of required public works and services, its variables are not of great concern in estimating near future requirements.

The more obvious approaches to estimating the magnitude of future public works in the absence of precise and established programs include: 

1. Projection of the past rate of public works construction into the future 173

2. 3. 

Measuring construction requirements by demonstrable deficiencies as indicated by comprehensive plans and as measured by established standards and Giving play to the imagination, with a reasonable anchorage to judgment, in possibilities of extension and expansion of public works and public services without restriction by tradition.

Approach (1) is more applicable to estimates of total magnitude of public works than to estimates of extent of individual classes of public works because of constantly shifting emphasis placed upon individual classes of service. It has the merit of representing an amount of public expenditure to which the public has become more or less accustomed. It is, however, less dependable at this time than in earlier years because of the abnormalities of the past ten or twenty years as to services required and demanded and as to money in circulation. 

Approach (2) is by far more rational than Approach (1) but assumes a degree of intelligence and judgment in public administration not yet widely attained or demonstrated. It offers, however, the only basis of estimating the magnitude of future public works in relation to relative service requirements. 

Approach (3) assumes a "New Deal" amounting to a revolutionized public opinion and is dependent upon perfected machinery of distribution and greatly improved social and economic organization. 

Based solely upon extension into the future of the average annual capital outlay for the entire United States of about $2,400,000,000 for the ten years 1921 to 1930, the total of capital outlays for the ten year period 1935 to 1944 may be estimated as approximately $24,000,000,000. 

By adjusting past rates of capital outlay for individual classes of public works according to readily available evidence as to relative need and with a degree of optimism as to possible correction of the more glaring deficiencies in service, estimate for the ten year period, 1935 to 1944, is approximately twenty-six billion dollars about two and a half billion a year. 111 Extension of public works into new fields and expansion of all public works, old and new, into the field of reasonable possibility from point of view of needful public service brings the estimate of magnitude of all public works over a period of perhaps twenty to thirty years to about one hundred and five billion dollars or about three and one-half to five billion dollars a year. Such an expanded program of public works would amazingly improve the public services and living standards of this country. Viewed in the light of traditional expenditures for public works it seems to involve a lot of money. Five billions a year for roads, parks, sewers, public libraries, forests, waterways, good housing and countless such other facilities is not such a shocking amount when compared with the 174

estimated thirteen billion dollar annual crime bill of the United States.*

Public Works as an Employment Stabilizer 

Two major difficulties stand in the way of using public works as a leveler of unemployment cycles by building up a reservoir of projects to be drawn upon in time of need. The first is current need for public works to correct demonstrable deficiencies in public service --deficiencies which are not properly to be postponed in anticipation of the possible employment needs of an indefinite future year. The other is the difficulty of curbing popular demand for public services and public expenditures during periods of prosperity and easy money.

The first places definite limitations upon the kind and extent of public works that should be postponed for such a purpose and suggests that the public works reserve is more properly to be comprised of those extra-needful public works included in an expanded program such as contemplated under the above extended estimate of potential public works and services.

The second obstacle might be overcome in part by urging or compellinga more extensive adoption of the pay-as-you-go policy in state and municipal improvement programs during periods of relative prosperity, with borrow ing held in reserve for periods of economic stress. 

Public works alone, even though greatly expanded into new fields and perfected services, as indicated by the above estimates of expanded magnitude, are not sufficient in themselves greatly to reduce technological unemployment over a considerable period of years. Such expansion of public work's, including the additional labor of operation and maintenance, would, however, make some in-roads on chronic unemployment and, by careful manipula~ tion especially in the field of "extra-needful" works and services, might be made to serve as an effective primer of the economic pump. Difficulty would be in knowing just when and in what volume to apply the priming. 

Guidance of Public Works Toward Improved Public Service 

Selection and programming of public works and estimating the mag nitude of needful public works cannot be done successfully or intelligently except upon the basis of an accomplished national network of comprehensive plans. All efforts otherwise are shots in the dark becoming less dependable as they become more specific in application. 

Planning, however, is not infallible. It involves the unknowns of human inventiveness and of evolving public opinion and public policy. 

Public services, as anticipated by plan, are measures of social progress. 

History shows how slow and tortuous may be the path of social progress. *Fortune Magazine, August, 1934. 175

< It is an easy thing to over-estimate in calculating near future probabilities. 

The only real justification of public works is that of their contribution to human welfare, as measured by improved standards of living. 

Justified public works include facilities for serving all those functions, not delegated to private enterprise, contributing to general well-being, comfort, and convenience, and for which there is material and knowledge to produce. Such a definition of public works reaches beyond the accepted field of public responsibility but not beyond the bounds of reason. Toleration of services and of living standards in the United States, so disproportionate to national real wealth, is due to general failure of the people and their leaders to comprehend and to order the complexities of a social organism of such magnitude and is not due to any inherent physical incapacity to produce. 

It may be that the human race, as represented by the people of the United States, has not yet attained that degree of social comprehension whereby the problems of a great people spread over a wide and diverse area can be reduced to simple and tractable terms, But, in principle, the problems of the larger and more complex community are much the same as for the smaller and simpler entity and would seem to require but greater courage and persistence in solution. A man with a shovel can remove a mountain by persisting with the same methods and movements that he may use to remove a molehill. The difference in practice is that, seeing neither the top nor the other side of the mountain, he may quit on the job before he starts. A further difference in the two jobs is that the bigger job requires planning, else the laborer may succeed only in digging a hole, to build another moun tain.

Comprehensive planning for the nation, for a region, a state, or a city is a necessary part of the process of reducing complex problems to simple and comprehensible terms. The comprehensive plan is both a compass and a gyroscope pointing direction and maintaining balance. 176

Chapter X AN APPROACH TO SUMMARIZATION OF CRITERIA AND STANDARDS 

For

MEASURING THE RELATIVE NEEDFULNESS OF PUBLIC WORKS AND THE MAKING OF PUBLIC WORKS PROGRAM 

(The following outline is merely by way of suggestion as to a possible approach to public service standards which might be further developed to serve as one of the more important body of criteria in the planning, selection and programming of public works. It is admittedly incomplete and includes tentative standards not yet well established.)

I. COLLECTIVE CRITERION 

The relation of project to all other projects included in the comprehensive plan and consistent with established policy national, state, county, and/or municipal.

II. SPECIFIC CRITERIA A. Land Utilization 

B. 1. 2. 3. 

All land to be used for highest potential purpose consistent with a permissive rate of deterioration.

All land not needed or profitably to be used for higher service than that of forest or natural lands to be retained in or returned to forest and/or recreational use, under public or private ownership or management as circumstances may suggest. 

Land areas involving cost for servicing with roads, schools and other necessities, greater than their productive capacity to be withdrawn from occupation requiring these services when disparity between cost and production cannot be removed by improved management.

Other Natural Resources 

To be developed and utilized in the interests of greatest public welfare viewed over an indefinite future period. 

C. Transportation a. 

Roads and Highways

A national trunk highway system including three to five coast-tocoast and three to five north-and-south routes, with necessary transverse and interconnecting routes. 177

2. 3. c. d. 

Paved highway access to all sizable communities and paved highway interconnection between all concentrations of population or of industry, or of other activity or interest, with capacity of not less than one free moving traffic lane for every 500 vehicles per hour of normal traffic flow.

A network of strategically located highways within the national system of sufficient strength and capacity to serve the purposes of transportation of munitions in time of war. 

Hard or all-weather surface road access to all agricultural or other areas occupied to the extent of four or more families per square mile. Streets a. For residential districts percentage of area devoted to streets not to exceed twenty to twenty-five per cent. b. c. d. e. 

All street paving widths to be in multiples of standard traffic lane widths eight, nine, and ten feet with total width not to exceed prospective functional requirements. 

Arterial streets to be arranged at intervals of from one-half to one mile.

Street pattern to be such as to isolate residential neighborhoods from all high speed or through traffic.

All streets to have hard or all-weather surface of adequate and efficient width. Railroads a. Primary system to be retained and completely modernized. 1. 

All unnecessary duplication of service to be eliminated.

C. All non-paying secondary service to be supplanted by bus and truck service where possible and to be accomplished without serious disruption of activities served. d. e. 1Unification of all railroads on basis of national system, in the interest of efficiency and improved service.

Coordination of railways with water, highway, and air transportation, in the interests of efficiency, improved service, and economies to be gained by elimination of unnecessary duplications. 178

D. 4. 5. Waterways a. Natural channels to be served and safeguarded in proportion to potential use. b.

New or deeper channel projects to be justified upon basis of: (1) Net cost after values of other purposes served have been deducted. (2) Whether or not service requirements are already met by other existing agencies such as railroads or highways. (3) Whether or not prospective revenue from or savings to users will approximate operating and maintenance costs plus carrying charges upon net investment. (4) Reasonable consideration as to effect upon other existing and regionally essential public carriers.

Adequate landing fields at all isolated points of population concentration.
